# Siddhānta-ratnam (JIVA)

## Introduction

Gauḑiya writers‘ extensive production in the field of Sanskrit poetry has earned them a distinct place in the history of literature, particularly for consolidating bhakti as one of the prominent rasas. From Lord Caitanya’s days, their deepest philosophical conclusions were inbuilt in their poetical expression, either in vernacular or Sanskrit compositions. At that early point, no need was felt ta translate the subtleties of the Gauḑiya ideology into the conventional technical language adopted by ancient schools of thought, both Vaiṣṇava and non-Vaiṣṇava. Their intricate concepts would naturally spring forth during eloquent discourses on the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam. Taking this lead, Śri Jiva Gosvāmi was the first Gauḑiya to make a substantial effort to vindicate and define the Bhāgavata philosophy in terms of the dialectic approach that had been in vigour for many centuries amongst learned circles in India. He thus brought out the Bhāgavata-sandarbhas, the six treatises in which he reads the Gauḑiya philosophy through the verses from the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam in a thematic and methodical manner. His autocommentary, entitled Sarva-samvādini, gave even a further dimension to his work by directly engaging with, and debating on various ancient systems of thought in the light of the Bhāgavata view.

The decades that followed were marked by an acute decline in the literary activity that had blossomed in the 16th century and met its acme in the works of Śri Rūpa and Śri Jiva. It was not until late 17th century that another prolific author of a similar stature emerged in the form of Śri Viśvanātha Cakravarti, who made up for a long felt dearth in the scholarly tradition started by Mahāprabhu’s followers. While his compositions were much alike those of Rūpa in his elaborations on bhakti-rasa and the path of devotion, he did not take the same interest as Jiva did in going to great lengths in comparative philosophy. That task was awaiting another eminent personality that would appear about a couple of generations later.

By divine providence, it was through the hand of Śri Baladeva Vidyābhūṣaṇa that the Gauḑiya-sampradāya came to be widely accepted as a bona fide school of Vedānta. Under the patronage of Sawai Jai Singh II (1688-1743 AD), King of Amber, Vidyābhūṣaṇa composed important works and became an exalted saint and scholar both in Vrndāvana and Jaipur. The King not only commissioned him to write important treatises but also made him a member of his court, as confirmed at the end of this book. Several documents preserved at the Rajasthan State Archives corroborate Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s lifelong relationship with the royal family. It was King Jai Singh who first request him to present a commentary on the Brahma-sūtras, both for his personal studies as well as to appease the claims raised against the authenticity of the Gauḑiya lineage. In response, Vidyābhūṣaṇa shortly produced the Brahma-sūtra-kārikā-bhāṣya, which starts by declaring that he is thereby fulfilling the King’s order to have such a commentary. Although the manuscript is undated, related documentation seems to indicate the text may have been written in the late 1730s. It is not clear when Vidyābhūṣaṇa started to work on a more comprehensive commentary that he named ‘Govinda-bhāṣya‘, but many years may have elapsed after the Karika-bhāṣya. Jai Singh suddenly passed away in 1743, probably many years before the completion of the Govindā-bhāṣya, a text that has never been added to his large collection of commentaries on Vedānta. Since the copy Vidyābhūṣaṇa sent to Gopiballabhpur is dated 1758 AD, this is possibly the year in which the Govinda-bhāṣya was concluded. Aware of the scarcity of philosophical treatises from the Gauḑiya perspective, he later envisioned the composition of yet another text that would further elaborate on many of the topics discussed in that commentary but without the structural restrictions imposed by a particular sequence of aphorisms. He also felt appropriate to provide the readers a basis to enable them to derive more benefit from the complex arguments that abound therein. Hence, this treatise came into existence and was named Govinda-bhāṣya-piṭhakam, a throne (piṭhaka) or support for the Govinda-bhāṣya. Because it presents multiple jewel-like (ratna) philosophical conclusions (siddhānta), it is also named Siddhānta-ratnaṁ.

The earliest dated manuscript available was written down in 1780 AD, when Vidyābhūṣaṇa was probably in his seventies or early eighties. The commentary refers to the Vedānta-syamantaka, Sūkṣmā-tikā, Gitā-bhūsanam, and Sāhitya-kaumudi. All these indications suggest that the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ may have been one of his latest works, possibly written in the 1760s or 1770s.

At the end, Vidyābhūṣaṇa acknowledges Śri Govindadeva for giving him knowledge and making him renowned as a scholar. Although for many years he was silent about the matter, here he also reveals that Lord Govinda Himself appeared in his dream and ordered him to write a commentary on the Brahma-sūtra. The commentary here hints that this is actually a personal secret that should not be openly revealed, but perhaps now at old age, Vidyābhūṣaṇa decided to share this fact with others to glorify the Lord‘s magnanimity. The commentator narrates, “Upon being questioned regarding the conclusive meaning of the Brahma-sūtra that the son of Nanda Maharaja is the Supreme Lord Himself, the author became very dejected because the conclusion was not taken as such. Unable to tolerate his dejection, the Supreme Lord, Ṡyāmasundara, wearing yellow garments, a sacred thread, an ūrdhva-puṇḍra tilaka, and braided hair, appeared in his dream and ordered him three times. ” Contrary to the flowery versions we hear of the incidents that ensued, this seems to indicate that Vidyābhūṣaṇa may have been able to prove the legitimacy of the Gauḑiya-samprādaya, yet his presentation of Śri Govinda as the avatāri, the source of even Lord Nārāyaṇa, did not meet the approval of the local Vaiṣṇavas and others. Up to the present day, they may not have changed their minds, but at least Vidyābhūṣaṇa submitted his final answer on the matter by composing the Govinda-bhāṣya, wherein he unequivocally defends Lord Govinda’s supremacy to his best capacity from whomever may question His status.

In composing the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ, Vidyābhūṣaṇa based his arguments and conclusions on what he studied in the works of the previous ācāryas. Śri Jiva Gosvāmi’s Sandarbhas, and particularly the Sarva-saṁvādini, were a major source of references. The theological views in Śri Rūpa Gosvāmi’s Laghu-bhāgavatāmṛta were also extensively adopted here. From beginning to end, Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s allegiance to Madhvācārya is also visible, especially by defining simultaneous difference and non-difference in terms of ‘viṥeṣa’. He also borrowed several arguments from Jayatlrtha’s Nyāya-sudha, a sub-commentary on Madhvācārya’s Anuvyākhyāna on the Brahma-sūtra. These were great sources of inspiration in the three chapters wholly devoted to the refutation of various brands of Advaitavāda. Such extensive elaboration on the topic remains a unique and unsurpassed accomplishment amongst the Gauḑiya works ever written.

In his concluding words, Vidyābhūṣaṇa also acknowledges Pitāmbara dāsa, by whose mercy he was able to compose this treatise. The commentator clarifies that this was Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s ‘vidyā-guru‘ (a preceptor who imparts knowledge) and ‘pūrṇaprajna-guru’. Now, this last term seems to hint that Pitāmbara belonged to the line of Madhvācārya, who is also known as Pūrnaprajna. The title ‘dāsa‘ is particular to the Dāsakūṭa, a bhakti movement that flourished in Karnataka and had renowned personalities like Purandara dāsa and Kanaka dāsa (both 16th century). If it is true that Vidyābhūṣaṇa studied in Mysore, as we hear from certain sources, then he may have accepted the tutelage of Pitāmbara dāsa there. This is quite plausible and corroborates Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s initial background as a Mādhva-vaiṣṇava.

## The Commentary

It is somewhat startling that several publishers have attributed the Sanskrit commentary here to Baladeva Vidyābhūṣaṇa. This may either mean that they did not actually read the commentary, or may not have been sufficiently acquainted with his writings, scholarship, vocabulary, consistency, and style. As far as I have seen, Vidyābhūṣaṇa has not written any self-commentary to any of his works. He rather commissioned scholars like Vedāntavāgisa and Nanda Miśra to comment on his texts. Without any reservations, when he needed to refer to himself, he used ‘we’.  At the end of almost every one of his writings, he also clearly mentions his name as the author. On the other hand, we see that here the commentator refers to the author as ‘the saint‘ and ‘the acarya There have been no instances of Vidyābhūṣaṇa ever using these words to refer to himself, nor would such usage be appropriate for oneself. At the end, the commentator just declares that ‘this commentary was composed by a certain ’sadhu’.  It is obvious that he refrained from signing the text out of humbleness, as did Vidyābhūṣaṇa in the Govinda-bhāṣya, since the credit of the authorship is given to Lord Govinda. Moreover, there is no similarity in the writing style of both. Rather, the commentator falls short of the way of expression and scholarship found in the writings of Vidyābhūṣaṇa. A number of lapses could never have been found in the hands the latter. In various places, the commentator skipped technical and difficult passages but did not refrain from redundant explanations on others, whose meaning is simple and obvious. His frequent and superfluous reference to lexicons to gloss random terms is also noticeable. It suffices to mention that not less than nine times throughout the book, the commentator has given wrong references for a scriptural text quoted by Vidyābhūṣaṇa. This is something unheard of in the latter’s works. In several instances, the factual source was one of the major Upaniṣads that could have been very easily crosschecked. Yet there is no doubt that the commentary was written with the consent of the author, or perhaps even at his request. This may be corroborated by the fact that there are only three drafts of the text without the commentary, all kept at the same place, while the latter is found in all the other copies available around India, with no exception, many of which were written down during Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s lifetime.

Although no manuscript copy gives any hint of his name, it is evident that the commentator was thoroughly familiar with Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s works and could be none other than a close associate.

Indeed, the very fact that the commentary refers to the Sūkṣmā-ṭikā, which had been just recently written and would be known only to a few individuals, indicates that both were penned by the same person —Vedāntavāgiṥa. Moreover, the Sūkṣma-ṭikā also refers to the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ several times, which also had been just recently written and no manuscript copies had been yet circulated by the author. This means that Vedāntavāgiṥa had already studied the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ and was either inspired to comment on it later, or was requested by Vidyābhūṣaṇa to do so. His authorship is further corroborated by his commentary on the Prameya-ratnāvali. By a comparative study of the commentaries of both texts, it becomes apparent that they share the same explanations, illustrations, vocabulary, and quotations in multiple instances. This could have been possible only if both were composed by the same person or if one of the commentators were a plagiarist. Since these works were not yet available to others, the latter option is totally ruled out. In fact, Govinda-bhāṣya, Siddhānta-ratnaṁ, and Prameya-ratnāvali are a trilogy, the last two being supplementary works on the first. Thus, it is quite expected that Vedāntavāgiṥa would have commented not only on one or two, but on all three of them.

How this confusion started is unclear, but for a long time Vedāntavāgiṥa has been mistaken for Kṛṣṇadeva Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya, a prominent disciple of Viśvanātha Cakravarti who also played an important role in the Jaipur debates. Bhaṭṭācārya’s name is mentioned among five of Cakravarti’s disciples in a document dated Saṁvat 1769 (1712 AD) in connection with the Gokulananda ji Kunja at Radhākuṇḍa. Soon after, he settled in Amber and became the Mahanta of the VinodI Lāl Temple. A document dated Saṁvat 1773 (1716 AD) records a grant he received from King Sawai Jai Singh II to be used in the service of the deity.Ample documentation corroborates his relationship with the King, who commissioned him to compose several treatises, such as Bhakti-vivrti, Karma-vivrti, Jnāna-vivaraṇa, Bhakti-phala-viveka, and Siddhāntaikya-prakāśikā, all of which are part of the Khasmohor Collection, preserved at the Maharaja Sawai Mān Singh II Museum in the City Palace in Jaipur. These works seem to have been written between 1715 and 1735 AD. His best known book is the Padanka-dutam, whose earliest known manuscript is dated Sakabda 1645 (1723 AD).  Commentaries on various works of Viśvanātha Cakravarti, such as Alaṅkāra-kaustubha, Kṛṣṇa-bhāvanāmṛta, and Sahkalpa-kalpa-druma also have the name ‘Kṛṣṇadeva Sārvabhauma ‘at the end. Bhaṭṭācārya was a householder, and by 1735 AD, his son Ramanatha Deva Sarma was already sharing some of his duties. Kṛṣṇadeva Bhaṭṭācārya passed away either in the mid or late 1740s, as a document dated Saṁvat 1802 (1749 AD) describes that after his demise, his grandson, Vṛndāvana Bhaṭṭācārya, claimed the same grant that had been previously given to his grandfather.

The obvious conclusion from all this is that Kṛṣṇadeva Sārvabhauma Bhaṭṭācārya can by no means be the author of the commentaries on the Vedānta works of Vidyābhūṣaṇa, which were likely composed between 1745 and 1770 AD. On the one hand, Vidyābhūṣaṇa would have been about half the age of Bhaṭṭācārya and an absolutely unknown personality when he first arrived in Jaipur. On the other hand, Bhaṭṭācārya was already reputed as a Mahanta and scholar as early as 1715 AD, while Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s name is not seen in any document related to Jaipur before 1741 AD. Moreover, in none of his works has Bhaṭṭācārya signed ‘Vedāntavāgiṥa, ‘‘Vāgiṥvara, ‘or any similar name. We also know from the commentary on the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ that its author was a sādhu or ascetic rather than a householder.

Some believe that Cakravarti had another disciple called Kṛṣṇadeva Sārvabhauma Vedāntavāgiṥa, who is the actual author of the commentaries on the Alaṅkāra-kaustubha, Kṛṣṇa-bhāvanāmṛta, and Sankalpa-kalpa-druma. Such a claim could be definitely proved if we could actually find manuscripts of these commentaries where the name Vedāntavāgiṥa is mentioned, but up to the present day, I have not come across any. Until then, this proposition is haunted with too many coincidences to be easily taken for granted. In view of the above dates and facts, both of these theories may well be yet another distortion of the incidents, so common when hearsay prevails over recorded history. Furthermore, oral tradition says that one Kṛṣṇadeva accompanied Vidyābhūṣaṇa in Jaipur at Cakravarti’s command. If there were two individuals named Kṛṣṇadeva, which one is meant in this case?

However, it is possible that Vedāntavāgiṥa was also called Sārvabhauma. This view may be corroborated by Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s words at the end of the Aiśvarya-kādambini, where he, in a wordplay, declares that “this text has been composed by the kindness of Sārvabhauma Prabhu”. Since this work is dated Śakābda 1701 (1779 AD), we know that Bhaṭṭācārya could not have taken any participation at that point, more than three decades after his demise. Yet, without any evidence, to equate the name Vedāntavāgiṥa seen at the end of the Prameya-ratnāvali to the name Sārvabhauma seen at the end of the Aiṥvarya-kādambini may be a hasty assumption. Whether Vedāntavāgisa was also named Kṛṣṇadeva is doubtful, and only further research will confirm or deny this hypothesis.

Each chapter has a different title, about which the commentator writes at the end of the book: “In this treatise there are eight chapters, whose titles and order are as follows: Pāṅcajanya, Kaumodaki, Sudarśana, Tārkṣya, Vāmana, Trivikrama, Nandaka, and Padma. The chapters should be understood to be named as such due to their being similar to each of these”. Only in a few instances does he clarify the association between the title and the contents, and leaves to the reader to figure out the rest. Paficajanya, the Lord‘s conch, marks an auspicious beginning, and its sound terrifies those who inimical towards Him. Kaumodaki, the Lord‘s club, smashes into pieces many stone-like doubts. Sudarśana, His disc, dispels the darkness of ignorance by its effulgence and annihilates those who oppose the Lord. Tārkṣya, another name of Garuda, swallows all snake-like objections against the Vaiṣṇava siddhānta. In short form, Vāmana defeats the opponents by His mere astuteness. By extending His form, Trivikrama further humiliates the opponents by quoting and glossing various scriptures. Nandaka, the Lord‘s sword, uses sharp reasoning to make short of the opponents ‘crooked arguments. Padma, the lotus flower, presents the beautiful conclusion: Lord Kṛṣṇa is the sole Supreme Lord and the individual souls are eternally different from Him. The ultimate goal of life is to render pure devotional service unto Him, by which one attains transcendental happiness and the end of all distress.

## Sources

The following editions and manuscripts have been consulted in this critical edition and translation:

ka) Text and commentary, Bengali script, published by Ṥyāma Lāl Gosvāmi with his Bengali translation of the text, Bengali year 1304 (AD 1897), Kolkata.

kha) Text and commentary, Devanāgari, edited by Gopinātha Kavirāja, The Princess of Wales, Saraswati Bhavana Texts, n.10, in two parts published in 1924 and 1927, Benares.

ga) Text and commentary, Devanāgari, published by Kṛṣṇadāsa Bābā, Saṁvat 2022 (AD 1965)\> Kusuma Sarovara.

gha) Text published by Anādimohana Gosvāmi with his Bengali translation of the text, in two parts, Bengali year 1377 (AD 1970), Katwa.

ṅa) Text and commentary, Bengali script, published by the Gauḍiya Vedānta Samiti with a Bengali translation of the text, 1973, Navadwip.

ca) Text and commentary, Devanāgari, edited by Gaurasundara Bhāgavata Darśanācārya, published by Kasinatha Vedāntasastri and Kṛṣṇadeva Sastrācārya, undated, Kolkata.

cha) Text up to chapter 5, Oriya script published by Jagadānanda Dāsa with his Oriya translation, undated.

ja) Govinda-bhāṣya-ṭippaṇi-piṭhika siddhānta-ratnākhyā saṭippaṇi, Devanāgari, manuscript preserved at the Kṛṣṇapura Maṭha, Udupi, complete, undated.

na) Siddhānta-ratna-bhāṣya-piṭhakam saṭippaṇam, Devanāgari, manuscript preserved at the Rajasthan Oriental Research Institute in Jodhpur, accession number 37216, incomplete, undated,

Although Śyāma Lāl Gosvāmi deserves credit for publishing the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ for the first time, a number of discrepancies compromise the integrity of his edition to a great extent. In his short introduction, he has made several claims without even attempting to support them. According to him, Vidyābhūṣaṇa was a Bengali brāhmaṇa born in the family of Rasikānanda, undertook his studies in Bengal, became a disciple of Viśvanātha CakravartI in Vṛndāvana, and accompanied by him, installed a deity of Caitanya Mahāprabhu in Galta. All this is a sheer distortion of what we know as fact based on several sources, and it makes us wonder whether Śyāma Lāl actually read the book he published, at the end of which Rādhā-Dāmodara is unambiguously mentioned as Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s mantra-guru, while Cakravarti’s name has been totally omitted. The commentary does state that Rasikānanda belongs to the fourth generation preceding the author, but for those acquainted with the topic, this is an obvious reference to his religious affiliation rather than a family background, and the tables of Rasikānanda’s family lineage are easily available nowadays to confirm this. Rasikānanda did not belong to a brāhmaṇa-gotra either. Moreover, Vidyābhūṣaṇa used the Devanāgari script to write his treatises, contrary to the works of the ācāryas who factually hailed from Bengal, such as Rūpa, Sanātana, Jiva, Viśvanātha, and many others, all of whom wrote only in the Bengali script. Even the texts that Vidyābhūṣaṇa personally sent to Gopiballabhpur, the headquarters of the Śyāmanandi-parivara in Bengal, were all in Devanāgari. This is an indication that Vidyābhūṣaṇa was most likely from Odisha, where, in the context of the eighteenth century, Sanskrit scholars would often use Devanāgari besides Oriya script. In the Vṛndāvana-dhamanuragavali (chap. 32), Gopāla Kavi declares that Cakravarti had taken a vow to never leave Vraja. Most accounts corroborate that he was at a quite advanced age and never went to Jaipur, let alone installing any deity there. In a further twist, this hearsay later turned into a depiction of a deity called Vijaya Gopāla, supposedly installed by Vidyābhūṣaṇa in Galta. While the Galta Temple actually used to have a deity named thus, that was another of the several ancient deities that were installed and worshipped by the local Vaiṣṇavas. Up to the present day, there is not a single piece of evidence that any Gauḑiya ever installed any deity in Galta. Further to this, the present Vaiṣṇavas residing there do not have any information in this connection. More serious than this misrepresentation are the liberties that Śyāma Lāl took with the text itself, including inserting his own Sanskrit comments amounting to more than two thousand words in the first chapter within the original commentary, without even notifying the reader. He also changed, omitted, and added words at random throughout the text without any apparent reason. Unfortunately, this was not the only instance, as he also freely interpolated his edition of the Govinda-bhāṣya and Sūkṣma-ṭikā with his own comments and verses. Even if his interpolations seem to be harmless, this is a serious breach of etiquette, integrity, and genuine scholarship. Although he did not say a word about the manuscript used, it is quite clear that it may have been one of those copies in Bengali script available in Kolkata, all of which are riddled with many wrong readings. As for the Bengali translation, it mostly leaves the Sanskrit compounds and technical words as they are, without any explanatory note or attempt to clarify their meanings, contrasting to his enthusiasm to interpolate other passages. This offers little help to average readers and gives the impression that the translator himself may not have fully grasped the text.

Gopinātha Kavirāja’s edition is much more in conformity with the original, but it has been poorly revised. Although Kavirāja states that two manuscript copies were utilized, in fact it appears that most of the text was based only on the manuscript ‘pha‘, as it repeats the very same lapses, most of which are not seen in the manuscript ‘na’. From his introduction, it appears that he did go through the text, but his remark that the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ “in a sense is only a summary of the Govinda-bhāṣya” seems to indicate that he was not much familiar with the latter, for not even remotely this can be considered any sort of summary, albeit the thematic commonality of both texts.

According to his own words, Kṛṣṇadāsa Bābā’s edition was meant to make the text available once again, after being out-of-print for so many years. In spite of some corrections here and there, this edition was based on the two previous ones, and some of the interpolations have been repeated. Bābā expressed his wish to prepare a Hindi translation for a future edition, but he departed before being able to do so.

Anādimohana Gosvāmi’s edition does not include the commentary. In the initial chapters, he gave a new translation to the text, but after a certain point, it seems that he lost patience and started to repeat the same translation given by Śyāma Lāl Gosvāmi, including the same mistakes committed by the latter. In the introduction to the second part, he reveals that after working hard to publish rare texts like this, he has come to the conclusion that he has been wasting his time, as the Gauḍiyas have no interest at all in such material.

The Gauḑiya Vedānta Samiti’s edition follows Śyāma Lāl’s text and translation from beginning to end, and despite their claim that both were thoroughly revised and corrected, the same shortcomings have been indiscriminately repeated, including all of Śyāma Lāl’s interpolations. They repeated the same blunder by also following his readings in their edition of the Govinda-bhāṣya and Sūkṣmā-tikā. It is somewhat surprising to hear that they spent a large sum of money visiting major libraries all over India but could not locate a single manuscript copy of the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ.

Gaurasundara Bhāgavata’s edition also follows the Sanskrit text and commentary seen in Śyāma Lāl’s edition but transcribed into Devanāgari. More editorial mistakes were incurred in the process. No translation was given.

The edition published by Jagadānanda Dāsa has neither an editorial page nor an introduction, but it seems to be based on the previous editions. He may have translated and published the first part while teaching at the Jagannātha Veda Karmakāṇḍa Mahāvidyālaya in Puri. As far as I was told, the second part was never published.

When I first came to know that there were some of Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s works at Kṛṣṇapura Maṭha in Udupi, I was thrilled by the idea that they could have been possibly sent by the author himself and this would corroborate a connection with that Maṭha. However, upon analyzing manuscript ‘ja, ‘it became clear that this possibility is ruled out. The scribe starts by saluting Rāmānujācārya, and his reproduction of the text is so flawed that there is hardly a single line free from mistakes. Needless to say, this copy was not of much help.

Out of the four copies found in Jodhpur, manuscript ‘na’ had the most accurate readings, and so was the case with manuscript ‘da ‘from Alwar, which is the oldest dated copy, (AD 1780), still within the lifetime of the author. The manuscripts in Bengali script were later transcriptions, and as expected, had many more lapses than those in Devanāgari.

Manuscript ‘na‘ has by far the best readings. It was thoroughly revised by someone, who also added very pertinent side notes glossing some particular terms. While we could surmise that this was the copy used by Gopinaṭha Kavirāja, his edition follows mostly manuscript ‘ba‘, which seems to be a copy of ‘na‘ but has several mistakes not seen in the latter. Both lack the last folio, which was probably misplaced and later entered as ‘pa‘ in their catalogue. Manuscript ‘pha‘ at first seemed to be interesting, as the title suggests that it is a summary. In fact, it is just a random selection of sentences from the text and the commentary, without any apparent criteria or relation to each other, and sometimes without much import out the original context.

The only three copies without the commentary were found in Vṛndāvana, and despite being undated, are evidently the oldest copies. They have neither full stops (daṇḍa), nor numbers in the paragraphs. Manuscript ‘ya ‘has some corrections done by Dayānidhi, who served Vidyābhūṣaṇa as a scribe for many years. Yet the text has a visible number of spelling mistakes. Manuscript ‘va ‘is the only copy that is free from mistakes that were repeated in all the subsequent copies. Manuscript ‘la ‘is possibly the earliest copy of the text and may have been used by Vidyābhūṣaṇa as a draft, full of emendations as it is. Some paragraphs seem to have been rewritten later, with either additions or subtractions. These may have been worked out in cooperation with the commentator.

The other manuscript copies listed above often do not have correct readings or any further details but were nevertheless occasionally helpful to determine the final text. On the basis of all these multiple copies, after discarding all spurious readings, interpolations, grammatical errors, and spelling mistakes, this critical edition presents the original Sanskrit text and commentary as written by the respective authors. Nothing more, nothing less. It is beyond the scope of this work to record illegitimate readings, which would result in a huge amount of abstruse and purposeless notes. Only a few legitimate variations of words were actually found, among which those that may have some relevance to the general readers have been mentioned here. A full Sanskrit version in searchable format containing more peculiarities found in the manuscripts will be subsequently uploaded on the web.

Some catalogues have references of other manuscript copies besides those listed above, some of which are either doubtful or do not exist anymore. In the Catalogue of Sanskrit Manuscripts in Mysore and Coorg, published in 1884, Lewis Rice lists a text called Siddhānta-ratna, entry number 1698, reportedly a thousand granthas long and preserved at the Śṛṅgeri Maṭha. Gustav Oppert refers to the same text in the Lists of Sanskrit Manuscripts in Private Libraries of Southern India, volume 2, published in 1885, entry number 5059.1 was courteously welcomed at the library of the Śṛṅgeri Maṭha, the headquarters of the Śaṅkara-sampradāya, but upon consulting their manuscript list, I verified that no such text was mentioned. Speaking fluent Sanskrit, the librarian told me that a large number of manuscripts had been destroyed many decades ago in some unfortunate incident. In any case, it is doubtful whether that text was the Govinda-bhāṣya-piṭhakaṁ, as there are a few known texts called Siddhānta-ratna written by other authors.

In the Catalogue of Sanskrit Manuscripts in Oudh for the Year 1893, Paṇḍita Deviprasāda lists a text named Siddhānta-ratna, 254 folios, Devanāgari, dated 1843, belonging to Rāmānuja Dāsa, Faizabad. The description given is “a treatise on devotion to Rama according to the Rāmānuja school.” This could had sufficed to dismiss the idea that it is a Gauḍiya work, but the very next entry reads “Govinda-bhāṣya, a commentary on the above, by Vidyābhūṣaṇa,” from which we can easily understand that the above description is not accurate. I am aware that there are many personal manuscript collections in the Ayodhyā area, but most of them are neither classified nor properly maintained, and I could not afford spending time there to search for this copy, especially without knowing if it still exists.

In the Notices of Sanskrit Manuscripts, Second Series, volume 1, part 1, published in 1898, Haraprasāda Śāstri lists a copy of the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ by Baladeva Vidyābhūṣaṇa, 128 folios, Devanāgari, Saṁvat 1947 (AD 1890), belonging to the Maharaja of Hatwa. The entry includes the beginning and end of the text and commentary. The whereabouts of this collection is unknown to me, and since this is a quite later copy and the given descriptions do not add anything to the remaining available manuscripts, I did not try to locate it.

There is yet another manuscript copy that Baladeva Vidyābhūṣaṇa himself offered to Śri Govindadeva in Jaipur and is still part of the temple collection, and which in many respects could be possibly more important and helpful than any other copy. Sadly, the present Goswamis who run the temple are openly callous about any efforts to propagate the literary heritage of the Gauḍiya-sampradāya and decline to offer the slightest cooperation to scholars and Vaiṣṇavas alike, including renowned professors and even the Radhākuṇḍa Mahanta.

## Acknowledgements

This publication would not have been possible without the help of many individuals and institutions. Despite his busy schedule, Dr. Satyanārāyaṇa Dāsa Bābāji, director of the Jiva Institute, gave many hours of his time to this project. He offered invaluable assistance by clarifying difficult passages and replying to innumerable questions. He also met the printing costs. The staff of the various institutes and libraries listed above assisted me in several visits. Vidyāsāgara Tirtha Swami very kindly allowed me to copy the manuscript ‘ja‘ preserved at the Kṛṣṇapura Maṭha in Udupi. Vikram Majumdar assisted me in several expeditions in West Bengal. Amarnath Shil helped to secure some manuscript copies. Dr. Adrian Burton did an extensive research on historical records, and I borrowed some references from his thesis. Bhāratacandra Dāsa and his Centre for Traditional Education kindly sponsored the cover design. Tilak Sharma and family arranged the picture for the cover. B.V. Giri Swami, Dr. Gerald Surya, Madhusūdana Dāsa, Jagannivāsa Dāsa, Jaya Gosāi Dāsa, and Mahāprabhu Dāsa helped by editing, revising, and proofreading different portions of the English translation. Bṛjbāsi Dāsa revised the translation from Bengali. Bhakti Vikāśa Swami has been regularly giving financial assistance to my research work and is always ready to help in whatever he can. I am very much obliged to all of them and whomever has assisted the Baladeva Vidyābhūṣaṇa Project in any capacity.

Dr. Demian Martins

21st Marth, 2019, Gaura-purnima

Śri Vṛndāvana Dhāma

## Summary of the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ

by Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura

I shall briefly write about the way Śri Vidyābhūṣaṇa Bābāji analyzed the process of sambandha, abhidheya, and prayojana, as found in the treatise called Siddhānta-ratnaṁ. In this book, his reflections on the truth have been divided into eight chapters.

In the first chapter, the author states that everyone is inclined to attain happiness and remove distress. The means to achieve both ends as described by Kapila, Patanjali, Kaṇāda, Gautama, and Jaimini are all defective. Refuting all those views, Vedavyāsa compiled the Vedānta aphorisms on the basis of the Vedic scriptures and instructed that the only means is God realization accompanied by self-knowledge. God, the Supreme Lord of all, is Puruṣa, the Supreme Person, Whose svarūpa is knowledge and bliss. He is endowed with all potencies, and He is inconceivable, transcendental, beyond logic, and distinguished by eight divine qualities starting with the power to realize all desires. Within His svarūpa there is no type of difference; not even internal difference between attributes and their possessor. Yet, on the strength of His inconceivable potency, there is distinction. He and His various attributes are known by the power of the denotation of the scriptures. The performance of Vedic rituals cannot be taken as a direct means to attain the two aforementioned ultimate ends.

Jnāna and bhakti have been described as direct causes of both. Realization of the meaning of the word ‘tvam‘ (you) is knowledge of non-difference, in which liberation is characterized as absolute oneness. Realization of the meaning of the words ‘tat ‘(that) and ‘tvam‘ is knowledge of one’s svarūpa as a devotee, and it is compared to a lovely side-glance. By bhakti in the form of complete knowledge of the pure meaning of the word ‘tat, ‘one attains any of the four kinds of liberation, starting with salokya. By bhakti in the form of complete knowledge of a particular pure relationship with the Supreme Lord (tat), one attains the goal of life — service to His lotus feet. Such bhakti is the essence of samvit combined with the essence of hlādini. It is not merely the soul’s happiness. Such bhakti gives bliss both to the Supreme Lord and to the jtva. Sandhini, saṁvit, and hlādini are the three functions of the Lord‘s superior energy (parā-śakti). Becoming manifest in a living entity’s present body and mind, bhakti turns all the senses into one’s svarūpa in a form of pure bliss. Even without waiting for the mind to be purified by the performance of Vedic rituals, upon faithfully associating with saintly persons, many people have purified their minds by worshipping the Supreme Lord, and thus attained God realization. It is said that the various kinds of liberation follow the devotees, even though they do not entertain any desire for them. “Kṛṣṇa’s happiness is my happiness,” — the result of such selfless devotion is none other than bhakti itself. This bhakti has descended from the Lord‘s servants amongst the devotees of the present age as a sampradāya, just like a stream of the Ganga. Although all His desires are fulfilled, the Supreme Lord affectionately accepts the devotee’s worship. Kṛṣṇa’s mercy is inconceivable and beyond logic.

In the second chapter, the author explains that the Lord‘s nature as the Supreme is manifest in twofold varieties: mādhurya and aiśvarya. According to these two varieties, the living entity’s knowledge and devotion is twofold. In mādhurya, the manifestation or non-manifestation of the Lord‘s supreme opulence does not transgress in any way His pastimes in a human-like form; while knowledge of aiśvarya is said to be an attribute that slackens one’s natural mood by causing heart palpitations and reverence. When knowledge about aiśvarya is dormant, mādhurya becomes stronger. A devotee situated in mādhurya experiences knowledge of aiśvarya in situations like astonishment, separation, and danger. Both aiśvarya and mādhurya are transcendental attributes of the Supreme. Mādhurya, the svarūpa of the bliss of the Lord‘s pastimes and the essence of His spiritual potency, is non-different from His svarūpa. The Lord‘s svarūpa is His very body, which is devoid of the eighteen kinds of defects. The Supreme Lord has unlimited contradictory qualities like confusion and omniscience, all of which are subordinate to His supreme power and unfold His pastimes without any contradiction. Bhakti is twofold: that which reveals aiśvarya is called vidhi-bhakti; and that which reveals mādhurya is called ruci-bhakti. Vidhi-bhakti is twofold: mixed and pure. Devotees engaged in vidhi-bhakti are called svaniṣṭha, and they eventually attain Vaikuṇṭha through the path that starts with light (arciradi-marga). As for the pure devotees, feeling anxiety for them, the merciful Lord brings them to His abode on the shoulders of Garuda. Ruci-bhakti (raga) consists of mādhurya and is mixed with the mood of a kinsman towards the Lord. Such devotees free from reverence attain the Lord‘s abode. Kṛṣṇa, the son of Mother Yaśodā, is a relative of the cowherd men. Actually, Kṛṣṇa is the Supreme Person, the reservoir of all potencies and the Supreme Lord Himself. When not all potencies are visible in the Lord‘s form, but only a few of them are manifest, that is an expansion of His known as vilāsa (expansion), aṁśa (plenary portion), or kalā (portion of a plenary portion). Kṛṣṇa is the avatāri, the source of all avataras, while Nārāyaṇa, the Lord of the spiritual sky (Vaikuṇṭha), is Kṛṣṇa’s expansion (vilāsa). Kṛṣṇa Himself is the original form and is independent of any other form. A host of associates who are His complete energies, such as Srimati Rādhārāṇi; the sweetness of the sound of His flute, which bewilders all moving and non-moving beings; the sweetness of His form, which astonishes even Himself; a transcendental ocean with waves full of pastimes which are astonishing to all and display qualities such as unsurpassed mercy — these four uncommon qualities are found only in Lord Kṛṣṇa and are not manifest in Nārāyaṇa or other forms. Śri Radhika, love personified and the essence of hlādini, is the Supreme Lord‘s superior potency (parā-śakti). Lakṣmi, Durgā, and other goddesses are a particular portion or reflection of her. Therefore, being endowed with sixty-four transcendental attributes that are fully manifest, Kṛṣṇa alone is the original form of the Supreme. In the Vedas, Kṛṣṇa’s exceedingly wonderful and perpetual abode of pastimes is called Goloka. Beneath Goloka is Mathura, beneath which is Dvārakā, beneath which is Vaikuṇṭha, beneath which is Lord Siva’s abode, beneath which is Devi’s abode which comprises of this material universe. According to the Lord‘s will, these abodes become manifest within the material universe in order to display their respective pastimes. Although these manifest abodes are fully transcendental, they appear as material to those whose vision has not been purified. The vision of the Lord‘s form yields religious merit. In unlimited forms, in unlimited manifestations, with unlimited pastimes, in unlimited material universes, in unlimited Vaikuṇṭha planets, with unlimited companions, all of Lord Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes are eternal. Without the Supreme Lord‘s mercy, it is not possible to understand this mystery. In the Lord‘s abode, it is always present time, without past and future. Therein, the places, objects, the sun, the moon, and so on are all transcendental. When the universe is destroyed, the occasional pastimes no longer exist, but the eternal pastimes in the company of Kṛṣṇa do not vanish. Both kinds of bhakti, based on vidhi or ruci, are certainly the causes of removing distress and attaining happiness. Without Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, engagement in devotional service is not possible. Comparatively, ruci-bhakti is superior to vidhi-bhakti.

In the third chapter, the author says that being devoid of anyone equal or superior, being characterized by His superior potency, and being devoid of the six kinds of transformations, the Supreme Lord is distinguished among the demigods. Lord Viṣṇu is the Supreme Deity for all the demigods and is to be worshipped by those who aspire for liberation. Only Lord Viṣṇu should be worshipped, but the demigods should not be disrespected. Those who hold the view that all divine beings are one; those who propound that Brahma, Viṣṇu, and Siva are one; and those who hold the view that Hari and Hara are one — among others, these are three types of people who are opposed to pure devotion to Viṣṇu. Taking to partial statements from the scriptures, they put hindrances against exclusive devotion to Viṣṇu, deceive others, and am themselves deceived. If all such sentences are analyzed together with other scriptural sentences, it is clearly understood that Lord Viṣṇu alone is the supreme divine being Who is to be worshipped by all living entities. The demigods act in the capacity of servants of Lord Viṣṇu, Who is their controller. Therefore, the Supreme Person, Viṣṇu, descends according to His own will among the three divinities — Brahma, Viṣṇu, and Siva. The other two personalities belong to the category of His separated expansions. They are not His personal expansions. His appearance, activities, and so on are transcendental and a mere manifestation of His kindness. His pastimes in the company of His separated expansions are eternal.

In the fourth chapter, the author refutes the doctrine that there is only one soul devoid of attributes. According to this monistic view, the entire śruti is to be divided into two parts: saguṇa and nirguṇa. The nirguṇa sentences of the śruti explain Brahman by secondary meanings (lakṣaṇā). Expressing Brahman’s empirical state of existence (vyāvahārika), the saguṇa sentences of the śruti have common descriptions (anuvāda) meant to establish pure consciousness devoid of attributes, as demonstrated by the nirguṇa sentences of the śruti. However, such division of the śruti is very inappropriate. The sages have divided the entire śruti into two sections: karma-kanda and jnāna-kānda. This division is irreproachable. Why? This is because the jnāna-kānda directly indicates the Supreme, while the karma-kanda, being subordinate to jnāna, indirectly indicates the Supreme. To divide all jnāna-kānda sentences of the śruti by distinguishing them as transcendental (pāramārthika) and empirical (vyāvahārika) is a foolish conception. Nowhere in the Vedic sentences is there any indication of such a division. Being beyond the grasp of direct perception and other means of knowledge, all saguṇa sentences of the Vedas establish the Supreme’s spiritual, transcendental qualities, while all nirguṇa sentences merely deny all material qualities. The Supreme Person described in the Upaniṣads is expressed by words. Exclusive and non-exclusive implication (bhaga-tyāga-lakṣaṇā) renders such imagined Brahman insentient. Words such as ’sāksi’ (witness), ‘kevala‘ (pure), and ‘nirviśeṣa‘ (devoid of attributes) appear in all sentences that establish nirguṇa, but don ‘t they actually establish saguṇa? Just as with sentences with words such as ‘omniscient/ sentences with words such as ‘witness‘ are all equally transcendental. The idea that the Supreme is mere consciousness without attributes is false. Māyāvāda appeared when faith in the words of the Vedas gradually slackened. Transcendental names, forms, and qualities intrinsic to His svarūpa do exist in the pure Supreme Absolute Truth — this is the direct meaning of all the sentences of the jnāna-kānda of the Vedas. Although the Supreme Lord is not expressed by all words, He is expressed by the Vedas, and He is different from the living entities and the material manifestation. One who knows Him as the Supreme Person, beyond both the entangled souls and the liberated souls — such a living entity has attained the goal.

In the fifth chapter, it is demonstrated that non-duality is never proven. If it is said that non-duality exists apart from the Supreme, then there is no non-duality. If it is said that non-duality is itself the Supreme, there ensues the flaw of being a means to an objective already accomplished. That is futile. Since the svarūpa of the soul is something already accomplished, how can ignorance cover its non-duality? Due to lacking the characteristic of being a means to apprehend something unknown, the scriptures would be unauthoritative. If someone argues that ignorance exists apart from the Supreme, then there would be duality. If someone argues that ignorance does not exist, then the already perfect soul would have no need to attain liberation. “Ignorance is neither existent nor non-existent.” Then what is it? “It is indescribable.” Just see how the mental concoction has gradually increased! As soon as a false philosophy is created, concoctions come one after another. The whole monistic ideology is false like a flower in the sky. Considering the monistic view, there would be neither a topic, nor a purpose, nor a person entitled to pursue this path. So, this philosophy has no connection with the scriptures, for a connection is possible only with something factually existent.

In the sixth chapter, all sorts of arguments put by the monist are refuted. According to the view of the Vedas, by distinctions such as knower, knowable, and knowledge, difference is perceived to exist in the non-dual Supreme. This perception is absolutely real, and not delusive. Non-difference is not the highest truth. To become Brahman is not the real result; the real result is realizing the bliss of Brahman. Non-difference from the Supreme is not found in the scriptures. The soul is not merely dull consciousness; it is rather an entity with specific qualities, endowed with the attributes of being a knower and enjoyer. In the soul, the meanings of the words ‘asmat‘ (the ‘I‘) and ‘yuṣmat‘ (the ‘you‘) are not creations of maya; they are its real characteristics. Words such as’satya ‘(truth), ‘jnāna‘ (consciousness), and ‘ananta‘ (unlimited) are all qualities of the Supreme. Sentences from the śruti, such as ‘dva suparṇā sayuja‘, reveal that difference is absolutely real. The jivas are not a superimposition in the material manifestation; rather, they are different real entities related to the Supreme. The difference between their respective svarūpas is real. The form of the Supreme described in the śruti is not imaginary. The monists ‘instructions on spiritual practice are purposeless. The beginning (upakrama) and the end (upasaṁhāra), repetition (abhyāsa), singularity (apūrvatā), result (phala), praise (arthavāda), and reasoning (upapatti) — upon reflecting by means of these six kinds of indications, real spiritual difference will be observed in all the sentences of the Vedas, and one will conclude that the Supreme possesses attributes. This universe is said to be a form of the Supreme because its sustenance is dependent on the Supreme. This universe is proved a form of the Supreme due to its relying on the Supreme and being pervaded by the Supreme. It is a mistake to consider that in the stage of worldly life, this universe, full of ignorance, is independent from the Supreme. If conclusions are drawn based on partial scriptural statements, there will be mistakes. There is no mistake in a conclusion accepted on the basis of the whole śāstra. This material manifestation made by the energy of the Supreme is never described in the scriptures as false. Having impermanent characteristics such as creation, this universe is said to be impermanent. The universe is real, since in the three phases of time it is not false. Rather, the universe is dependent on God. The Supreme has multifarious potencies, such as the creative energy. This is proven both in the śruti and the smṛti. The Supreme Lord, Para-brahman, is distinguished by having full opulence, strength, fame, beauty, knowledge, and renunciation. All living beings dwell within Him, and He dwells within all of them. He has no negative qualities. Viṣṇu is proven to be the Supreme Lord, beyond anyone else’s greatness. He is independent and replete with pastimes. He is the Supreme Truth even for the liberated souls. Being devoid of qualities is a partial attribute or manifestation of the Supreme. Merely by absorbing the intellect in the Supreme, ignorance does not disappear, but this is possible by surrender unto the Supreme. The Vedas only deny the limitation peculiar to material form. They do not deny form altogether. Rather, they actually describe His inconceivable, transcendental form! The falsity of the material manifestation is refuted by Vedic sentences such as ‘yato vā imāni bhūtāni’.

According to the Buddhist view, the material manifestation is false, hence the smṛti acknowledges Māyāvāda as disguised Buddhism (pracchannaṁ bauddham). The Buddhist theory of momentariness (kṣaṇikavāda) is found in Māyāvāda in the doctrine of drsti-srsti. Māyāvāda is just a reflection of the Buddhist theory of Śūnyavāda (voidism). Holding the concepts of existent, non-existent, existent and non-existent, and indescribable, the Māyāvādis can be called associates of the Jains. The theory of difference, which is proved as the meaning of all the Vedas, is transcendental.

In the seventh chapter, the author explains the Māyāvādi’s view as follows: “The Supreme Absolute Truth is mere consciousness (jnāna), one (eka) without a second (advitlya), truth (satya), unlimited (ananta), devoid of potencies and other attributes, and devoid of the three kinds of difference starting with sajatiya-bheda. Being expressed in the ‘bhava’sense (impersonal passive), the word ‘jnāna‘ (consciousness) is a nondual element denoted by words such as ’samvit’, ‘anubhuti’, and ‘jnapti’. If expressed as a grammatical case-relation, there is a flaw in the form of difference.” This statement is absolutely ridiculous. As demonstrated by the very definition of ‘jnāna‘ as ‘that by which something is known, ‘don‘t you have to swallow the implied existence of potency (śakti)? The existence of potency being implied, distinctions such as knowable, knower, and knowledge certainly ensue. Why should you be afraid to accept the existence of potency? Potency is unlimited, and consciousness is unlimited. By accepting the existence of potency, consciousness is not concealed at all. If someone argues that the sense of ‘I ‘belongs to the material body, this is not true. The ‘I ‘is the knower, the substratum of the quality of knowledge. Knowledge is a natural attribute of the soul. Just as by the sun’s being light itself and also an illuminator, no duality ensues, similarly, by consciousness being a knower, no duality ensues. Therefore, the Supreme is endowed with unlimited potencies such as consciousness. What is consciousness ? Consciousness is that which, by its own existence, is luminous to its own substratum, or that which illuminates its own objects. Consciousness devoid of attributes is unproven. If consciousness is proved, then it has potency. The concept of T cannot be called anything other than the soul. The concept of T has the pure soul for its basis’. I know, ‘‘I am happy, ‘ — such knowledge is admitted in śruti sentences like ’sukham aham asvāpsam na kincid avediṣam, ‘“I slept happily and was not aware of anything.” Self-consciousness is an attribute that has the pure knower for its basis and is nothing other than the soul. The false ego, which like the body is different from the soul, is a material element produced from the mahat-tattva. That is a totally different ego than the one based on pure consciousness. Pure self-consciousness is intrinsic to one’s svarūpa. It does not cause worldly life; rather, it removes worldly life. If the material false ego were the jiva’s real self, then who would strive for liberation? If one’s self is destroyed at liberation, then why would one hear instructions about liberation and strive for it? Therefore, the self-consciousness of those who aspire for liberation is actually based on the pure self. In this connection, just consider the words of Vāmadeva and others. The difference as subject and object is interwoven in the existence of consciousness. The soul is the one who experiences, and consciousness is its attribute. This attribute is understood as self-luminous at the time of revealing objects and is apprehended by knowledge at other times.

In the eighth chapter, the author corroborates that the soul, the ‘I ‘endowed with attributes like being a doer and whose svarūpa is both a knower and consciousness itself, is twofold: God (iśvara) and the individual living entity (jiva). God is all-pervading. He is the creator of the universe through the agency of His potencies. Through the agency of material nature, which is subordinate to His will, He is both the efficient and the material cause of the universe. The material manifestation constituted by prakṛti and the living entities is eternally different from God, Who is its shelter. The Supreme’s threefold energy — superior, inferior, and marginal — is eternally non-different from His svarūpa and is the cause of the creation, maintenance, and dissolution of the universe. Therefore, the universe is actually real and is eternally maintained by Lord Kṛṣṇa. The individual living entities are atomic, many in number, dependent on God, doers, thinkers, knowers, and conscious. All qualities eternally exist in the jivas in a small proportion. Although the living entities are atomic conscious beings, they have eternal attributes. They are parts and parcels of God and have atomic consciousness. Being like sparks emanating from the Supreme, they are His subordinate parts and parcels. Just as a philosopher’s stone creates large quantities of gold without undergoing any transformation, similarly unlimited jivas emanate from the Supreme, Who remains eternally immutable. Therefore, the living entities are eternally different from the Supreme. Being manifest from the marginal potency of the Supreme, the jivas are non-different from Him, the possessor of all potencies. Hence, between God and the living entities there is inconceivable difference and non-difference (acintya-bhedābheda). This relation is not the same difference and non-difference between cause and effect, etc., as accepted in other philosophical systems. In this view, the Māyāvādi concept of tatastha-lakṣaṇā does not apply. According to the concept of inconceivable difference and non-difference, the difference between God and the individual soul is eternal. The Māyāvādis claim that the goal of life is the annihilation of the concept of ‘I’. In such case, neither the removal of distress nor the attainment of happiness is accomplished; only the annihilation of the soul. That is all.

The view that God and the jivas are different is the conclusion of all scriptures. Despite being a part of the Supreme, by becoming averse towards the Supreme Lord, the jiva is gradually overwhelmed by the illusory energy. By saintly association, all illusion resultant from aversion towards the Supreme Lord disappears. It is by saintly association that the jiva receives the Lord‘s favour and turns towards Him. By the performance of continuous loving devotional service, the ignorance that covers the awareness of the Supreme Lord‘s eternal qualities is destroyed. Afterward, when the ignorance that covers the awareness of the Supreme Lord‘s svarūpa is dispelled, one can directly see Him. In this matter, mercy alone is the root cause. The scriptural sentences about non-difference merely express that everything is maintained by the Supreme, is dependent on Him, has Him for its basis, and is pervaded by Him. In fact, they don‘t indicate non-difference. In some places, the scriptures teach non-difference between God and the living entity due to their dwelling in the same place and sharing the same views. In some places, they speak of non-difference because the living entities and material nature are energies of the Supreme Lord, the possessor of all energies, and are hence non-different from Him. That is all. In such sentences, there is no defect at all. To propound absolute non-duality on the basis of all these statements is nothing but a mistake born of sheer ignorance. Some Vaiṣṇava philosophers posit the following: “The Supreme exists in the form of everything. Therefore, by syncretizing the view of both Śaṇkara and Madhva, all scriptures are duly respected and bhakti is not undermined.” The defect in this view is that if the material manifestation is the Supreme, then there would be no reason for renouncing it. If someone claims that the universe is false, then the Vedas would be contradicted. Moreover, there would be no reason for God’s qualities, such as His mercy towards the living entities. Keeping away from such a view of difference and non-difference, one should rather accept the conclusion of acintya-bhedābheda.

Revealing Śri Vidyābhūṣaṇa’s extraordinary scholarship in the Vedas and Vedānta, as well as his vast spiritual realization, this book first establishes the nature of the Supreme Lord and demonstrates Kṛṣṇa’s status as being Himself the Supreme Lord, following which it discusses the attainment of all goals through the practice of devotional service. Exhibiting supreme and astonishing attributes, both mundane and supramundane while being situated in His own abode, the topmost planet named Goloka, Lord Kṛṣṇa manifests transcendental pastimes in the material universe. Moreover, He creates the universe and establishes the demigods, whose status is that of His separated expansions, to serve in the capacity of superintendents; through His personal expansion in the form of Viṣṇu, the Supreme God, Whose nature is pure goodness (śuddha-sattva), He maintains the universe according to His own will. The lotus feet of Lord Viṣṇu, Who is to be known from all the Vedas, should be worshipped by everyone. Demonstrating that with due respect to all demigods, Lord Viṣṇu’s worship is the duty of all living entities, three views caused by ignorance, which hinder exclusive devotion to Viṣṇu, are discussed, according to which (l) all divine beings are one; (2) Brahma, Viṣṇu, and Siva are one; and (3) Hari and Hara are one. Subsequently, after refuting various doctrines opposed to pure devotion, such as Kevalatmaikavada, Advaitavāda, Māyāvāda, and Nirguna-jnānavada, all of which support one another and hold corrupt views, the author proclaims the doctrine of pure devotional service in the eighth chapter by explaining the pure Supreme as worshipable, the pure living entity as the worshipper, as well as the nature of liberation.

## First Chapter — Pāncajanya

### Text 1

*vedās tathā smṛti-giro yam acintya-śaktiṁ*  
*sṛṣṭi-sthiti-pralaya-kāraṇam āmananti \|*  
*tam śyāmasundaram avikriyam ātma-mūrtiṁ*  
*sarveśvaraṁ praṇati-mātra-vaśam bhajāmah \|\| 1 \|\|*

**Перевод:**

**We worship the immutable Ṡyāmasundara, the Supreme Lord of everything, Whose transcendental body is non-different from His soul, and Who can be controlled only by submissiveness. The Vedas and the smṛti repeatedly state that He has inconceivable energies and is the cause of the creation, maintenance, and destruction of the universe.**

**Комментарий:**

*yasya śriman-nāma-piyūṣa-varṣair āsid viśvaṁ dhūta-tāpaṁ kilaitat*  
*svāvirbhāvollāsitānanda-sindhur jiyāt sa śri-kṛṣṇa-caitanya-candraḥ*

**All glories to moon-like Lord Śri Kṛṣṇa Caitanya, Whose appearance manifests an ocean of bliss. By nectarean showers of His Holy Names, this universe becomes free from distress.**

*sāndrānanda-syandigovinda-bhāṣyaṁ*  
*jiyād etat sindhu-gāmbhirya-sambhṛt*  
*yasmin sadyaḥ saṁśrute mānavānām*  
*moha-cchedi jāyate tattva-bodhaḥ*

**All glories to the Govinda-bhāṣya, which is profound like an ocean that flows with intense bliss. Upon hearing it, knowledge of the truth immediately arises in men’s hearts, removing all illusion.**

*ālasyād apravṛttiḥ syāt puṁsāṁ yad grantha-vistare*  
*siddhānta-ratne saṁkṣiptā ṭippaṇi kriyate‘tra tat*

**Out of laziness, people may not feel inclined to do through a voluminous book. Therefore, I have written a concise commentary on the Siddhānta-ratnaṁ.**

The learned Vidyābhūṣaṇa, who is also known as Baladeva and is exclusively devoted to Lord Govinda, composed a commentary on the Brahma-sūtra entitled Govinda-bhāṣya. Desiring to compose its introduction in the form of this text named Siddhānta-ratnaṁ in order to substantiate it, he now recites an auspicious invocation starting with the words ‘vedas tatha, ‘expressing devotion for Śri Ṡyāmasundara, Who is directly the Supreme Lord Himself in the form of a deity. “We (vayam) worship (bhajāmaḥ) the Supreme Lord of everything (sarveśvaram), ” i.e., “we propitiate Him by devotional service, such as offering humble obeisances (pranati)’. ‘Expecting that someone may ask what His qualities are, the author says, “The Vedas as well as the smṛti repeatedly state (āmananti) that He is the cause (kāranam) of the creation (sṛṣṭi), maintenance (sthiti), and destruction (pralaya) of the universe. ”According to the Ṥridhara-kośa, the word ‘tatha‘ indicates similarity. How is it so? The author says, “He has inconceivable energies (acintya-śaktim)”. Someone may argue, “By taking on such a huge task, God would be subject to transformation characterized by effort and so on”. The author says, “He is immutable (avikriyam)». The idea is that He performs those activities by His mere will and therefore does not have the slightest trace of transformation. His soul, characterized by knowledge and bliss, is His very body. Such intense bliss and knowledge is devoid of outer and inner difference, like a golden statue, and is therefore said to be the ultimate goal of human life. This Supreme Lord, possessor of such transcendental attributes, has a blue complexion like that of a linseed flower. This is expressed by the name ‘Ṡyāmasundara’. This form propitiates easiness of meditation. The words ‘pranati-matra ‘(by mere submissiveness) express how the Lord is affectionate and becomes easily propitiated. It should be understood that only those whose vision has been stolen by the darkness of ignorance do not see the Supreme Lord as such.

### Text 2

*gaja-patir anukampā-sampadā yasya sadyaḥ*  
*samajani niravadyaḥ sāndram ānandam rcchan \|*  
*nivasatu mama tasmin kṛṣṇa-caitanya-rūpe*  
*matir atimadhurimṇā dipyamāne murārau \|\| 2 \|\|*

**Перевод:**

**May my mind always dwell on Lord Murāri’s form as Śri Kṛṣṇa Caitanya, which shines with exceeding sweetness. By receiving abundant mercy from Him, King Pratāparudra attained intense joy and became free from defects.**

**Комментарий:**

Starting with the words ‘gaja-pati, ‘the author now prays that his mind may be fixed upon his beloved Caitanya Mahāprabhu and Rasikānanda Prabhu. Here, ‘gaja-pati ‘means Prataparudra, the King of Utkala, and also the elephant well-known by the name ‘Gopāladāsa’. The former became free from his royal pride (niravadyaḥ), and the latter became free from his animal nature. Murāri is Lord Kṛṣṇa in the form of Śri Kṛṣṇa Caitanya, the beloved son of Mother Śaci Alternatively, this name can also refer to Rasika Murāri, who belonged to the fourth generation ofācāryas preceding the author, and who described Lord Kṛṣṇa Caitanya as the Supreme Lord Himself This kind of figure of expression (alaṅkāra) employed here is called ‘śleṣa‘ (pun).

### Text 3

*devābhyarthana-mandareṇa mathitād bhaktindirābhūd yataḥ*  
*śrimad-bhāgavatākhya-nirjara-taruḥ sat-sūtra-ratnotkaraḥ \|*  
*divyad-giti-sudhāṁsukāmṛta-rucir jṅānaṁ ca dhanvantarih*  
*sa śri-vyāsa-mahāmbudhir vijayate prityai samantāt satām \|\| 3 \|\|*

**Перевод:**

**All glories to Śri Vyāsadeva, who is like a great ocean, which upon being churned at the Mandara-like request of Devarṣi Nārada, gave rise to the goddess of fortune in the form of bhakti, a desire-fulfilling tree in the form of the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam, a heap of jewels in the form of the Brahma-sūtra, Dhanvantari in the form of knowledge, and a moon full of light and nectar in the form of the enlightening Bhagavad-gitā for the complete satisfaction of the devotees.**

**Комментарий:**

Starting with the word ‘deva‘, the author now speaks about the revered Vyāsadeva’s triumph, which smashes all fallacious philosophies. The meaning of the verse is clear. Knowledge (jṅānam) is the understanding by which one discriminates between God, the individual soul, and material nature. The figure of expression here is called ’sāṅga-rūpaka‘ (metaphor with several limbs). By the manifestation of abundant jewels, the superiority over other oceans is expressed. This is an example of ‘vyatireka’, dissimilarity out of pre-eminence. Here, there is also an instance of the figure of expression known as ‘tulya-yogitā ‘(equal joining) because the verb ‘abhūt ‘(arose) is equally connected with all the comparisons starting with ‘bhaktindirā’.

### Text 4

*govindābhidham indirāśrita-padaṁ hasta-stha-ratnādivat*  
*tattvaṁ tattvavid-uttamau kṣiti-tale yau darśayāṁ cakratuḥ \|*  
*māyāvāda-mahāndhakāra-paṭali-sat-puṣpavantau sadā*  
*tau śri-rūpa-sanātanau viracitāścaryau suvaryau stumaḥ \|\| 4 \|\|*

**Перевод:**

**We glorify the most excellent Śri Rūpa Gosvāmi and Śri Sanātana Gosvāmi, who are like a transcendental sun and moon, always dispelling the cover created by the dense darkness of impersonalism (Māyāvāda). Both performed wonders in this world, and being the best amongst knowers of the truth, they revealed the Supreme Absolute Truth named Govinda, Whose feet are the shelter of the goddess of fortune, just as one shows a jewel in the hand.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author glorifies the spiritual preceptors of his own philosophical school. The Supreme Absolute Truth (tattvam) is the Supreme Person. According to the Viśva-kośa(Va, 18),  the word ‘tattva ‘means a kind of musical instrument, one’s own nature, and the Supreme Soul. The affix ‘vat ‘(similar) here expresses the accuracy of their perception. “The Supreme Lord became directly manifest before their eyes in such way, ” — this is to be applied to the sentence. This is similarly understood in other instances also. Both of them have risen together like a shining transcendental (sat) sun and moon (puṣpavantau) to dispel the cover (patali) created by dense darkness (mahandhakara) in the form of impersonalism (māyāvāda). Such a sun and moon are said to be transcendental because they are devoid of defects such as heat, waning, and blemishes. According to the Amara-kośa (1.3.10), by the single expression ‘puspavantau, ‘the sun and the moon are indicated. Because both have done such deeds, they are described as having performed wonders (viracitāścaryau) and so on.

### Text 5

**Перевод:**

**It is seen in this world that people are inclined to acquire happiness and remove distress. Neither of these goals can be possible without a means, therefore the great sages, who know how to discriminate between the essential and the unessential, declare the proper mode. According to the Sāṁkhya philosophy of Kapila, the lack of discrimination between the individual soul (puruṣa) and material nature (prakṛti) is the source of one’s threefold miseries. By repeatedly discriminating between both, once beginning less indiscrimination ceases to exist, material nature’s power over the individual soul disappears, and thus one’s threefold miseries are destroyed. Such a destruction is an eternal effect because of its nature as non-existence (abhāva). The very destruction of suffering is taken figuratively as the attainment of happiness, just as one becomes happy upon being rid of a burden, and happiness is nothing more than that.**

**Комментарий:**

Desiring to describe the Supreme Absolute Truth revealed by the Vedānta philosophy composed by the revered Vyāsadeva, and to refute the theories made by others, the author here introduces the topic. People are inclined to acquire happiness and remove distress, but they do not know the means to achieve this. The great sages explain the means by which people can accomplish both goals. First of all, the author presents the philosophy of Kapila. The three kinds of suffering are called ādhyātmika, ādhibhautika, and ādhidaivika. The first one, ādhyātmika suffering is twofold, as pertaining to the body and the mind. Bodily suffering is caused by an imbalance of wind, bile, and phlegm. Mental suffering is caused by lust, anger, and so on. Because in both cases the means is to be effected internally, these two kinds of suffering are called ‘adhyātmika‘ (related to oneself). Adhibhautika suffering is caused by humans, animals, and other living entities. Adhidaivika suffering is caused by yakṣas, rāksaṣas, and other similar beings that possess one’s body. The root cause of these threefold miseries is material nature, and they are certainly destroyed when the influence of material nature ceases on account of discriminative knowledge. Although suffering caused by bodily ailments, lusty and evil spirits can disappear by respectively taking medicine, accepting a wife, and worshipping Durgā with mantras, it appears once again, just like cutaneous herpes recurs if the root cause has not disappeared. Therefore, the cause for the ultimate destruction of suffering is the practice of discriminative knowledge. An existent object, such as an earthen pot, is a temporary effect, but something non-existent, such as the destruction of an earthen poty is an eternal effect, as a pot that is destroyed never appears again. Such is the destruction of suffering, from which one acquires happiness. It should be understood that the Kapila referred to here is the atheist philosopher born in the Agni dynasty, who propagated twenty-five elements. The Kapila Who appeared as Kardama Muni’s son is Lord Vāsudeva, and He described twenty-six elements.

### Text 6

**Перевод:**

**According to Patanjali, by attaining maturity in yoga practice and detachment by reflecting on the nature of prakṛti and puruṣa while performing yama, niyama, āsana, pranayama, pratyāhāra, dhāraṇā, dhyāna, and samprajnāta-samādhi upon obtaining the mercy of the Supreme Lord, one is then able to restrain the fivefold activities of the mind, after which one attains the state of asamprajňāta-samādhi known as dharma-megha. This is the means by which a person can attain happiness and remove distress.**

**Комментарий:**

Now the author speaks about the philosophy of Patanjali. Here, the eight limbs of the yoga process are shown. Non-violence (ahimsa), truthfulness (satya), refraining from stealing (āsteya), celibacy (brahmacarya), and renunciation of mundane possessions (aparigraha) are called ‘yama’. Cleanliness (sauca), contentment (santosa), austerity (tapaḥ), study of the scriptures (svādhyāya), and submission to God (isvara-pranidhana) are called ‘niyama’. This is understood from Patanjali‘s aphorisms (2.30;32). Postures (āsana) and breath exercises (pranayama) are well-known: To detach the senses from the sense-objects is called ‘pratyāhāra’. Concentration of the mind on the navel, the tip of the nose, and so on, without any specific object of thought, is called ‘dharana’. Exclusive meditation on a particular thought is called ‘dhyana’. Absorption when the activities of the mind (citta-vṛtti) are still present is called ’samprajnāta-samādhi’. Correct knowledge (pramāna), incorrect knowledge (viparyaya), imagination (vikalpa), sleep (nidrā), and memory (smṛti) — these are the five functions or states of the mind. That which like a cloud (megha) pours forth virtue (dharma) that causes the ultimate goal of life is called ‘dharma-megha’. Patanjali states (4.29): prasaṁkhyāne‘py akusidasya sarvathā viveka-khyāter dharma-meghaḥ samādhiḥ, “ When one is detached even from the results of wisdom, then the state of dharma-megha-samādhi occurs on account of his thorough discriminative knowledge.” The word ‘prasaṁkhyāna‘ means reflection about the mutually different natures among the elements. Although such reflection is present, one who has no desire for the fruits of such wisdom then attains dharma-megha-samādhi on account of his fully developed discriminative knowledge of all sorts, since different thoughts do not arise anymore. In this state, the mind is said to be devoid of functions. The word ‘asya‘ in this paragraph means ‘of a person’.

### Text 7

**Перевод:**

**According to Kaṇāda, the soul is all-pervading, distinct from the body, senses, and so on, and is endowed with nine specific qualities. By factual knowledge about the similarities and dissimilarities of substance (dravya), quality (guṇa), action (karma), generality (sāmānya), particularity (viśeṣa), and inherence (samavāya), one attains self-realization, which, accompanied by the worship of God, causes the destruction of the activities of those nine specific qualities and the total annihilation of their prior state of non-existence (prāg-abhāva). This itself is the attainment of happiness.**

**Комментарий:**

Now the Vaiśeṣika philosophy is explained. Cognition (buddhi), pleasure (sukha), pain (duḥkha)y desire (icchā), hate (dveṣa), effort (prayatna), virtue (dharma), sin (adharma), and mental impressions (bhāvanā) — these are the nine specific qualities pertaining to the soul out of the seventeen gunas mentioned in the Vaiśeṣika-sūtra (1.I.5J. The production of the activities of these nine specific qualities is possible because of their state of prior non-existence. In this philosophical system, at the stage of liberation, the soul is similar to a stone.

### Text 8

**Перевод:**

**According to Gautama, by the enunciation, definition, and examination of sixteen categories starting with pramāna and prameya, one ascertains the twelve kinds of prameyas, by which it is then possible to realize the individual soul and the Supreme Soul. On account of such realization, accompanied by the practice of hearing, reflecting, and meditating, the destruction of false knowledge and all past impressions occurs. Consequentially, the by-products of false knowledge, that is, attachment, aversion, and illusion, also cease to exist. Then, the byproducts of attachment, aversion, and illusion, namely virtuous and sinful activities, as well as the inborn tendency for such acts, all vanish. The karma previously accumulated is then enjoyed by the soul by accepting multiple bodies simultaneously.  However, upon its exhaustion, there is no beginning of a new material body, and thus one attains complete release from all the twenty-one kinds of suffering characterized by pain. This itself is the attainment of happiness.**

**Комментарий:**

Now the Nyāya philosophy is explained. By the word ‘ādi‘ (starting with), saṁśaya and the other categories are included. It is so stated by Gautama in the Nyāya-sūtra (l.l.l): pramāna-prameya-samsaya-prayojana-dṛṣṭānta-siddhāntavayava-tarka-nirnaya-vāda-jalpa-vitaṇḍā-hetvābhāsa-cchala-jati-nigraha-sthananam tattva-jnānān niḥsreyasādhigamah, “Liberation is attained by true knowledge of pramāna (means of knowledge), prameya (objects of knowledge), saṁśaya (doubt), prayojana (aim), dṛṣṭānta (example), siddhānta (conclusion), avayava (members of a syllogism), tarka (hypothetical reasoning), nirṇaya (settlement), vāda (discussion), jalpa (wrangling), vitaṇḍā (cavilling), hetvābhāsa (fallacy), chala (quibbling), jāti (sophisticated refutation), and nigraha-sthāna (point of defeat)”. The twelve kinds of prameya are so defined in the Nyāya-sūtra (1.1.9): ātma-śarirendriyārtha-buddhi-manaḥ-pravṛtti-doṣa-pretyabhāva-phala-duḥkhāpavargās tu prameyam, “Atmā (soul), śarira (body), indriya (senses), artha (sense objects), buddhi (cognition), manaḥ (mind), pravṛtti (activity), doṣa (defects), pretyabhāva (rebirth), phala (results), duḥkha (suffering), and apavarga (liberation) —these are the prameyas. The twenty-one kinds of suffering are as follows: the gross body, the six senses (including the mind), the six sense objects (including thoughts), the six varieties of cognition pertaining to the sense objects, pleasure, and pain.  In this philosophical system too, at the stage of liberation, the soul becomes similar to a stone.

### Text 9

**Перевод:**

**According to Jaimini, by performing auspicious ritualistic ceremonies described in the Vedas, one attains happiness and becomes free from suffering.**

**Комментарий:**

Now the author explains the philosophy of Jaimini. By the pious merit acquired by performing Vedic rituals in the form of worship of the Supreme Lord, sinful results are destroyed, and one attains happiness, heaven, and liberation. The peculiarities in these five philosophical systems should be understood in their respective treatises and will not be delineated here due to the apprehension that this book may become too voluminous.

### Text 10

**Перевод:**

**Certainly, none of these systems should be adopted as means for accomplishing the ultimate attainment of happiness and removal of distress, for the greatest preceptor, the revered Vyāsadeva, has refuted all these philosophical systems.**

**Комментарий:**

This paragraph is spoken in order to reject these jive philosophical systems. The word ‘hi ‘is used in the sense of certainty. The word ‘tayoḥ‘ refers to the attainment of happiness and the removal of distress. The ultimate attainment of happiness is that which never ends. The ultimate removal of distress is when it never reappears.

### Text 11

**Перевод:**

**However, self-knowledge and thorough knowledge about the transcendental nature and qualities of the Supreme Person, Who is well-known as the Supreme Lord of all, bring about the ultimate attainment of happiness and disappearance of distress. As stated in the śruti, such as the Svetāśvatara Upaniṣad (l.ll; 2.15): jnātva devaṁ sarva-pāśāpahāniḥ kṣiṇaiḥ kleśair janma-mṛtyu-prahāṇiḥ, tasyābhidhyānāt tṛtiyaṁ deha-bhede viśvaiśvaryaṁ kevalam āpta-kāmaḥ, “Knowing God, all bonds vanish, and when all distresses are thus destroyed, one’s repeated birth and death also vanish. By meditating on the Supreme Lord, upon breaking out of the subtle body, one attains His pure abode, which has unlimited opulence’s, and all one’s desires are fulfilled.” yadātma-tattvena tu brahma-tattvam dipopameneha yuktaḥ prapaśyet, ajaṁ dhruvaṁ sarva-tattvair viśuddhaṁ jnātvā devaṁ mucyate sarva-pāśaih, “When one is endowed with knowledge about the self, which is like a lamp, one then contemplates the Supreme Absolute Truth.
