Their idea is that it is not possible to assume that such universe has any creator, and thus, it is eternal. The compound ‘jnāna-vidah ‘means ‘those who know the scriptures’. They see (prapasyanti) the universe as having the Supreme for its cause and instigator (jhanatmakam). The author clarifies this by quoting the words ‘tvad-rupam’. Otherwise, the Kumaras would have said, “They see the universe as eternal and independent like You.

### Text 45

**Перевод:**

**Those who say otherwise also contradict the previous and subsequent statements in this scripture. For instance, the question and answer between Maitreya and Parasara are seen as follows: nirguṇasyaprameyasya suddhasyapy amalatmanah, katham sargadi-kartrtvam brahmano ‘bhyupagamyate (Viṣṇu Purāṇa, 1.3.l), “How can it be understood that the Supreme Soul, although immeasurable, pure, unalloyed, and devoid of material attributes, is the creator of the universe and everything ?” This is the question. The meaning is that activities like production are seen among the imperfect jivas in contact with the modes of nature and subordinate to material activities. However, how can such things be accepted in the Supreme, Who is distinct from them? The answer is as follows: saktayah sarva-bhavanam acintya-jnāna-gocarah, yato ‘to brāhmaṇas tas tu sargadya bhava-saktayah, bhavanti tapatam srestha pavakasya yathosnata (7b., 1.3-2), “As the powers in all existent things are perceived only through inconceivable knowledge, therefore inherent powers, such as that of creation, maintenance, and dissolution, all belong to the Supreme Lord, just as heat belongs to fire, О best of ascetics.” The meaning here is this: Because (yatah) the powers (saktayah) in all existent things (sarva-bhavanam) are understood only by inconceivable intelligence, are distinct from each other, and are conceived according to their effects, therefore (atah) all such inherent powers (bhava-saktayah), such as that of creation, belong to the Supreme Lord (brāhmaṇah), Who is the greatest of all and is kind to all. Thus, there is no contradiction in His being the creator of the universe. Such (tah) powers are inherent in Him, as stated here by the analogy of fire and heat. This is also confirmed by the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad (6.8): parasya śaktir vividhaiva sruyate svabhavikl jnāna-bala-kriya ca, “It is heard from the scriptures that the Supreme Lord has multifarious intrinsic superior energies in the form of knowledge, power, and action.” The word ‘tu ‘indicates how much more this is true in the case of the Lord. Here, it is very clear that the Supreme has various energies.**

**Комментарий:**

“Those who say, ” i.e., the monists. “Otherwise," i.e., “by taking to the apparent meaning. ” The Supreme Lord is devoid of the three modes of material nature (nirguṇasya). His unlimited powers, qualities, and nature are immeasurable (aprameyasya). He is pure (suddhasya), i.e., free from the slightest influence of karma. “Who is distinct from them, ” i.e., “since He is free from contact with the modes, the influence of karma, and incompleteness." Such powers are to be understood by inconceivable intelligence. The words ‘bhava-saktayah ‘mean ‘inherent powers’. According to the Medinl-kośa (Va, 20), the word ‘bhava ‘is used in the sense of existence, nature, meaning, action, soul, and birth. The author explains their being inherent in the Lord by the analogy of fire and heat. If even material things like gems and herbs have inherent inconceivable powers, how much more this is true in the case of the Supreme Lord. This is the meaning.

### Text 46

**Перевод:**

**The same thing is also seen in another place in the Viṣṇu Purāṇa: suddhe maha-vibhuty-akhye pare brahmani sabdyate, maitreya bhagavac-chabdah sarva-karana-karane, sambharteti tatha bharta bha-karo ‘rtha-dvayanvitah, neta gamayita srasta ga-kararthas tatha mune, aiśvaryasya samagrasya viryasya yasasah sriyah, jnāna-vairagyayos capi sannam bhaga itingana, vasanti yatra bhutani bhutatmany akhilātmani, sa ca bhutesv asesesu va-kararthas tato ‘vyayah (6.5-72-75), “O Maitreya, the word ‘bhagavan ‘is used to address the pure Supreme Lord, the cause of all causes, Who is named Mahavibhuti (possessor of great powers). О sage, the syllable ‘bha ‘has two meanings — maintainer and protector. The syllable ‘ga ‘means leader, carrier, and creator. Full opulence, strength, fame, beauty, knowledge, and renunciation — all these six, either combined or individually, are expressed by the word ‘bhaga’. All living beings dwell within Him, the Supreme Soul of everything, Whose svarūpa has been previously proved, and He dwells within all of them and is therefore imperishable. This is the meaning of the syllable ‘va’. jnāna-śakti-balaiśvarya-virya-tejaihsy asesatah, bhagavac-chabda-vacyani vina heyair gunadibhih (6.5.79), “Complete omniscience, power, might, opulence, strength, and splendour without any undesirable qualities is expressed by the word ‘bhagavan’. evam esa maha-sabdo bhagavan iti sattama, parama-brahma-bhutasya vasudevasya nanyagah, tatra pujya-padarthokti-paribhasa-samanvitah, sabdo ‘yam nopacarena anyatra hy upacaratah (6.5.76-77), “O best of saints, in this way, this great word, ‘bhagavan, ‘pertains to Lord Vāsudeva, the Supreme Absolute Truth, and to no one else. Applied to Lord Viṣṇu, this word bears such definition as to express that He is worshipable, and it is not to be taken in a figurative sense. It is only figurative when applied to someone else.”**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author declares that the Supreme Soul is expressed by the word ‘bhagavan’. These verses are found in the fifth chapter of the sixth part of the Viṣṇu Purāṇa. They refute the idea that the meaning of this word is figurative. By mentioning the purity of the Supreme Soul, Who is distinguished by His powers, these are to be considered inconceivable like His svarūpa, and thus He is expressed by the word ‘bhagavan, ‘which includes all powers both individually and combined. This is described by Parasara here. The syllable ‘bha ‘means maintainer (sambharta) and protector (bharta) of all. The syllable ‘ga ‘means leader (neta), Who brings about the purity of the nature of those who meditate on Him; carrier (gamayita), Who brings those pure souls to His own abode; and creator (srasta), Who reveals to them various kinds of happiness in His abode. The next verse describes the meaning of the syllables ‘bha ‘and ‘ga ‘combined. The word ’samagrasya ‘(full) follows each quality. The word ‘ingana ‘means ‘name’. The syllable ‘va ‘means that all living beings dwell within Him, Whose svarūpa has been previously proved (bhutātmani), the Supreme Soul of everything (nikhilātmani), i.e., Who is the material cause of everything by His form endowed with energies. Similarly, the syllable ‘va ‘also means that Lord Hari existed before everyone else and is the inner Soul of all, the cause of all causes. The next verse states the meaning of all the three syllables combined. The word ‘jnāna ‘means ‘omniscience’. ‘Power ‘(śakti) is His creatorship of multiple universes by His mere will’. Might ‘(bala) is His capacity to maintain such universes’. Opulence ‘(aiśvarya) is His control over all of them’. Strength ‘(virya) is His being immutable, or His capacity to deliver those who worship Him’. Splendour ‘(tejah) is His power to drive away the illusory energy. The word ‘asesatah ‘means ‘completely’. The idea is that these six attributes are expressed by the word ‘bhagavan ‘because they are not different than His svarūpa. He is devoid (vina) of any undesirable qualities (heyaih), such as the material modes of nature (gunadibhih) and their effects in the form of activities and fruits. The meaning of the verse ‘evam esa maha-śabdah ‘is clear. The word ‘bhagavan ‘bears (samanvitah) such definition (paribhasa) as to express (ukti) that Lord Viṣṇu is the object (padartha) to be worshipped (pujya). It is not to be taken in a figurative sense (na upacarena) when applied to the Supreme Soul (tatra). Whose characteristics such as being the maintainer have been described. It is only figurative when applied to others (anyatra), such as great sages and kings, who have a small portion of those qualities. Definition (paribhasa) is that which establishes a rule where there is none.

### Text 47

**Перевод:**

**Therein (6.5.83-87), it is also said: sa sarva-bhuta-prakṛtir vikara-gunams ca dosams ca mune vyatitah, atita-sarvavarano ‘khilatma tenastrtam yad bhuvanantarale, “O sage, Lord Vāsudeva is the material cause of all created beings, beyond defects, transformations, and the modes of nature. He is the Supreme Soul of everything, beyond all coverings. Whatever exists within the three worlds is pervaded by Him.” samasta-kalyana-gunatmako’sau sva-śakti-lesad bhrta-bhuta-sargah, iccha-grhitabhimatoru-dehah samsadhitasesa-jagad-dhito’sau, aHe is the embodiment of all auspicious qualities, and everything created is supported by a portion of His energy. The welfare of the whole universe is accomplished by Him, and His exalted form is under the control of the desires of His devotees and is considered by them as His very svarūpa.” tejo-balaiśvarya-mahavabodha-suvirya-śakty-adi-gunaikarasih, parah paranam sakala na yatra klesadayah santi paravarese, “He is the highest among the highest, an aggregate of qualities like splendour, strength, opulence, great wisdom, great heroism, and power. Nothing like distress exists in Him, the Lord of the lower and the higher.” sa isvaro vyasti- samasti-rupo ‘vyakta-svarūpah prakata-svarūpah, sarvesvarah sarva-drk sarva-vetta samasta-śaktih paramesvarakhyah, “He is God, the Lord of all, Who is both manifest and unmanifest. All individuals and aggregates are a form of Him. He sees everything, knows everything, has all powers, and is known as ‘Paramesvara’. samjnayate yena tad asta-dosam śuddhaṁ param nirmalam eka-rupam, samdrsyate vapy atha gamyate va taj jnānam ajnānam ato ‘nyad uktam, “That by which His nature, which is pure, free from defects, supreme, unalloyed, and invariable, is understood, perceived, and attained, is called knowledge. Anything other than this is said to be ignorance.”**

**Комментарий:**

The word ’sah ‘refers to Lord Vāsudeva, Who is the material cause (prakṛtih) of all created beings (sarva-bhuta) through His sentient and insentient energies. He is beyond (atitah) all transformations (vikara), modes of nature (gunan), and defects (dosan) like cruelty, i.e., He is free from all these. He is beyond all coverings (atita-sarvavaranah), i.e., free from the covering of the illusory energy. He is the soul of everything (akhilatma), i.e., the originator of everything. Whatever (yat) exists within the three worlds (bhuvanantarale) is pervaded (astrtam) by Him (tena).

The next verse says that His qualities, such as being beyond transformations, are non-different from Him. The meaning is that all these qualities are intrinsic in His svarūpa. The word ‘bhrta ‘means ’supported’. As stated by Lord Kṛṣṇa in the Bhagavad-gita (10.42): vistabhyaham idam krtsnam ekamsena sthitojagat, “Supporting this whole universe by a single portion of Myself, I am standing here.” He is controlled (grhita) by the wishes (iccha) of devotees who are free from personal desires and have Him for their sole object, and His exalted (Uru) form (dehah) is accepted (abhimata) by them as non-different from His svarūpa. This is confirmed by His own words in the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (9.4.63,66). The next verse clarifies this last verse. The Lord is the highest (parah) among the highest (paranam), the eternally liberated souls. Nothing (na samastah) like distress (klesadayah) exist in Him (yatra), even in a small proportion. It is similarly stated by Patanjaji (Yoga-sūtra, 24): klesa-karma-vipakasair aparamrstah puruṣa-viśeṣa isvarah, “God is a special kind of person (puruṣa) untainted by distress, material activities, their results, and subconscious impressions. ” The words ‘vyasti-samasti-rupah 9 mean that through His energies, all individuals and aggregates are a form of Him, or that He is all these by qualities such as being their maintainer. His svarūpa is unmanifest (avyakta-svarilpah) to those averse to Him, and manifest (prakata-svarūpah) to those who turn towards Him. The meaning of the next half of this verse is clear. The knowledge being described in this scripture is that by which (yena) the Supreme Lord‘s svarūpa, whose characteristics have been thus described, is understood (samjnayate) through the mind, and perceived (samdrsyate) through eyes filled with devotion; and by which He is attained as one’s own Lord. Anything other (anyat) than this (atah), i.e., anything contradictory to this, or which furthers the idea of oneness, is said (uktam) to be ignorance.

### Text 48

**Перевод:**

**Here it is described that the Supreme Lord is the giver of heaven and liberation, is devoid of all defects, and is replete with a multitude of pure qualities like opulence and heroism. On account of His being the cause of the maintenance of all sentient and insentient elements different than Himself, they are a form of Him, Who creates, sustains, and destroys them. He is perceived through scriptural knowledge. From this description, it is understood that Brahman is not mere consciousness devoid of attributes. Other statements should be understood in the same way.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author reiterates the meaning of the verses quoted above in another way. The Lord is the giver of enjoyment in the heavenly planets and liberation. Other statements found in the Viṣṇu Purāṇa that seem to prove monism should be explained in the same way. With the apprehension that this book may become too long, they are not written here.

### Text 49

**Перевод:**

**In the verse ‘ksetrajnam capi mam viddhi, ‘the word ‘ca ‘adds the knower of the field. The word ‘ap’ is used in the sense of restriction. “You should know the field and the knower of the field as Me only, i.e., you should know that since they are dependent on Me and maintained by Me, both consist of Myself.” It is similarly said later: ksetra-ksetrajnayor jnānam yat taj jnānam matam mama, “Knowledge about the field and the knower of the field, i.e., whose object is their being dependent on Me and maintained by Me, that indeed is knowledge. This is my opinion.” The verse ‘na tad asti vina ‘ is explained in the same way.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author explains the statements of the Bhagavad-gita (13.3; 10.39) according to his own view. The word ‘anuktam ‘ means ’spoken later’. The meaning here is not that, “Except for Me, there is no moving or non-moving being. Due to My existence all moving and non-moving beings exist, just as an illusory serpent and the like exist due to the existence of a rope. ” Rather, the meaning is, “Being dependent on Me, maintained by Me, and pervaded by Me, all of them consist of Me». A different explanation of the verses starting with ‘ksetrajnam capi mam’ should be seen the Gita-bhūsana.

### Text 50

**Перевод:**

**Someone may argue, “The following is heard from the Kaivalya Upaniṣad (12-17): sa eva maya-parimohitatma sariram asthaya karoti sarvam, stry-anna-panadi-vicitra-bhogaih sa eva jagrat paritustim eti, ‘The Supreme Himself is God bewildered by may a. Assuming a body made of goodness, He creates the universe and becomes delighted with various enjoyable things like women, foodstuffs, drinks, and so on during the waking state’. svapne sa jivah sukha-duhkha-bhokta sva-mayaya kalpita-visva-loke, susupti-kale sakale viline tamo ‘bhibhutah sukha-rūpam eti, punas ca janmantara-karma-yogat sa eva jivah svapiti prabuddhah, ‘During sleep, He as the jiva experiences happiness and distress by His own illusory energy in a whole imaginary world. In the state of deep sleep, when all the senses have vanished, being overcome by ignorance, He as the jiva becomes happy. As a consequence of the activities of previous lives, the same jiva is again waken and sleeps’. pura-traye kridati yas ca jivas tatas tu jatam sakalam vicitram, adharam ānandam akhanda-bodharh yasmiml layam yati pura-trayam ca, ‘The Supreme as the jiva thus plays throughout the three worlds. From Him is produced this whole variegated universe. He is the reservoir, the bliss, and the absolute consciousness within which the universe merges’. etasmaj jayate prano manah sarvendriyani ca, kham vayur jyotir apas ca prthivi visvasya dharinl, ‘From Him are born the life air, the mind, all the senses, ether, air, fire, water, and earth, the support of everything’. yat param brahma sarvatma visvasyayatanam mahat, suksmat suksmataram nityam tat tvam eva tvam eva tat, ‘The Supreme Brahman, the Soul of all, the resting place of everything, the Great, the eternal, subtler than the subtlest — that is you, and you are that’. jagrat-svapna-susupty-adi-prapancam yat prakāśate, tad brahmaham iti jnātvā sarva-bandhaih pramucyate, ‘That by Whose existence the material manifestation appears in the waking, sleeping, and deep sleep states, that is Brahman, and that I am. Knowing this, one becomes free from all bondage’.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the non-dualist propounder of a single consciousness devoid of attributes diverges. Being bewildered by His own illusory energy (maya-parimohitatma), as in the analogy of the fisherman who is actually a king’s son, assuming (asthaya) a body (sariram) prominently made of the mode of goodness, the very same (sa eva) Supreme Soul becomes God, Hiranyagarbha, and then creates (karoti) the whole (sarvam) universe. Due to ignorance about His Own Self, becoming the individual soul (jivah), during the waking state (jagrat), the same Supreme Soul becomes delighted (paritustim eti) with enjoyable (bhogaih) things like women (stri), foodstuffs (anna), and drinks (pana). During sleep (svapne), He as the jiva experiences happiness and distress (sukhadi-bhokta). In the state of deep sleep (susupti-kale), when all the senses and the false ego have vanished (viline), being overcome by ignorance (tamo ‘bhibhUtah), He becomes happy (sukha-rūpam eti). As stated in theśruti: sukham aham asvapsam na kincid avedisam, “I slept happily and was not aware of anything». As a consequence of the activities of previous lives (janmantara-karma-yogat), the same jiva is waken up (prabuddhah) and sleeps (svapiti), i.e., again attains the results of its work, such as ascending on a palanquin, etc. He Who is (yah) the Supreme Soul, being now the jiva, plays (kridati) throughout the three worlds (pura-traye) or the three states. From Him as God, Hiranyagarbha, is produced (jatam) this whole (sakalam) variegated universe (vicitram) characterized bymovingand non-moving beings. When this Supreme Soul becomes God and the jiva, even then He is the same Brahman in the form of bliss (ānandam), absolute consciousness (mahavabodham), and so on. The universe (pura-trayam) merges (layam yati) within Him (yasmin) at the time of the final dissolution and at the time of realizing the self “From Him” (etasmat) means from God, Hiranyagarbha, the Supreme Soul. The śruti then instructs one who is inquisitive: yat param brahma, etc. “The soul of all” (sarvatma) means ‘Who is the svarūpa of all knowers of the field, or jivas’. He is the resting place of everything (visvasyayatanam), i.e., just as an illusory serpent and the like exist due to the existence of a rope, so does everything appear because of His existence. The words ‘yat prakāśate ‘mean ‘from which it appears, ‘‘by whose existence it appears’.

### Text 51

**Перевод:**

**“Therefore, the quoted verse from the Bhagavad-gita should be explained in this way: Although being the Supreme Lord, by avidya He becomes the knower of the field, just as a rope becomes a snake. The instruction ‘ksetrajnam capi mam viddhi ‘given by the Supreme Lord, the greatest authority, is meant for the destruction of this avidya. Just as the misapprehension of a snake is removed when someone reliable instructs that ‘this is a rope, and not a snake,’ similarly the misapprehension of oneself as a jiva disappears by hearing this verse.” This idea is foolish, for in this way no instruction would be possible. For instance, does the instructor, the Supreme Lord, know the truth or not? In the first case, knowing that there is no second self, He would not see Arjuna and others as different than Himself, and thus no instruction to them would be possible. In the second case, this would also not be possible, for one who is ignorant cannot teach spiritual knowledge. Nor is it possible to say that on the basis of the śruti ’sa eva maya-parimohitama, ‘the Supreme Lord is affected by avidya, for according to the śruti: yah sarvajnah sarvavit (Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad, 1.1.9)9 “The Supreme Lord is omniscient, all-knowing;” parasya śaktir vividhaiva sruyate svabhavikl jnāna-bala-kriya ca (Svetdsvatara Upaniṣad, 6.8), “It is heard from the scriptures that the Supreme Lord has multifarious intrinsic superior energies in the form of knowledge, power, and action.” The smṛti also confirms this by statements such as: vedaham samatitani vartamanani carjuna, bhavisyani ca bhutani mam tu veda na kascana: (Bhagavad-gita, 7.26), “O Arjuna, I know all beings that existed in the past, that exist at present, and that will exist in the future. Yet nobody knows Me.”**

**Комментарий:**

The word ‘atra ‘refers to the verse ‘ksetrajnam capi mam’. “By avidya, ” i.e., by perplexing ignorance. The words ‘ksetrajna-bhavah ‘mean ‘the state of being a jiva’. The meaning of the rest of the sentence is clear. “In the first case, ” i.e., in case He knows the truth. “In the second case, ” i.e., if He does not know the truth. The word ‘māyā ‘means ‘bewildering ignorance’. By the word ‘adi ‘(etc.), other verses are included, such as this one spoken by Lord Brahma: vilajjamanaya yasya sthatum iksapathe ‘muya, vimohita vikatthante mamaham iti durdhiyah (Śrimad-Bhāgavatam, 2.5.13): “Those of poor intellect boast, ‘I am so, and this is mine, ‘being bewildered by His illusory energy, which is embarrassed to stand before His eyes. ” Therefore, since this is contradicted by many scriptures, the objection raised by quoting the śruti ’sa eva maya-parimohitatma, etc’. is driven far away. The illusory energy certainly does not bewilder its own substratum.

### Text 52

**Перевод:**

**Someone may argue, “Although Lord Krsna has realised nonduality, His vision of others as different is a kind of badhitanuvrtti, and hence it is not impossible that He is an instructor.” This is not true, for it is actually contradicted by their illustration. Although the perception of water in a mirage persists (anuvrtti) even after being proved false (badhita), one does not engage in fetching or drinking that water. Similarly, although the vision of difference may persist even after being proved wrong by knowledge of non-duality, one would not engage in giving instructions, since the object of instruction is understood as something false.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author points out the flaw in the idea that instruction is possible, as demonstrated by the monists with the analogy of the mirage in the desert. “For it is actually contradicted by their illustration,” i.e., “because the analogy used by the monists is incompatible’. ‘According to the Amara-kośa (1.2.37), the words ‘mrgatrsna ‘and ‘maricika ‘are synonyms of ‘mirage’.

### Text 53

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, this cannot even be called badhitanuvrtti, for by means of such knowledge of the non-duality of the soul to prove the opposite as false, there should be the destruction of the ignorance that causes the perception of duality between other souls. When the perception of two moons happens, by knowledge that there is only one moon, the cause of the perception of two moons, such as real visual defects, is not destroyed, and therefore badhitanuvrtti is suitable here. On the other hand, in the given illustration, since duality and its perception are destroyed by knowledge of the non-duality of the soul, badhitanuvrtti is not possible whatsoever. Thus, this argument is insignificant. Therefore, since their sustenance, activities, and so on are dependent on Him, the jiva’s non-difference from God is figurative. Hence, by being surcharged with deep friendship with the Supreme Lord, the creator, and maintainer of the universe, such a jiva becomes liberated. This is what is said in the Kaivalya Upaniṣad (l7)**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author says that badhitanuvrtti is not possible in this idea of yours (the opponent’s), as there is no reason for it. The words ‘tadrsatma-jnanena ‘mean ‘by knowledge about the absolute non-duality of the soul’. Someone may argue, “If it is so, then how should be the meaning of the text ’sa eva maya-parimohitatma’ ?” ‘The author replies, “Therefore, since their activities, etc. ” The meaning is that because the jivas’sustenance, activities, and so on are dependent on God, the statements about the non-difference between God and the jivas found in the quoted texts from the Bhagavad-gita and Kaivalya Upaniṣad is figurative. “Hence, ” i.e., since non-difference is figurative, since non-difference is not the primary meaning. Such jiva that was bewildered by God’s illusory energy becomes liberated by worshipping Him in the mood of friendship. This is what is said in the text ‘yat param brahma’. When there is deep friendship, the mood ‘I am you, and you are I ‘is clear.

### Text 54

**Перевод:**

**The monists hold the idea that the universe is imagined to exist on pure consciousness on account of avidya and is to be set aside by knowledge. This view is refuted by the same argument. Moreover, it is not defined who is the one imagining. Since it has no distinguishing attributes, Brahman cannot imagine the universe. Nor can the jivas, since they did not exist before being imagined. Nor can avidya, for it is insentient.**

**Комментарий:**

The author writes the present paragraph in order to refute the meaning of the text ’sa eva maya-parimohitatma ‘as interpreted by the monists. According to them, consciousness being non-dual, the jivas and everything else are imagined to exist only due to ignorance of the truth, just as in the analogy of the fisherman who is actually a king’s son. However, when the truth is realised, their existence is proved false. This idea is refuted by the same argument that refuted the idea that the Supreme Soul bewildered by māyā becomes the jiva’. ‘Since it has no distinguishing attributes,” i.e., because in this case, Brahman would have attributes. “Before being imagined, ” i.e., if the universe is an imagination, the jivas must actually be said to be parts of it, and it also cannot be said that they exist before the imagination of the universe, for then the jivas would be their own substratum. In illustrations like the shell and silver, it is seen that the one who imagines is conscious and has a mind. Avidya is insentient, hence it cannot imagine.

### Text 55

**Перевод:**

**Furthermore, avidya cannot be called real, for in that case its disappearance would not happen, and duality would occur. Nor can it be unreal, for this contradicts clear perception. Nor is their desired view proved by claiming that avidya is different than existent and nonexistent, for there is no evidence of avidya as such. Nor is the text ‘na sad asit ‘ evidence of that, for the meaning conveyed there is this: Individual sentient and insentient beings are expressed by the words ’sat ‘and ‘asat’. At the time of the destruction of both, they have no separate state because they are then merged in the collective unconscious, which is expressed by the word ‘tamah’. The collective unconscious expressed by the word ‘tamah ‘is seen in the Subala Upaniṣad (2): bhutadir mahati viliyate mahan avyakte viliyate vyaktam aksare viliyate ‘ksaram tamasi viliyate tama eki-bhavati parasmin parasman na san nasan na sad-asat, “Bhūtadi merges in mahat-tattva, mahat-tattva merges in avyakta, avyakta merges in aksara, aksara merges in tamah, and tamah becomes one with the Supreme. Above the Supreme there is nothing sentient or insentient, nor sentient and insentient.” The Viṣṇu Purāṇa (1.2.23) also says: naho na ratrir na nabho na bhumir nasit tamo jyotir abhuc ca nanyat, srotradi-buddhyanupalabhyam ekam pradhanikam brahma pumams tadasit, “During the time of universal destruction, there was no day, no night, no sky, no earth, no darkness (tamah), no light, and nothing else. The only thing existing then was material nature, the Supreme Lord, and the jivas, none of which were perceptible by sensory knowledge.” Here, it is clear that material nature, the Supreme Lord and thejivas remain existing during the universal destruction.**

**Комментарий:**

In order to refute avidya as supposed by the opponent, the author writes the present paragraph. If avidya is real and is imagining the universe, then it would not disappear, and difference would occur. If avidya is unreal, then the clear perception that 7 am ignorant ‘would not happen. Someone may argue, “Avidya should not be considered real or unreal, but it is just said to be the one imagining. Its true nature is proved when it is defined as indescribable». In response, the author says, “Nor is their desired view, etc». “Avidya as such,” i.e., avidya being different than existent and nonexistent. The text ‘na sad asit ‘is also no evidence. Things like earthen pots are existent; things like flowers in the sky are non-existent; only existent things like earthen pots may exist or not according to time, place, and circumstance. These are the three kinds of perceptions experienced by the wise, and therefore this śruti gives no evidence to any other perception. It should not be forgotten that this has been previously demonstrated. The author then explains the meaning of the quoted śruti according to his own view and quotes the śruti and the smṛti to justify the stated meaning. The word ‘bhūtadih ‘means ‘false-ego in the mode of ignorance’. The verse ‘naho na ratrih ‘is from the Viṣṇu Purāṇa (1.2.23). During the time of universal destruction (pralaye), there was no day (ahah) and no night (ratrih), since there was no sun. There was also no darkness (tamah), since there was no night. There was no light, as there was no day. Sky (nabhah), earth (bhūmih), and anything else also did not exist. Then what did exist? Only three things, none of which were perceptible (anupalabhyam) by sensory knowledge (srotradi-buddhya): the Supreme Lord (brahma), the jivas (puman), and material nature (pradhanikam). The affix ‘thak ‘(ika) is applied to the word ‘pradhana, ‘but its meaning remains the same.

### Text 56

**Перевод:**

**Someone may argue, “This subtle collective unconscious denoted by the word ‘tamah ‘is indescribable because it is so expressed by the word ‘māyā ‘mentioned in the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad (4.Ю): mayam tu prakṛtim vidyat, ‘One should know that material nature is māyā (illusion)’. ” It is not so, for the word ‘māyā ‘is not seen anywhere in the sense of something different than existent and non-existent. No such meaning that should be expressed by this word is seen anywhere either. Nor is the word ‘māyā ‘used everywhere to express deceit, for it has multiple meanings. For instance, the lexicons read as follows: According to the Visva-kośa (Ya, 61), it is used in the sense of deceit and compassion. According to the Trikandasesa (3.319), it means magic and wisdom. According to the Nighantu (3.9), the words ‘māyā ‘and ‘vayuna ‘are synonyms of knowledge. We shall later explain that if what is expressed by the word ‘māyā ‘is false, then this view pertains to non-believers (nastikas), since the Vedic scriptures give no evidence of it. Therefore, here the Supreme Lord‘s amazing creative energy is called ‘māyā ‘and is real. Its nature as such is shown by the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad (4.9): asman map srjate visvam etat, “The Supreme Lord, the controller of maya, creates this universe by means of the material nature.”**

**Комментарий:**

Someone may argue, “By the word ‘māyā ‘it is proved that the meaning of avidya is indescribable.” The author writes the present paragraph with the intention to say that such vain hope should not be entertained. The idea that something indescribable is expressed by the word ‘māyā ‘is also seen in the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (10.8.40) when the Queen of Vṛndāvana saw the universal form and used this word to define her vision: kith svapna etad uta deva-maya, “Is this a dream or God’s maya?” The word ‘tasya ‘means ‘of the word maya’. “No such meaning, ” i.e., “no such meaning, as intended by you, that avidya is different than existent and nonexistent, which should be expressed by this word is seen anywhere either.” Someone may argue, “As the word ‘māyā ‘expresses deceit, this proves that is means indescribability». The author replies, “Nor is the word ‘maya, ‘etc. ” The word ’sarvatra ‘means ‘everywhere in the scriptures’. The word ‘tasya ‘means ‘of the word maya’. “For it has multiple meanings, ” i.e., because it expresses four kinds of meanings: deceit, compassion, wisdom, and knowledge. The quoted definitions are respectively from the Visva-kośa, Trikandasesa, and Nighantu. Thus, the idea is that the word ‘māyā ‘may be sometimes used to express deceit in cases where the object is something false but not in cases where the object is something real, such as in the text ‘mayam tu prakṛtim vidyat’. Someone may argue, “Since the word ‘māyā ‘indeed also expresses ‘deceit, ‘then this must also be the meaning expressed in the text ‘mayam tu prakṛtim vidyat. ” ‘In reply, the author says, “If what is expressed by the word ‘māyā ‘is false, etc». That means ‘of the meaning expressed by the Vedic word maya’. He then explains its meaning according to his own view, “Therefore, etc». The words ‘vicitra-sarga-kari ‘mean ‘this creative energy that makes us feel astonished’. It is for this reason that Rukmint is compared to māyā in the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (10.53.51): tam deva-mayam iva vira-mohinim, “Seeing her, who was like God’s māyā and enchanted all heroes, etc. ”In the Hari-vamsa (99.6) it is also said about Rati: “Pradyumna’s wife, named Mayavati, was like māyā and looked like a goddess». Maya is certainly real. Its reality is understood from the aja-mantra, etc. “Its nature as such, ” i.e. as such amazing creative potency. The word ‘asmat ‘means ‘by means of pradhana (material nature)’.

### Text 57

**Перевод:**

**As for the statement that avidya again disappears by knowledge about the oneness of the soul with Brahman, this is a foolish idea. What is knowledge that dispels avidya actually like ? It cannot be consciousness without attributes, for this being eternal, avidya would be eternally prevented. Thus, since no perception of the material world — which is based on avidya — would be possible, all scriptures would serve no purpose. This would also contradict common experience. Nor can it be a mental state. If such a mental state is real, there would be duality. If it is false, how can it dispel ignorance ? Only real knowledge about real things like a rope is perceived to dispel the ignorance that causes its misapprehension for a snake. Nor can it be said that knowledge causes the removal of itself and of something else, like fire after burning wood or like the powdered seeds of clearing-nut tree, for in this case, the time accomplishing causation being real, non-duality is lost.**

**Комментарий:**

In paragraph nine it had been said that avidya again disappears by knowledge about the oneness of the soul with the Supreme, and not by surrendering unto the Supreme. The author writes the present paragraph in order to refute this. He asks what knowledge is like. The word ‘kevalam ‘means ‘without attributes’. “Based on avidya,” i.e., caused by it. If knowledge is devoid of attributes, then the common experience that everyone has would not happen, such as ‘I am a living entity, ‘‘I am ignorant, ‘and ‘I am subject to fate’. The words ‘vrtti-rQpam ‘mean ‘knowledge derived from the mental state of identity with Brahman’. Next, the author explains that knowledge that dispels ignorance cannot be false. If knowledge about a rope is false, then its misapprehension as a snake would exist always. Two illustrations are given by the opponent to support the idea that vrtti-jnāna (knowledge of that mental state) is false. Just as fire itself disappears after burning wood, similarly after removing material illusion, vrtti-jnāna itself disappears. The author then refutes this view. Vrtti-jnāna is the cause of the disappearance of material illusion and is characterized as invariably occurring before such effect. Therefore, duality is proved by the occurrence of the time accomplishing causation. Someone may argue, “Vrtti-jnāna will also remove time, since it is included within the material manifestation». In this case, vrtti-jnāna’s causation would not be accomplished. If another time is accepted to exist, then there will be regressus ad infinitum. Moreover, both illustrations of yours are incompatible, because there is fire without wood in mahatejas; because fire is also seen coming from ashes; and because both the seed powder of clearing-nut tree and the mud remain at the bottom of the water. In the Bhagavad-Gita (7.14), Lord Kṛṣṇa says that māyā disappears by surrendering unto Him, the Supreme Lord: mam eva ye prapadyante mayam etam taranti te, “Those who surrender unto Me cross beyond this illusory energy. ”

### Text 58

**Перевод:**

**In this way, another idea has also been refuted, according to which just as one experiences factual pleasure when dreaming about union with a woman, and pain when dreaming about one’s head being cut off, although the scriptures, the preceptors, and the practices taught are actually false, one attains real liberation by them. The reason is that only real perceptions of such things in dreams produce pleasure or pain. Otherwise, a deer would quench its thirst with the water in a mirage, and the like. Someone may argue, “It is seen that alphabet sounds attributed to drawn lines convey meaning, and the suspect of poisoning causes death.” It is not so, for just as a word is conventionally attributed to a meaning, drawn lines are attributed to an alphabet sound, and only real alphabet sounds recollected through lines express meaning. In the case of the suspect of poisoning, the cause of death is real upheaval of the bodily elements resultant from fear occasioned by doubt.**

**Комментарий:**

“In this way, ” i.e., having refuted the idea that something false can produce real results. “Real perceptions of such things, ” i.e., the perception of things like union with a woman, and having one’s head cut off. “Otherwise,” i.e., if something false would produce something real. The meaning of the rest of the paragraph is clear.

### Text 59

**Перевод:**

**It had been argued that on account of the inconceivable power of words or by excluding anything different from it, Brahman is understood as mere consciousness in sentences like ’satyam jnānam anantam brahma ‘without any semantic cause or secondary meaning. This idea is very foolish, for it is not accepted by the commentator and other scholars.**

**Комментарий:**

The monists declare that if semantic causes, such as generic ones (jati), are accepted, then Brahman would be saguṇa. If secondary meanings are accepted, then it would be expressed by another word, which contradicts statements about its being indescribable and devoid of attributes. Therefore, Brahman, which is devoid of attributes and Whose svarūpa is truth (satya), consciousness (jnāna), and eternity (ananta), should be understood by resorting either to the inconceivable power of words, or to word meanings that exclude anything different than it, such as ‘a bull is different than anything other than a bull’. Here the author refutes this view. The commentator referred to is Śaṇkara, who accepts four kinds of semantic causes according to which words are employed: generic words (jati-śabda), quality words (guna-śabda), action words (kriya-śabda), and proper nouns (sathjna-śabda). The same is also accepted by Acarya Bharata. The followers of Mimāṁsā accept the power of words in jati, and the followers of Vaiśeṣika in viśeṣa. This is the course taken by all authorities on the topic, and this being so, what is this new invention that words have inconceivable power? The method of exclusion from anything different is also accepted by non-believers (nastikas), and thus those who resort to it should be known among non-believers.

### Text 60

**Перевод:**

**Since no evidence applies to Brahman, it should not be believed that Brahman is devoid of attributes. For instance, direct perception (pratyaksa) is no evidence of that, for Brahman is devoid of colour and so on. Nor is inference (anumana) evidence, for there is no sign invariably concomitant to Brahman. Nor is comparison (upamana) evidence, for there is nothing similar to it. Nor is verbal testimony evidence, since no semantic causes apply to Brahman. Nor is a secondary meaning (lakṣaṇā) evidence, for Brahman cannot be expressed by any word. This has been previously explained. Nor is postulation (arthapatti) evidence, as except for Brahman, there is nothing else to be ascertained. Nor is non-perception (anupalabdhi) evidence, for being really existent, Brahman is beyond its range.**

**Комментарий:**

The demonstration of an object of knowledge is subordinate to an evidence. Here, the author states that due to the lack of evidence, an attributeless Brahman cannot be proved. Pratyaksa happens when in proximity with their objects, sense organs like the eyes directly perceive things that have colour and so on. This serves as no evidence of the attributeless Brahman, for it is devoid of colour and so on. Anumana is a means of inferential knowledge, like knowledge about fire from an inference such as ’since there is smoke, there is fire on the hill’. Knowledge from the smoke and so on is inferential knowledge. Here, smoke, which is invariably concomitant to fire, is a sign of fire, but in the case of Brahman, there is no sign concomitant to it, since it is devoid of attributes. The term ‘vyapti ‘means invariable concomitance of a middle term and a major term, such as ‘where there is smoke, there is fire’. Therefore, inference is no evidence of Brahman. Upamana is a means of comparative knowledge. Knowledge about the relation between the name and the named, such as the name ‘gavaya ‘and the animal gavaya (Gayal) in the sentence ‘go-sadrso gavayah ‘(Gayal is like a bull), is called comparative knowledge. Knowledge of a particular kind of animal similar to a bull, accompanied by the remembrance of a statement about their analogy, is actually inference, since it is a means of comparative knowledge. In the case of a Gayal, knowledge about its similarity with a bull is its identifier, but in the case of Brahman, you cannot say that knowledge of some other thing similar to it is its identifier, for there is no other thing. Thus, comparison is no evidence of it. Nor is verbal testimony evidence of it. The meaning of this has already been explained. Arthapatti, postulation, results from something that otherwise would be inconclusive. For instance, ‘John is fat but does not eat during the day’. Here, since John does not eat during the day, without eating at night his fatness would not be explained, which is evidence that he eats at night. In a similar way, without Brahman, which unexplained thing would be evidence of Brahman ? Therefore, postulation is no evidence of it. If you say that the universe exists, then you agree with us. In the statement ‘There is no pot here, ‘the absence of perception of a pot is evidence of the nonexistence of a pot. Since Brahman is actually existent, non-existence is no evidence of it.

### Text 61

**Перевод:**

**In the text ‘athata adeso neti neti, ‘ limitation of form is denied, and not all form, for this is stated in the Brahma-sūtra (3-2.22): prākṛtāitavattvam hi pratisedhati tato braviti ca bhuyah, “Because the śruti denies that the Supreme Lord has a form and later speaks extensively about His form.”**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author shows that it is only by mistake that the text ‘athata adeso neti neti ‘is given as an example that Brahman is devoid of attributes. He then states the reason with a quotation from the Brahma-sūtra (3.2.22). The meaning is this: It has been established that the jivas are conscious like Lord Hari. Someone may argue, “The jivas are just a reflection of consciousness, and not conscious like Lord Hari. In the Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad (2.3.1) it is said: dve vava brahmano rūpe mUrtam caivamūrtam ca, ‘The Supreme Lord has a form and is also formless’. Then, after presenting elements like earth and colours like saffron as forms of Brahman, by the text ‘athata adeso neti neti ‘it is again denied that there is anything other than Brahman. If the jivas were similarly conscious, it would not be possible to deny the existence of anything other than Brahman. Therefore, Brahman is devoid of any attribute other than itself ” If such proposition is made, we say that the quoted śruti does not actually declare that Brahman is non-dual consciousness devoid of attributes and denies the existence of anything other than it. That śruti rather states that Brahman has particular forms and denies that it is limited to the mentioned forms. It does not deny them as forms of Brahman. Later (tatah), after denying that Brahman has limited form, the śruti speaks (braviti) extensively (praeuram) about its names like ’satya ‘(truth) and so on. The word ‘hi ‘means ‘because’. The meaning of the text ‘athata adeso neti neti ‘is as follows: Now (atha), after describing that Brahman is both endowed with and devoid of form and so on, since it is unlimited, the instruction (adesah) ‘neti neti ‘is given. As the word ‘iti ‘means ‘end, ‘the instruction is that Brahman is not limited (neti) to being formless or having the forms previously described. However, Brahman’s unlimited (neti) form is different and consists of names like ’satya, ‘and so on. The details should be seen in the Govinda-bhāṣya on the mentioned aphorism.

### Text 62

**Перевод:**

**Nor can it be said that, “On account of the word ‘atma, ‘Brahman itself is the real form of the worshipper,” because the word ‘atma ‘also means the essence of all-pervasive happiness and consciousness in the form of the Supreme Person, and also because of the śruti statements about difference.**

**Комментарий:**

In the sentence ‘atmety evopasita, ‘ the meaning is not that Brahman itself is the real form of the worshipper and is nothing other than the worshipper. The word ‘tasya ‘means ‘of the word atma ‘which comes from the verbal root ‘at ‘in the sense of that which pervades, illuminates, and is attained by the liberated souls. According to this etymological definition, the word ‘atma ‘means the essence of all-pervasive happiness and consciousness. Its sense as a form of the Supreme Person is understood from the Chāndogya Upaniṣad (8.12.3), in the passage ending with the sentence ’sa uttamah puruṣah, ‘"He is the Supreme Person». Some consider this as a fact on the basis of statements that the all-blissful Self (atma) is a form of the Supreme Lord (puruṣa).

### Text 63

**Перевод:**

**Nor is this difference useless or known by people, because in the sentence ‘prthag atmanam preritaram ‘ it is heard that results exist only when difference exists, and because such difference of counterparts distinguished by opposite attributes is perceived only through the scriptures.**

**Комментарий:**

On the basis of śruti statements on difference, such as ‘dva suparna sayuja, ‘it has been said that Brahman itself is not the real form of the worshipper. It had been previously argued that on account of being useless and already known by people, difference cannot be the intended meaning of the scriptures. Here, the author refutes the idea that the difference described by him is useless and known in the world and then demonstrates difference as the intended meaning of the scriptures. The result is immortality, as heard from the scriptures. The difference pertains to the counterparts, God and the jivas, which are distinguished by opposite attributes like being all-pervasive and atomic. Such difference is certainly not known by people. Rather, only differences pertaining to the body are known by them.

### Text 64

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, in the Brhat-samhita  it is said; upakramopasamharav abhyaso ‘purvata phalam, arthavadopapatti ca lingam tatparya-nirnaye, “The beginning (upakrama) and the end (upasathhara), repetition (abhyasa), singularity (apūrvata), result (phala), praise (arthavada), and reasoning (upapatti) — these are the indications to ascertain the purport of a text.” These six kinds of indications to ascertain the purport of the scriptures, as mentioned in this verse, are also seen as regards to difference.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author shows that by six kinds of indications, the purport of the scriptures is difference. The quoted verse is from the Brhat-samhita. The uniformity of the beginning and the end comprise one indication. The meaning is that difference is stated both in the beginning and at the end. Repetition means stating the same words again. Singularity means to be comprehended only through the Upaniṣads. The result is the goal of life. The word ‘arthavada ‘means ‘praise, ‘and ‘upapatti ‘means ‘displaying reasoning’.

### Text 65

Another male gives her up once she has been enjoyed by him.” This passage ends with the statement: justam yada pasyaty anyam isam asya mahimanam eti vita-sokah, “When one sees that the blissful Supreme Lord is different than oneself and understands His greatness, one then becomes free from sorrow.” Repetition is seen in the statements that one (anyah) out of the two birds is eating, and the other one (anyah) is not eating. The singularity is that without the Upaniṣads, such difference would not be understood from other people. Freedom from sorrow is the result. Praise is seen in the statement that one understands His greatness. Reasoning is seen in the statement that one bird is eating and the other just observes without eating. The same should be similarly seen in other statements like ‘prthag atmanam’.**

“Such difference, ” i.e., the difference caused by eternal attributes like being atomic and being all-pervasive.

### Text 67

**Перевод:**

**One actually decries the authority of the Vedas by establishing that being a superimposition, the material manifestation is false. The reason is that Vedic sentences about rituals like fire sacrifices, and sentences such as those beginning with ‘yato va imani ‘ are authoritative having the material world as object. If such sentences had no real object, they would be similar to sentences like ‘This is the son of a barren woman’. Therefore, such a view is non-Vedic (nastika).**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author starts to corroborate that the view of those who propound superimposition is non-Vedic. The words ‘tad-visayataya ‘mean ‘having the material world as object’. If the material world were false, the Vedic sentences would have no real object. Being purposeless, sentences like ‘yato va imani ‘would have no authority, just as sentences like: kūrma-loma-pata-cchannah sasa-srnga-dhanur-dharah, esa bandhya-suto bhati kha-puspa-krta-sekharah, “This is the son of a barren woman, clad with clothes made of hairs of a turtle, holding a bow made of horns of a rabbit, and wearing a chaplet of sky-flowers. ”

### Text 68

**Перевод:**

**Someone may argue, “We do not say that the material world is false like a flower in the sky, for we accept the empirical state of existence. Therefore, our view does not decry the authority of the Vedas, nor is it non-Vedic.” This is not true, for such idea is due to inattention. For instance, according to your philosophy, there are two states of existence: the real and the unreal. The latter is twofold: the empirical and the apparent. These three states exist respectively in Brahman, in the material world, and in the misapprehension of silver in a shell. Since it is accepted that the empirical state exists in the material world, there is no denial of the authority of the Vedas, which teach about this. The falsity of the material world is due to the absence of the real state. This is your view. However, this is incorrect, for the object of teaching being something that is to be removed, the denial of the authority of the Vedas is not precluded. What is to be removed is the material manifestation, as it is said: tat-tvam-asy-adi-vakyartha-samyag-dhl-janma-matratah, avidya saha karyena nasid asti bhavisyati, “By the mere appearance of the correct understanding of the meaning of the sentence ‘tat tvam asi ‘(you are that), avidya along with its effect has no existence in the past, present, or future.”**

**Комментарий:**

Here the Māyāvādi objects again with a fallacious reasoning. The word ‘tasya ‘means ‘of the material manifestation’. It should be understood that the empirical state actually exists in the material world. “Therefore, ” i.e., “by accepting the existence of the empirical state, the material world has factual existence, and the Vedas, which teach about this, are not devoid of authority like sentences such as ‘This is the son of a barren woman’. Hence, this view is not non-Vedic». It should be understood that the sentences “there are two states of existence, etc’. ‘portray the view of the Māyāvādi. “What is to be removed is the material manifestation” — the idea here is that even when it is manifest, the material world has no real existence. The author then quotes a verse from the Muktavall as evidence. The meaning is that upon realizing by the sentence ‘tat tvam asi ‘that there is only one real consciousness devoid of attributes, avidya along with its effect, the material world, has no existence at any time.

### Text 69

**Перевод:**

**Nor is monism different than foreign (non-Vedic) philosophies because of accepting twofold states of existence, for this is also accepted by those. As it is said: sattvamtu dvi-vidham proktam samvrtam pāramārthikam, samvrtam vyavaharyam syan nivrttau pāramārthikam, “The states of existence are said to be twofold: the one covered (samvrta) by illusion and the real one (pāramārthika). The covered state is empirical, and the real one is the state when that ceases to exist.” dve sattve samupasritya bauddhanam dharma-codana, loke samvrta-satyatvam satyam ca paramarthatah, “The religious precepts of the Buddhists are based on two states of existence: the state covered by illusion in this world, and the real state.” vicaryamane nasattvaij sattvam capi pratiyate, yasya tat samvrtam sattvam vyavahara-padam ca tat, “Upon deliberating on this, there is neither existence nor nonexistence. The illusory state that appears to exist is the empirical state of existence.” Therefore, the monists are not different than the Buddhists. Smrtis like the Padma Purāṇa (Uttara, 263.7) also corroborate this: māyāvādam asac chastram pracchannam bauddham ucyate, “Māyāvāda is a wrong doctrine and is said to be Buddhism is disguise.”**

**Комментарий:**

Being afraid of the stain of propounding Buddhist philosophy, the Māyāvādi has accepted two states of existence in order to remove this stain. Here, the author says that even in this way the Māyāvādi cannot get rid of this stain. “Accepted by them, ” i.e., by the Buddhists. The word ‘tasya ‘refers to the two states of existence. The meaning of the quoted verses is clear. The Māyāvādis are not different than those who are foreign to the Vedic philosophy. The verse ‘māyāvādam asat ‘is from the Padma Purāṇa (Uttara, 263.7) and was spoken by Rudra. The rest of the verse is: mayaiva kathitam devi kalau brāhmaṇa-rupina, “O goddess, this philosophy is propagated by me in the form of a brāhmaṇa in the Age of Kali. ” Similarly, in the Nrsimha Purāṇa (9.6)85 Yamaraja speaks the following words: visadhara-kanabhaksya-sahkaroktir dāsabala-pancasikhaksapada-vadan mahad api suvicarya loka-tantram bhagavad-upastim rte na siddhir asti, “Even after reflecting well on the words of Patahjali, Kaṇāda, and Śaṇkara; on the philosophy of Buddha, Pancasikha, and Gautama; and on many worldly treatises, one still cannot attain perfection without worshipping the Supreme Lord. ”

### Text 70

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, the word ‘existence ‘(sattva) does not have multiple meanings, for there is no evidence of different existences. If the word ‘existence ‘had different meanings expressing both falsity and absolute reality, then the cognition of existent objects according to their respective forms would not be possible, because different existences are not seen in words that have multiple meanings, such as the word ’saindhava, ‘and because one who says that something false exists contradicts oneself. Therefore, like the word ’samvrta ‘(covered), the word ‘vyāvahārika ‘(empirical) is used in the sense of falsity in order to deceive others, just like the word ‘vaktrasava ‘(juice from the mouth) is used in the sense of saliva. Thus, by presenting two states of existence as evidence, no principle is proved.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the idea that the word ‘existence ‘has multiple meanings. A single existence among all existent things is called universality. Since it exists eternally, its nature is that of inherence in multiple things. According to the Sridhara-kośa, the word ’saindhava ‘is used in three senses: rock-salt, horse, and that which is produced in the Sindhu territory. The idea is that universality is accepted only among things that have the same name and belong to the same class, and not among things that have the same name but belong to different classes. The meaning of the rest of the paragraph is clear.

### Text 71

**Перевод:**

**The opponent may argue, “It may be so. Just as Brahman has existence (satta), so does the material manifestation. Hence, we do not decry the authority of the Vedas. The statement about falsity is because there is no existence other than Brahman, and not that the universe is a nonentity. The śruti ‘neha nānāsti kincana ‘ also denies the existence of anything other than Brahman. There is no over-application (atiprasanga) here, for there is an invariable existence of the material cause (upadana) and the effect, just as gold and a golden crown. Otherwise, the statement ‘taj-jalan ‘ would fail to justify the identity of case-relation in the sentence ’sarvam khalv idam brahma’. ” This idea is not wise, for it does not bear scrutiny.**

**Комментарий:**

Although defeated, the shameless Māyāvādi objects again here. The word ‘nihsvarupatvat ‘means ‘being a nonentity’. “If the material manifestation exists because Brahman exists, then a cloth also exists because a pot exists — this kind of over-application does not happen here. Why ? This is because only things that share the state as material cause and effect share the same existence. Thus, there is no over-application, for so is the state between Brahman and the material manifestation». “Otherwise,\* i.e., if such state did not exist, then the statement that everything is born from Brahman and merges into it would fail to justify the identity of case-relation in the sentence (sarvam khalv idam brahma’. The author then refutes this view. The word ‘ksoda ‘means ‘grinding ‘in the sense of deliberating. The meaning is that this does not stand deliberation.

### Text 72

**Перевод:**

**For instance, what do you mean by Brahman’s sattd ? Does satta rest on Brahman? Or is it Brahman’s svarūpa? Or is it non-different from Brahman? Or is it abhava (non-existence) apart from Brahman?**

**Комментарий:**

Here the siddhanti asks the opponent what they mean by Brahman’s existence. He deliberates on the existence of Brahman through four propositions.

### Text 73

**Перевод:**

**In the first case, is satta real or is it to be removed? If it is real, this is opposite to your philosophical conclusion, for you do not accept that there is any attribute in Brahman which is not removed by knowledge of the substratum. In that case, being endowed with real existence, the material manifestation must also be real like Brahman. Nor can it be said that, “Although Brahman exists in it, the universe is unreal because it has no factual nature,” for something that really exists is nowhere perceived as unreal. If the universe exists, it is not false; if it is false, it does not exist. Satta is seen to exist even if devoid of satta-samanya. If although existent, the material world is unreal, then it would not be visible at all. Moreover, the universal equally present in similar objects and expressed by the word ’satta ‘cannot be applicable to both real and false things. As it is said: satyatvam na ca samanyaij) mrsartha-paramarthayoh, virodhan na ca vrksatvam samanyam simha-vrksayoh, “Reality cannot be equally applicable to false and real objects, for this is contradictory. The nature of a tree is certainly not equally seen both in a tree and in a lion.” Nor is sattd to be removed, for the denial of the authority of the Vedas would not be precluded in this way. A teaching that existence is to be removed is certainly no authority. Furthermore, in this proposition even Brahman would be unreal, for Brahman also has satta and this would be removed. The Upaniṣads would also lack authority, for they would have no object of knowledge. Nor can it be said that “Brahman’s being unreal is out of the question because even lacking satta, its nature is real,” for in a similar way the material manifestation would also not be unreal, since its having a real nature is mentioned in the śrutiy such as ’sad eva saumya, etc’. As for the argument that the Vedas are authoritative because they have been causing the illusion of the universe since time immemorial, this is foolishness, for in this way whatever causes the illusion that the sky is blue and the moon is small must also be authoritative, as it is similarly causing such illusions since time immemorial.**

**Комментарий:**

Having mentioned the propositions, the author now refutes them one by one. "In the first case, ” i.e., in the proposition that real existence is situated in Brahman. It cannot be said that although the existence of Brahman is also situated within the material manifestation, the latter is not real because it lacks a factual nature. The reason is that something existent is not perceived as unreal. Existence cannot be equally applied to false things as well as real things, such as the universe and Brahman. As Brahman’s existence is also situated within the material manifestation, it would also be removed, thus the second option in the first proposition is also not valid. Since Brahman is the object of knowledge to be known through the Upaniṣads, if Brahman has no existence, they have no object at all. The words ‘tad-anapatteh ‘mean that the universe would not be unreal. The words ‘dirgha-bhrama-janakatvat ‘mean ‘causing the illusion of the universe since time immemorial, ‘and ‘tathatvat ‘means ’similarly causing illusion since time immemorial’.

### Text 74

**Перевод:**

**Nor is the second proposition valid, according to which existence is Brahman’s svarūpa, for in this way, having unrestricted existence, the material manifestation would not be false. Nor is the third proposition valid, for oneness between two opposites is not possible. Nor is the fourth proposition valid, for in this way the identity of case-relation would not be possible in sentences like ’sarvam khalv idam brahma’. If the universe does not exist, identity with Brahman is not possible. Nor can it be said that, “Such identity should be taken in the sense of negation, such as when pointed out that what was thought to be a thief is actually a post.” \[99\] The reason is that the denial of the authority of the Vedas would not be precluded in this way.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author first points out the flaw in the second proposition. According to the third proposition, the universe is non-different than Brahman, thus the existence of Brahman implies the existence of the universe. This meaning is not possible. Why ? The author says, “Because opposite things — sentient Brahman and the sentient-insentient universe — cannot possibly have the same identity». The fourth proposition is also not correct, according to which the universe, which is different from Brahman, does not exist. The word ‘aikarthya ‘means ‘identity of case-relation’. The word ‘tat ‘refers to this identity.

### Text 75

**Перевод:**

**Moreover, the theory of drsti-srsti is accepted by the Māyāvādi, according to which creation happens at the same time as the vision, and not otherwise. This is nothing besides the theory of Ksanika-vijnāna, as in this theory all objects are actually momentary. Nor can it be said that, “In Śaṇkara’s philosophy there is eternal pure consciousness, and by accepting this, it is different from Buddhism.” The reason is that there is no evidence of the theory of mere consciousness devoid of attributes. This has been previously demonstrated.**

**Комментарий:**

It has been stated that the view of those who propound that the universe is false is non-Vedic (nastika). Now the author clarifies that such view of the monist is mixed with Buddhist doctrine. The theory of drsti-srsti is corroborated by the Māyāvādi, i.e., by Śaṇkara, in a philosophical treatise called Vakya-sudha. The author then describes its character. Only when there is vision, there is creation. In the absence of vision, there is no creation. “In this theory, ” i.e., in the theory of Ksanika-vijnāna taught by a disciple of Buddha called Yogacara. The word ‘atra ‘refers to the philosophy of non-dual pure consciousness imagined by Śaṇkara. The word ‘aksanikam ‘means ‘eternal, ‘and ‘tatra ‘means ‘in mere consciousness devoid of attributes’. “Previously, ” i.e., in paragraph 60 of this chapter. Therefore, one who holds the view of non-dual consciousness devoid of attributes actually holds the view of Yogacara.

### Text 76

**Перевод:**

**Furthermore, Māyāvāda is nothing besides Śūnyavāda (voidism). Consciousness is modified by ignorance, and the void is modified by illusion. By the strength of the reflection that everything is consciousness, when ignorance is destroyed, only consciousness remains. By the strength of the reflection that everything is void, when the illusory world is destroyed, only the void remains. Being the cause of the destruction of the effect, each of these is liberation itself. Existence in the form of higher universality (para-samanya) and lower universality (apara-samanya), non-existence, which is its counterpart, happiness in the form of a favourable feeling, and unhappiness in the form of an unfavourable feeling — none of these are real at all in either of these views. Both consciousness and the void are unalloyed, undecaying, immortal, unobstructed, self-manifest, and unknowable by any evidence.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author shows that one who holds the view of non-dual consciousness devoid of attributes actually holds the view of Madhyamika, a disciple of Buddha. “The universe is consciousness modified by ignorance. By the strength of the reflection that everything is consciousness, when ignorance is destroyed, only consciousness remains” — this is the philosophy of one who holds the view of non-dual consciousness devoid of attributes. “The universe is the void modified by illusion. By the strength of the reflection that everything is void, when the illusory world is destroyed, only the void remains ” — this is the philosophy of the Madhyamika. Being the cause of the destruction of the effect, mere consciousness is liberation itself Being the cause of the destruction of the effect, mere void is liberation itself This is the order of the words to be understood here. Liberation is the cause of the destruction of the effect. “Either of these ” means either mere consciousness or mere void. Universality (samanya) is eternally one, applies to many, and is the cause of a common concept. It is twofold: the higher (para) universality is existence (satta), because it subsists on substance (dravya), quality (guna), and action (karma). The lower (apara) universality is the attributes like being a substance, being a quality, and being an action, for it subsists exclusively on them. It does not have existence itself like substance, quality, and action do. The counterpart of a pot is the non-existence of a pot. The counterpart of existence actually does not exist, therefore abhava is also non-existent. These philosophical conclusions of both the Māyāvādis and the Sunyavadis are indeed similar and cannot be known by any kind of evidence. The meaning of the rest of the paragraph is clear.

### Text 77

**Перевод:**

**Someone may argue, “Since Brahman is unknowable through the senses, its self-luminosity means that Brahman is able is to be directly realised (aparoksa-vyavahararhatva), or else, its realization is not dependent on something else. Void is not self-luminous. So, how can both philosophies be similar?” This argument is insignificant. Is such direct realization done by Brahman itself having itself as the object, or is Brahman the object of someone else’s realization? The first case is not possible, for Mayavadls do not accept that Brahman can be object of its own realization. If they would accept this, then being perceptible as an object of consciousness, Brahman would be false, and being both subject and object, there would be difference. The second case is also not possible, for Brahman cannot be the object of someone else’s perception, since no one else exists in the stage of liberation. Therefore, void (sūnya) is a synonym of consciousness (jnāna), and illusion (samvrti) is a synonym of ignorance (avidya), and hence the Mayavadi is actually a Madhyamika. It has been explained that the Māyāvādis accept that the Upaniṣads teach the integral meaning (akhandartha) and generate correct knowledge about that which is not perceived by someone connected with the modes of nature, and thus, on the basis of the sentence ’satyam jnānam anantam, ‘they define Brahman’s svarūpa as different from something false, insentient, and limited, just as the Apohavadi Buddhists define a cow as that which is different from anything that is not a cow.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the Māyāvādi attempts to explain the difference between his view from that of the Madhyamikas. “You cannot say that even a twice-born is a sudra merely because of a similarity in nose and ears, ” — this is the idea here. The author then refutes this argument by calling it insignificant. Does Brahman realise itself, or does someone else, the jiva, realise it? The rest is clear. As it is thus proved that there is a thorough mixture between the Māyāvāda and the Madhyamika doctrines, the author says, “Therefore, etc.” Next, the author describes another common characteristic, “It has been explained, etc. ” “Not perceived in connection with the modes of nature, ” i.e., not an object to someone in connection with the modes. All the Upaniṣads generate correct knowledge whose object is such Brahman, devoid of attributes, unlimited, and one and the same. The Buddhists define a cow as that which is different from anything different than a cow. The word ‘apoha ‘means ‘different from that’.

### Text 78

**Перевод:**

**Furthermore, a close similarity between the Māyāvādis and the Jains is also seen. The Jains describe six kinds of elements classified as living entities and non-living entities, which have opposite attributes like existence and non-existence. For this, they resort to the sapta-bhangi-nyaya, according to which an object somehow exists, somehow does not exist, etc. The Māyāvādis also describe the universe as different than existent and non-existent, and Brahman as not expressed by words, although known by the Upaniṣads, and so on. The meaning of the text is clear. The sapta-bhahgi-nyaya should be understood from the Govinda-bhāṣya (2.2.33). Due to the apprehension that this book may become too long, that is not written here. Although there may be some differences between their doctrines, Māyāvāda is actually nastika. One should fear any contact with nastikas, as it is said: hastinapidyamano ‘pi nagacchej jaina-mandiram, “Even if attacked by an elephant, one should not enter a Jain temple. ” syad asti nasti - perhaps it is and it is not; 4) syad asty avaktavyah - perhaps it is and it is indescribable; 5) syan nasty avaktavyah - perhaps it is not and it is indescribable; 6) syad asti nasty avaktavyah - perhaps it is, it is not, and it is indescribable; and 7) syad avaktavyah - perhaps it is indescribable.

### Text 79

**Перевод:**

**It had been argued that the verses starting with ‘jyotimsi vis null ‘ state that since the empirical state of existence is superimposed on Brahman, which is mere consciousness, the world and its duality are to be removed. This idea is not correct. The whole universe made of sentient and insentient beings is expressed by the words ‘existence\* and ‘non-existence’. That is the body of the Supreme Lord, Viṣṇu, and because its sustenance and functions are dependent upon Him, it is said to consist of Him. Although He is the Supreme Soul of everything, He is distinct from everything. Although the living entities, the knowers of the field, have a form of consciousness, their avidya, which covers their svarūpa, is the cause of their attaining various conditions, such as a human or a demigod, and the transformations of insentient matter, which is enjoyed by them, nevertheless, simply by worshipping Lord Viṣṇu, they attain liberation from avidya. This is the meaning stated in those verses.**

**Комментарий:**

The author writes the present paragraph in order to refute the raised objections. Although the Supreme Lord consists of everything, either by His śakti, or by providing maintenance, or by being all-pervasive, He is untainted by anything. The word ‘karma’ should be understood here in the sense of ‘avidya’.

### Text 80

**Перевод:**

**For instance, in the verse ‘jyotimsi visnuh" it is declared that since their sustenance, activities, and so on are dependent upon Lord Viṣṇu, sentient and insentient beings are a form of His. The verse ‘jnāna-svarūpah’ states that Lord Viṣṇu is different from both of them. Because (yatah) it is heard from the scriptures that the Supreme Lord‘s form is knowledge, it is not a changeable material manifestation (asesa-murtih) but rather an immutable conscious form (vastu-bhūtah). The next line, ‘tato hi,’says that the universe was produced because of Him. The use of the neuter gender in the word ‘vijrmbhitani9 is poetic licence taken by the sages (arsa). The meaning is that the differences of various objects was created for the enjoyment of the living entities (vijndna- vijrmbhitani). As it is stated in the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (10.87.2), “buddhindriya-manah-pranan jananam asrjat prabhuh, matrartham ca bhavartham ca atmane ‘kalpanaya ca, “The Supreme Lord created the living entities ‘intelligence, senses, mind, and life air so that they may engage in worldly life, enjoy sense objects, acquire spiritual knowledge, and obtain liberation.” The verse ‘yada tu śuddhaṁ’ states that although the living entities’ svarūpa consists of consciousness, they transmigrate in this world due to material activities performed on account of beginningless aversion towards the Supreme Lord. However, when one turns towards Him, transmigration vanishes and one’s svarūpa becomes manifest. When (yada) all material activities (karma), which are the cause of the connection with varieties of bodies such as demigod and human, are destroyed (ksaye) by attaining the mercy of a bona fide spiritual master and by the worship of the Lord accompanied by knowledge about Him, and one’s spiritual nature as consciousness becomes purified (śuddhaṁ) and situated in its original form (nija-rūpi), then for one whose bodily conception was that of a demigod, human, and so on, difference between objects (vastu-bhedah) such as rocks and oceans, which are meant for the enjoyment of those material bodies and are the result (phalani) of their activities and desires (sankalpa), no more exist (no bhavanti) as such. This is due to the destruction of material activities, which is the cause of those objects. Thus, because the insentient portion changes every moment and is about to be destroyed, it is expressed by the words ‘nasti ‘(which does not exist), as explained above. The verse ‘vastv asti kim ’says that the reason for this is the decay of their state at every moment. The sentient portion (vastu), however, is devoid of beginning, middle, and end (adi-madhya-paryanta-hinam), has always one form (satataika-rūpam), and the concept of ‘nasti ‘is never applied to it. On the other hand, the insentient portion is never seen as such. Then what next? The next line states that something (yat) that is otherwise, i.e., repeatedly changing (anyathatvam), never again (na bhūyah) becomes as it previously was. When an object is devoid of its previous state and attains another state (tatra), since it is not ascertained as the very same object, how can it be factually expressed by the words ‘tat tvam asi? ‘The meaning is that it cannot be. The verse ‘mahl ghatatvam ‘then says that this is understood as such. By its lumps, clay (mahi) becomes an earthen pot (ghatatvam). From a broken pot comes a potsherd (kapalika), which when crushed becomes small pieces and then dust (cūrna-rajah), which becomes very minute atoms. In this way, an actually worthless object that is inanimate and continuously changing is observed (alaksyate) by people (janaih) as meant for one’s enjoyment. Tell (brūhi) me, do people ever observe any object (vastu) other than God and the jiva that is expressed by the word ‘asti ‘and has always only one form while a series of material transformations (atra) happens? The meaning is that no such object is observed. The reason for this is that their spiritual knowledge has become stagnated due to their material activities (sva-karma-stimitatma-niscayaih).**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author explains the meaning according to his view. The word ‘tadrūpyam ‘means ‘a form of Lord Viṣṇu’. "From both of them, ” i.e., from sentient and insentient beings. The different material objects were created (vijrmbhitani) for the enjoyment of the conscious living entities (vijtiana). The author then quotes the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (10.87.2) as evidence of this. Beginningless desire to enjoy sense objects is caused by the aversion towards the Supreme Lord, by which one becomes entangled in material nature and performs material activities, by which they roam up and down in this world. The words ’sat-tirtha ‘mean ‘a bona fide guru’. “Situated in its original form, ” i.e., being an atomic consciousness, having consciousness as its quality, the original form of the jiva then becomes manifest. “No more exist as such, ” i.e., no more appear as meant for their enjoyment. The insentient portion is the material portion like rocks, oceans, and mountains. The author then explains the reason for which they are expressed by the word ‘nasti, ‘“Because the insentient portion changes, etc. ” The insentient portion never has one and the same form always like the sentient portion. “This is understood as such, ” i.e., material object are understood to have many states. The word ‘tasyah ‘refers to the potsherd.

### Text 81

**Перевод:**

**The verse ‘tasman na vijnānam’states that because it is so, therefore the insentient portion is not expressed by the word ‘asti’.
