Thus, as the same evidence is found in the beginning and at the end, it is proved that the sense of ‘I ‘pertains to the soul. "In the truth, ” i.e., in the truth that the svarūpa of the soul is the ‘I’. The words ‘prthak-karanena ‘mean ‘by the cause in the form of the material false ego, which is different from the I of one’s svarūpa’. The word ‘nispadyam ‘means ‘brought about’.

### Text 16

**Перевод:**

**Others interpret these verses in the following way: “Since the multitude of jivas situated within all bodies have the same form constituted of consciousness and happiness, there is no distinguishing class, qualities, and so on, and hence the question ‘Who are you? ‘and the response ‘I am such-and-such ‘are not possible. Therefore, Bharata says, ‘yady anyo’sti parah ко ‘pi mattah’. The word ‘parah ‘means ‘having a different class and so on’. Having accepted the multiplicity of individuals with the same form within all bodies, dissimilarity is denied, for otherwise, the use of both the word ‘atiyah ‘and ‘parah ‘would be inconsistent in this description of the nature of the soul.”**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author states another explanation. “Others” means the Sri-vaiṣṇavas. The words’sarva-bhutesu ‘mean ‘within all bodies’. The words ‘atma-jatasya ‘refer to the multiple living entities. “Otherwise, ” i.e., “if this were not explained in this way." The word ‘ekatra ‘means ‘regarding the nature of the soul’. In this way, there would be tautology.

### Text 17

**Перевод:**

**Someone argues further, “Something that exists is also perceived. Since it is observed that both existence and consciousness are connected to all substances, both are proved to be present everywhere. Only existence is real, because it is continuous, while sound, touch, etc., are unreal, because they disappear. This should be understood like the illustration of the rope and the snake. The rope is real, since it continues to exist as the substratum, but the snake is unreal, since it disappears. According to the Viṣṇu Purāṇa (1.4-40), existence is actually consciousness: jnāna-svarūpam akhilam jagad etad abuddhayah, ‘The ignorant see this whole universe as matter, although it consists of consciousness’. The reason is that if difference is accepted, there would be regressus ad infinitum. Moreover, consciousness is selfaccomplished, for it is experienced. If it were accomplished otherwise, it would be material like sound and so on. Nor is it possible to say that consciousness is produced and destroyed, for there is no witness of either of these stages. Nor is consciousness itself the witness of both, for consciousness would not be able to perceive either, since they belong to itself.**

**Комментарий:**

Refuting another deceptive concocted argument presented by the monists, the author here introduces it. From the understanding that things such as sound, touch, etc., exist, and are perceived, both existence and experience are proved to be present in sound, touch, etc. Someone may object, “From the conclusion that sound, touch, etc., also exist, difference ensues». The monist says, “Only existence is real in everything». Existence is real, for when there is touch, even while touch is being perceived, its existence is continuous. Yet sense objects like touch and sound are themselves false because while being perceived, they disappear. The monist clarifies the argument with the illustration of the rope and the snake. The rope is real, for its existence is continuous even when it is perceived as a garland or snake. The snake is false because it disappears during perception. Someone may argue, “If both existence and consciousness are steady, difference is proved.” The monist replies, “Existence is actually non-different from consciousness.” The difference between existence and consciousness would be different from both, and thus there would be infinite regress and contradiction to the scriptural sentences about oneness. Someone may argue, “Consciousness may be non-different from existence. Yet it is produced, since it is perceived, as understood when one says ‘I have heard the sound’. ” The monist says, “Their non-difference is self-proved». According to the Nyāya philosophy, the cause of knowledge of sense objects such as sound is the soul’s inherent connection with knowledge. Having this in view, it is said, “Otherwise, ” i.e., “if knowledge were produced. ” The word ‘tasyah ‘refers to consciousness, and ‘tayoh ‘means ‘of both production and destruction’. It cannot be said that consciousness itself directly observes its own production and destruction. This is the meaning.

### Text 18

**Перевод:**

**“Nor does consciousness require another consciousness to be experienced, for it is luminous by dint of its own existence. The perception of sense objects is certainly not non-luminous like the sense objects themselves. Nor is such knowledge inferential due to an indicative sign — the preponderance of the manifestation of the perceived sense objects, for no manifestation other than perception itself is known to exist in the sense objects. Since it is accepted by both of us that it is only by perception that all affairs are carried out, it is improper to consider perception as being imagined through inference. Nor is perception manifold, for it merely appears in a single form as perception. The reasoning applied here is as follows: The perception under dispute is devoid of natural difference. It is like ether, for without contact with a limiting adjunct, no difference is observed. The perception of sound is non-different from the perception of touch, for it is mere perception, just as the perception of fragrance.**

**Комментарий:**

It has been thus demonstrated that the real nature of perception is devoid of production and destruction because it is self-accomplished. Now, what is the evidence of perception ? Expecting this question, here the monist says that perception does not require any evidence, because it is self-luminous. No other perception needs to be searched for so that perception may be experienced. Why ? It is so by dint of its own existence. The idea is that it is certainly not required to obtain a lamp to see the sun. The monist clarifies this self-luminosity by explaining that perception is not non-luminous like the sense objects such as sound, etc. Someone may argue, “Its experience must be due to inferential knowledge. ” In response, the monist says that it cannot be said that just as fire is inferred on a hill due to the cognizance of smoke, perception is inferred to exist in sense objects due to the indicative sign — the preponderance of their manifestation. Why? Because perception itself is the manifestation, and nothing other than perception is known to reveal the sense objects. Both the monist and the Vaiṣṇava agree that perception is that which reveals the sense objects, and it is only by perception that all affairs that involve cognizance and the cognizable are carried out. Hence, it is improper to assume that because of the preponderance of the indicative sign — the manifestation of the sense objects — perception must be imagined through inference. If someone argues that according to the different kinds of sense objects, there must be different kinds of perceptions in contact with different sense objects, the monist refutes this idea by saying that perception is not manifold, for either in the perception of sound or in the perception of touch, perception is ultimately manifest in the same way. Perception in this controversy is devoid of natural difference, for without a connection with the sense objects, no difference is manifest.

### Text 19

**Перевод:**

**“Therefore, such consciousness, Brahman, is eternal, self-luminous, devoid of attributes, and one without a second. This is the very same svarūpa of both the jiva and God. Anything other than this is just a display made by māyā in this world.” This is what the monists say. It should be understood that this theory that the soul’s nature is mere consciousness devoid of attributes is defeated by the explanation that the soul is actually self-luminous because of its nature as being the experiencer, being known by the concept of ‘I, ‘and revealing other objects.**

**Комментарий:**

The monist concludes his argument here. "As consciousness is continuous, it is real, and since it is free from birth and annihilation, it is eternal. It is self-luminous, for it is realised without requiring any proof (pramāna). Such consciousness, Brahman, is devoid of attributes, as it is ultimately untouched by any distinguishing characteristic, and it has no second, since it is manifest as one. On account of limiting adjuncts (upadhi) in the form of ignorance and knowledge, the jiva and God are respectively imagined to exist on the consciousness. Yet the svarūpa of both is mere consciousness itself, just like the space within a pot or a house is of the same nature as the unlimited space outside. Anything other than this is created by māyā in the form of the ignorance of the svarūpa of consciousness. ” The word ‘vijrmbhitam ‘means ‘made’. It should be understood that the argument started in paragraph seventeen, according to which the soul is mere consciousness devoid of attributes, is defeated by the explanation that the soul is the knower and so forth. This is the connection between the words. It has been explained here that the soul has a form of consciousness and is conscious, just as in sentences like ‘there is existence, ‘the substratum of existence is existence itself. The concept of ‘I ‘pertains to the soul itself, the knower, which is self-revealing because of having a nature that reveals oneself as well as objects of perception. For these reasons, the fallacious objection that was raised has been refuted. It is certainly not possible to say that the knower, the ‘I, ‘whose existence reveals other objects, is mere consciousness devoid of attributes.

### Text 20

**Перевод:**

**Furthermore, it is not possible to say that, “Subsequent to the perception of sound at the moment of the perception of touch, the sound that disappeared is not real,” for sound is being perceived by others at that same moment. Because cognizance does not diffuse, there is no multiple perception simultaneously. People with diffused cognizance, such as the astavadhanis, are indeed seen. The sense objects do not disappear when perceived by them. In the gradual realization of the individual soul and the Supreme Soul, the latter is not unreal because if disappears after being realised, for this conception would contradict śruti sentences like ‘nityo nityanam’. This same evidence also refutes the statement that both the individual soul and the Supreme Soul are imagined to exist on pure consciousness, which is therefore considered as being devoid of any attributes. Moreover, it is not defined who would be the one imagining. Examples such as the rope and the snake are no analogy at all, since they are incongruous.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the apparently appealing interpretations presented in the objection raised above. The same sound that was heard by a person is being heard by others at the time that person is perceiving touch. Someone may object, “ Why does the person who is perceiving touch not hear that sound?” This is because cognizance is limited, unfortunately. The author then states that those who have a wide range of cognizance by dint of many meritorious acts previously performed actually experience multiple sense objects together. The word ‘anubhutasya ‘refers to the Supreme Soul. The Kaṭha Upaniṣad (2.2.13) text ‘nityo nityanam cetanascetananam ‘has already been previously explained. The monist has stated that the jiva and the Supreme Soul are imagined to exist on pure consciousness, and since both are just imagination, they are false, and hence only pure consciousness is real. This statement is refuted by the same evidence from the quoted śruti, according to which both the jiva and the Supreme Soul are eternal, and therefore their being an imagination is not possible. Just as a person imagining that a rope inside the house is a snake is understood to be different from the rope, the one imagining the sense objects in the consciousness must be understood as different from consciousness. However, you do not accept the existence of anything other than consciousness. Therefore, you are presenting an incongruous illustration. This is the meaning here.

### Text 21

**Перевод:**

**Nor is mere existence consciousness itself, for it is proved that there is difference as subject and object. Nor is perception of sense objects what you believe to be the soul, for you accept vrtti-jnāna as noneternal. Nor can such consciousness, the svarūpa of Brahman, be the soul, for recognition essentially belongs only to the ‘I, ‘the person experiencing it, such as when one thinks ‘I am the same person who in childhood perceived my parents and now in old age perceives my grandchildren’. Nor is consciousness inert matter because it is an object of perception like the sense objects, for it is perceivable on account of its very nature as consciousness. Therefore, consciousness should not be considered inert matter, although it is apprehended by knowledge, as corroborated by the text ‘mrgair mrganam grahanam’. It is also stated in the Viṣṇu-sahasra-nama-stotra (66): jnāna-gamyah puratanah, “The Supreme Lord is the oldest person and can be apprehended by knowledge.” The sun, whose form is exclusively light, is certainly not seen as devoid of light, although it is an object to the eyes, which are luminous in nature. In this way, it is proved that someone else’s consciousness can be perceived by knowledge inferred from indicative signs, such like someone’s giving up attachment and taking to devotional service. The perception of one’s own consciousness is proved by the perception that ‘I am the same person, etc’. The statements of the śruti and the smṛti that teach about the knowability of the Supreme, Whose svarūpa is knowledge, should not be rejected, such as: tam tv aupanisadam puruṣam prcchami (Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad, 3-9-26), “I am asking you about the Supreme Person taught in the Upaniṣads.yy jnānam jneyam jnāna-gamyam (Bhagavad-gita, 13-18), “The Supreme Soul is knowledge, is the object of knowledge, and can be perceived by knowledge.” Therefore, the one who experiences is the very soul, while consciousness is its attribute. This attribute is understood as self-luminous at the time of revealing objects and is perceived by knowledge at other times. As for the statements in the Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad (3.4.2), after denying that the topic is the individual soul, it is said that one should worship God, the Supreme Soul within everyone. By this explanation, no doubt is left at all.**

**Комментарий:**

**Thus ends the seventh chapter of the introduction to the Govinda-bhāṣya, named Siddhānta-ratnaṁ, whose topic is the rebuttal of monism.**

It had been previously claimed that there is no difference between existence and consciousness, for existence is consciousness itself Now, the author refutes this idea. Nor is regressus ad infinitum bound to happen if we accept difference between both, for consciousness being the locus of existence, infinite regress is out of the question. Nevertheless, their appearance as different is actually due to viśeṣa. The word ‘you ‘refers to the monists. “You cannot say that consciousness devoid of attributes is Brahman s svarūpa, from which all perceptual knowledge (jnāna-vrtti) like blue and yellow become manifest.” Why? The author says, “For recognition, etc. ” The word ‘tattvena ‘means ‘essentially’. By the statement that consciousness does not require another consciousness to be perceived, it had been denied that the soul can be perceived by knowledge. The author refutes this by saying “Nor is consciousness inert matter». This is stated in case someone argues that consciousness must be material like the sense objects. In order to clarify the instruction that upon accepting similarity, dissimilarity does not occur, the author says, “The sun, etc. ” “In this way, ” i.e., “accepting that consciousness is perceived by knowledge». “Someone else’s consciousness can be perceived by knowledge inferred from indicative signs accepted and given up’. ‘For instance, in the sentence ‘This brāhmaṇa knows the Supreme, for giving up sense enjoyment, he takes up devotion to the Supreme, ‘it is inferred that the brāhmaṇa knows the Supreme. The meaning of the rest of the sentence is clear. This same argument also refutes the following idea: phala-vyapyatvam evasya śāstra-krdbhir nirakrtam, brahmany ajnāna-nasaya vrtti-vyaptir apeksyate,  “Compilers of philosophical treatises have denied only phala-vyapti in the perception of consciousness. Vrtti-vyapti is required to destroy the ignorance about Brahman. ” According to the Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad (4.4.19), Brahman is vrtti-vyapti: manasaivanudrastavyam, “The Supreme is to be observed by the mind. ” Brahman is not phala-vyapti, as stated in the śruti: yan mano na manute, “That which the mind does not perceive». This is what the monists say. When a mental modification pervades the consciousness modified by ignorance in order to make its covering disappear, that is called vrtti-vyapti. When the covering disappears, the self-luminous consciousness is called ‘phala-caitanya ‘and the citta-vrtti does not pervade it. As the covering has already disappeared, since vrtti-vyapti serves no purpose, and since even the mind has already been destroyed, how can one speak of vrtti-vyapti? “Therefore, ” i.e., on account of the stated conclusion, the soul is the experiencer and knower, and its svarūpa is not mere consciousness devoid of attributes. Consciousness is an attribute inherently connected with the soul. Someone may argue, “If the soul is a knower, then how to explain the scriptural sentences that deny its being a knower, such as ‘na drster drastaram? ‘” The author says, “As for the statements, etc.” The meaning is clear. Because there is much trouble for those who worship the impersonal Brahman, it is declared by the Supreme Lord in the Bhagavad-gita (12.3-5): ye tv aksaram anirdesyam, “Those who worship the imperishable, indescribable, etc. ”

Thus, the seventh chapter, entitled ‘Nandaka, ‘has been explained in this commentary on the introduction to the Govinda-bhāṣya.

## Eighth Chapter — Padma

### Text 1

**Перевод:**

**In this way, the atma is the knower and the ‘I’. Its svarūpa is consciousness and it is endowed with attributes like being a doer. There are two kinds of atma: God and the individual soul. God is all-pervading and the creator of the universe through Himself. Through the agency of prakṛti, whose functions are dependent upon Him, God is also the material cause of the universe. This is concluded from five aphorisms and the Govinda-bhāṣya on them starting with ‘prakṛtis ca pratijna dṛṣṭāntanurodha ‘(1.4.23). The difference between the material manifestation, which consists of thejivas and prakṛti, from God, Who is their shelter, is proved in the Brahma-sūtra sections starting with the Ananda-māyā Adhikaraṇa.**

**Комментарий:**

In the beginning of this book, it was stated that the Lord of all and the husband of Goddess Laksmi is Viṣṇu, Who is knowledge and bliss personified, an ocean of transcendental qualities like omniscience, and that devotion to Him is the cause of the ultimate removal of distress and attainment of happiness. It had also been established by the view of the venerable Vyāsadeva that devotion is performed with knowledge about the Supreme Lord, accompanied by self-knowledge. In order to corroborate this, some theories have been refuted, such as the view that all divine beings are one. In chapters five, six, and seven, the theory that there is only one soul devoid of attributes was condemned. Thus, the view that the Supreme is endowed with attributes has been firmly established. Now, in order to describe the mentioned goal of life according to this view, the eighth chapter, entitled ‘Padma, ‘is starting. The words ‘through Himself mean ‘through His self, which is endowed with His svarūpa-śakti, His superior energy’. The Supreme Lord Himself is both the efficient and the material cause of the universe. Why? Because this is in conformity with the declaration that by knowing this single truth, one knows everything, as well as the illustration that by a lump of clay, one knows everything made of clay. The other aphorisms should be understood in the Govinda-bhāṣya.

### Text 2

**Перевод:**

**The Lord‘s svarūpa is characterized as truth, eternity, and knowledge, and it is the cause of the creation, maintenance, and destruction of the universe. Being endowed with sentient and insentient energies, He is the efficient and the material cause of the universe. Therefore, being Himself both causes, since no limiting adjunct (upadhi) can ever apply to the Supreme, it is also not very reasonable to assume an invariable tatastha-lakṣaṇā, also named upalakṣaṇā (metaphor). It was taught by Vyāsadeva, the compiler of the scriptures, that the Supreme is characterized as the sole cause of the creation, maintenance, and destruction of the universe: janmady asya yatah (Brahma-sūtra, 1.1.2), “The Supreme Lord is the source of the creation, maintenance, and destruction of this material universe.” Otherwise, the description would be incoherent. It is thus demonstrated that the universe is also real, since it emanates from the ultimate reality.**

**Комментарий:**

The Māyāvādis say, “An essential definition (svarūpa-lakṣaṇā) indicates something that is non-different from itself, for example, the truth of Brahman. An indicative or accidental definition (tatastha-lakṣaṇā) indicates something different from itself, for example, Brahman’s being the creator, maintainer, and destroyer of the universe. Although the universe is different from Brahman, it indicates Brahman as its creator. Therefore, since this is a metaphorical reference to Brahman, this is an instance of tatastha-lakṣaṇā.” This idea is not very intelligent. Just as an ox’s peculiarity, such as the dew-lap, is non-different from its essential definition, and hence its essential definition is possessing dew-lap and so on, the three energies — superior, inferior, and marginal — of Brahman, the possessor of those energies, are its peculiarity and are non-different than its svarūpa, and not conditional (aupadhika) like the redness of a China rose flower. Therefore, through the agency of those three energies, Brahman’s being the efficient and the material cause of the universe is its essential definition, just as possessing dew-lap and so on is the essential definition of an ox. This is the idea. The meaning of the quoted aphorism is this: the cause by which (yatah) the creation, maintenance, and destruction (janmadi) of this universe (asya) are made possible is Brahman.

### Text 3

**Перевод:**

**As for those who say that the universe is false and superimposed on Brahman, just like a snake on a rope, in their view the universe would have no substratum. The principle regarding the substratum is that while being known, it certainly has unknown attributes in common with the illusory object. Such principle is not possible in Brahman, which is devoid of attributes and aspects in common with anything else. Illustrations like clay and earthen pot are given by the Vedas, while illustrations like shell and silver are just imagined. Therefore, Brahman’s form as the efficient and material cause of the universe is real.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the theory that the universe is an illusion. In the circumstance of an illusion, the substratum of the illusion is known and also has unknown attributes in common with the illusory object, such as a shell with silver or a rope with a snake. Yet you do not accept Brahman in such a way. Therefore, this theory of illusion is incoherent. Moreover, the illustrations through which this theory is propounded are not found in the Vedas. Someone may argue, “These illustrations are found in some places in the Purāṇas». The author replies, “They are imagined illustrations». This means that they are meant to produce detachment and convey the concept of the transience of the universe. The idea here is that such illustrations are made by the imagination of preceptors with the desire to instigate others to become detached.

### Text 4

**Перевод:**

**The Mahā-bhārata states the same thing: esa prakṛtir avyaktah karta caiva sanatanah, paras ca sarva-bhutebhyas tasmad vrddhatamo ‘cyutah, buddhir mano mahan vayus tejo ‘mbhah kham mahl ca ya, catur-vidham ca yad bhutam sarvam krsne pratisthitam (2.35.23-24)\> “The unmanifest and immutable Supreme Lord Acyuta is the efficient and the material cause of everything and hence the oldest of all beings. The mahat-tattva, the mind, the false ego, the senses, the four kinds of living beings, the great five elements — ether, air, fire, water, and earth — all these are maintained by Lord Kṛṣṇa.” catur-vidhanam blmtanam trisu lokesu madhavah, prabhavas caiva sarvesam nidhanam ca yudhisthira (2.37.14), “Lord Madhava is the creator of all four kinds of living beings, and their destroyer as well, О Yuddhisthira.” As for śruti sentences like ‘niskalam niskriyam, ‘ they just say that nevertheless, the Supreme undergoes no transformation whatsoever. Therefore, everything is consistent.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author gives evidence that the Supreme Lord is both the efficient and the material cause. The quoted verses are from the Sabha-parva of the Mahā-bhārata and were spoken by Bhisma. Lord Kṛṣṇa is both the efficient cause (karta) as well as the material cause (prakṛtih). The word ’sanatanah ‘means ‘immutable’. The word ‘buddhih ‘means ‘mahat-tattva, ‘and ‘manah, ‘the mind, refers to its cause, the false ego. By this, the senses and their controlling demigods are also included. The great elements are fivefold — ether, air, fire, water, and earth. The four kinds of living beings are the species born from the womb, from eggs, from seeds, and from moisture. All these are maintained (pratisthitam) by Lord Kṛṣṇa. He is the cause of their creation (prabhavah) and destruction (nidhanam). Therefore, Lord Kṛṣṇa is said to be the only cause of everything. Someone may argue, “If it is thus proved that He is the creator of the universe, then why does the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad state that the Supreme is devoid of action?” The author’s response is that the meaning of this sentence is that the Supreme Lord is free from any kind of transformation, such as effort.

### Text 5

**Перевод:**

**The individual soul is atomic, as stated in the śruti and the smṛti: balagra-ata-bhagasya satadha kalpitasya ca, bhago jivah sa vijneyah sa canantyaya kalpate (Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad, 5-9), “The individual soul is meant for liberation and should be understood as having a dimension conceived as the hundreth part of the tip of a hair again divided by one hundred.” eso ‘nur atma cetasa veditavyah (Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad, 3.1.9), “This atomic soul should be understood by the intellect.” suksmanam apy aham jivah (Śrimad-Bhāgavatam, 11.16.11), “Amongst the subtle, I am the individual soul.” Its qualities like being a doer and enjoyer are real and dependent on the Supreme Lord. This is understood from ten aphorisms starting with ‘karta śāstrarthavattvat ‘(Brahma-sūtra, 2.3.31), “The individual soul is a doer, for then the instructions in the scriptures are meaningful.”**

**Комментарий:**

Having thus described God, the author now describes the individual soul. The text ‘balagra-sata-bhagasya ‘is from the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad (5-9). The word ‘anantyaya ‘means for liberation’. The text ‘eso ‘nur atma ‘is from the Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad (3.1.9). The verse ‘suksmanam api ‘is from the Srimad-Bhāgavatam (11.16.11). The word ‘parayattam ‘means ‘dependent on God’. The jiva is a doer. Why ? Because only if there is a conscious doer the scriptural instructions that one should perform sacrifices, meditate, etc., would be meaningful. If the doer were material nature, then such instructions would be useless. This is the meaning.

### Text 6

**Перевод:**

**It should be admitted that the multiple living entities also emanate from the Supreme Absolute Truth, for the plurality of those whose ignorance has been destroyed and attain similarity with the Supreme Lord is stated in the Bhagavad-gita: jnanena tu tad ajnānam yesam nasitam atmanah, tesam adityavaj jnānam prakāśayati tat param (5-16), “The transcendental knowledge of those whose ignorance has been destroyed by knowledge of God reveals His form just like the sun reveals everything.” idam jnānam apasritya mama sadharmyam agatah, sarge ‘pi nopajayante pralaye na vyathanti ca (14.2): “Taking shelter in this knowledge, the living entities attain a nature similar to Mine and thus they are neither born at the time of creation nor afflicted at the time of dissolution.” It must also be accepted that although the individual soul’s svarūpa is consciousness itself, it has the quality of possessing consciousness, as said in the scriptures: yo vijnane tisthan vijnānad antarah (Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad, 3.7.22), “The Supreme Soul dwells in the individual consciousness but is distinct from it.” manta boddha karta vijnānatma puruṣah (Praśna Upaniṣad, 4.9), “The individual soul is consciousness itself, the thinker, the knower, and the doer.” This is an eternal quality, as it is said: na hi vijnatur vijnater viparilopo vidyate (Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad, 4.3.30), “The consciousness of the conscious being is never destroyed.” yatha na kriyate jyotsna mala-praksalanan maneh, dosa-prahanan na jnānam atmanah kriyate tatha, yathodapana-khananat kriyate na jalantaram, sad eva niyate vyaktim asatah sambhavah kutah, tatha heya-guna-dhvamsad avabodhadayo gunah, prakasyante na janyante nitya evatmano hi te (Viṣṇu-dharma Purāṇa, 1.ЮО.55-57)\> “Just as a jewel’s light is not created by cleansing it from dirt, a living entity’s consciousness is not created by removing material contamination. Just as water is not produced by digging a well, only an existent being becomes manifest. How can a non-existent being be brought into existence? Similarly, after negative qualities have been destroyed, qualities like consciousness are merely revealed, and not produced, for they are eternal attributes of the soul.” This is also proved by the rebuttal that pure consciousness is devoid of attributes. Thus, although the jivas are similar to God, they are said to be His parts and parcels, since they are subordinate to Him on account of the minuteness of their svarūpa and capacities. This is explained in the Govinda-bhāṣya on the Amsa Adhikaraṇa (2.3.41-51). In this way, it is proved that the svarūpa of the individual soul is minute consciousness that has eternal qualities like knowledge.**

**Комментарий:**

In this paragraph the author states that the individual souls are not one like God, but many. The jivas are multiple. The two quoted verses starting with ‘jnanena tu ‘are from the Bhagavad-gita. The consciousness, the living entity, has the quality of being conscious, just as the sun, which is light itself, has the quality of illuminating. This is said by the author, “It must also be accepted, etc." The text ‘yo vijnane ‘is from the Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad (3-7.22). The Supreme inner Soul (yah) dwells (tisthan) in the jiva, which has consciousness for its single element (vijnane), and rules it from within. The text ‘manta boddha ‘is from the Praśna Upaniṣad (4.9). The word ‘boddha ‘means ‘being the knower’. Someone may argue, “Consciousness is not eternal, for it is produced when the soul is united with the mind. ” The author replies, “This is an eternal quality. ” The text ‘na hi vijnatuh ‘is from the Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad (4.3.30). The dharma-bhuta-jnāna (vijnateh) of the jiva (vijnatuh) is never destroyed (viparilopah),for it is indestructible. This is the rest of the sentence. By the word ‘adi ‘(etc.), other sentences are also implied, such as ‘avinasi va are ayam atmanucchitti-dharma, ‘(Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad, 4.5.14), “The individual soul is imperishable and has indestructible attributes.” The verses starting with ‘yatha na kriyate ‘were spoken by Saunaka. The negative qualities (heya-gunah) are evils like decrepitude. Knowledge (avabodhah) is a quality that once raised destroys the darkness of worldly life, which cannot be dispelled even by a thousand suns, without which one cannot attain liberation, just like an animal, even though one may have renounced everything. When such knowledge arises, even householders like King Janaka attain liberation. A similar statement is found in the Moksa-dharma section of the Mahā-bhārata (12.308.50), spoken by Janaka to the mendicant Sulabha: akincanye na mokso’sti kincane nasti bandhanam, kincane cetare caiva jantur jnanena mucyate, “One does not attain liberation merely by being a destitute, nor is one bound by having properties. Either one has properties or not, it is by knowledge that one attains liberation. ”

### Text 7

**Перевод:**

**Some speak as follows: “By the connection with an atomic upadhi (limiting adjunct) in the form of the mind, Brahman itself is named jiva. Therefore, the jiva is a portion of Brahman.” This statement is ill thought out. For instance, the jiva cannot actually be a fragment of Brahman cut off by a real limiting adjunct, like a piece of stone cut by a chisel, since it is admitted that Brahman is the complete whole and is indivisible. That would also imply that the jiva has a beginning, for cutting means to split an existent unity into two. Nor does Brahman remain undivided and a particular part of it becomes endowed with an upadhi, because while the upadhi would be moving, that part of Brahman endowed with upadhi would not be carried together, hence at every moment a different part of Brahman would be endowed with upadhi, and hence at every moment different parts would be either in bondage or liberation.**

**Комментарий:**

It was stated above that the jivas are parts and parcels of the Supreme, since they are subordinate to Him. In this regard, the Advaitavadis speak as follows: “Since the word ‘amsa ‘expresses a part of something, a part of Brahman endowed with an upadhi is named jiva, which is a portion of Brahman. ” The author starts this paragraph to refute this idea. He refutes the view that the jiva is a part cut off from Brahman, “The jiva cannot, etc.” According to the Amara-kośa (2.10.34), ‘tahkah ‘is an instrument for cutting stone. Someone may argue, “The split is not said to be done as if by a chisel-like upadhi, but rather part of Brahman becomes the jiva by the mere connection with the upadhi, and hence transformation is out of the question. ” The author responds, “Nor does Brahman, etc. ” The meaning is that since the upadhi in the form of the mind would be thus divided and filled with the mode of passion, there would be movement. Consequently, the part of Brahman endowed with the upadhi would be in bondage, and the part free from it would be liberated.

### Text 8

**Перевод:**

**Nor is the individual soul the very svarūpa of Brahman endowed with upadhi, for this idea would render invalid the descriptions that Brahman is unconditioned, and it would also contradict śruti sentences like ‘ya ātmani tisthan’.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the proposition that the jiva is the conditioned Brahman. This view would contradict scriptural sentences that describe the Supreme as being turiya (transcendental), such as ’sivam advaitam caturtham manyante, ‘ and also sentences that describe the Supreme as the Inner Soul within the jiva, such as ‘ya ātmani tisthan’.

### Text 9

**Перевод:**

**Nor is the jiva the upadhi itself, whose substratum is Brahman, for in this case the jiva would be destroyed at the time of liberation.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the proposition that the mind is the jiva itself. The proposition is that the mind itself, which is standing on Brahman, is the jiva. This idea would also contradict śruti sentences like ‘nityo nityanam cetanas cetananam’.

### Text 10

**Перевод:**

**Nor is the individual soul Brahman itself when bewildered, for this would contradict śruti sentences that teach about its omniscience.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the proposition that the bewildered Brahman is the jiva. It cannot be said that just like a bewildered son of a king becomes a fisherman, the bewildered Brahman becomes the jiva.

### Text 11

**Перевод:**

**Nor is the individual soul Brahman itself reflected on the upadhi, for Brahman is all-pervasive and has no form. Nor is the individual soul a reflection of Brahman, just like formless form is reflected, because since form has a substratum, this analogy is incongruous. Nor is the individual soul a reflection of Brahman just like the redness of a China rose flower is reflected on a crystal, for in this case, a substance with colour is the cause of a divided reflection, and hence your intended conclusion is unproved. Nor is the individual soul a reflection of Brahman just like the sky is reflected on water, for Brahman being all-pervasive and formless, this idea is refuted. That reflection is just of a portion of the light of the heavenly bodies within the sky, which is divided by a limiting adjunct. The belief that it is a reflection of the sky is unwise. Otherwise, air, time, and so on would also have reflections. As for those who, while explaining Brahman’s reflection, describe it like a sound and its echo, they have started in one extreme and then reach the other extreme, for there is no similarity between reflection and echo.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the proposition that the jiva is Brahman reflected on the upadhi. The word ‘tasya ‘refers to Brahman. Just as a reflection of the sky, which is all-pervasive and formless, is not possible, the reflection of Brahman, which is similarly all-pervasive and formless, is also not possible. Someone may argue, “Just as pervasive and formless form reflects on a mirror, etc., so is the jiva a reflection of Brahman. ” The author replies that this analogy is incongruous because Brahman does not have a substratum as colour does. It cannot be said that the jiva is a reflection of Brahman without any substratum, just as the redness of a China rose flower reflects on a crystal without requiring a substratum. Why ? Because all reflections are seen to have a substance with colour as their cause, and not merely colour. Besides that, the China rose flower itself is reflected on the crystal, and because the redness of the flower is thoroughly diffused on that very transparent object, the round shape of its petals cannot be perceived. Therefore, the conclusion you intend to prove, according to which the jiva is a reflection on the formless Brahman, is not proved at all. Nor is it like a reflection of the sky. The meaning here is clear. Someone may argue, “Then how to justify the perception that the sky is being reflected ?” The perception of the luminous halos of the stars and planets as a reflection of the sky is an illusory impression. “Otherwise, ” i.e., “if it is accepted that something formless and all-pervasive has a reflection». The word ‘tatra ‘means ‘in describing the reflection’.

### Text 12

**Перевод:**

**As for the scriptural statements about Brahman’s reflection, they are consistent in the figurative sense. Vyāsadeva, the revered compiler of the Brahma-sūtra, states the same: ambuvad agrahanat tu na tathatvam, vrddhi-hrasa-bhaktvam antarbhavad ubhaya-samanjasyad evam (3.2.19-20), “However, since the Supreme Lord cannot be perceived, the jiva is not His reflection in the same way as a reflection is seen on water. The reason is that by taking this as an illustration of full expansion and minuteness, the meaning intended by the scriptures is fulfilled, and the explanation being so, there is consistency between the subject and the object of the comparison.”**

**Комментарий:**

Someone may argue, “If the jiva is not a reflection of Brahman, then how would scriptural sentences that describe such a reflection be meaningful? For instance: akasam ekath hiyathaghatadisuprthag bhavet, tathatmaiko hy aneka-stho jaladharesv ivamsuman (Agni Purāṇa, 375.14), ‘Just as the same sky is divided within pots, etc., the same soul is situated within many bodies, like the sun reflected on various reservoirs of water’». The author replies to this in the present paragraph. Due to the implied similarity with a reflection, such sentences should be taken in a figurative sense (gauna-vrtti), like the sentence ‘John is a lion’. This is also the conclusion of Vyāsadeva, the compiler of the Brahma-sūtra. The meaning of the first quoted aphorism is this: Being all-pervasive and formless, Brahman cannot be perceived (agrahanat) on avidya in the same way something limited and with form such as the sun is perceived on water (ambuvat). Therefore, the jiva is not a reflection of Brahman as such (tathatvam). The meaning of the second quoted aphorism is this: Scriptural statements like that of the quoted verse from the Agni Purāṇa should not be taken in a primary sense (mukhya-vrtti), but they should be rather taken in a secondary sense by considering the similarity of the nature of the Supreme Lord as all-expanding (vrddhi) and the jiva as minute (hrasa). The reason is that by taking this analogy partially, the meaning intended by the scriptures is fulfilled (antarbhavat), because the explanation being so (evam), there is consistency (samanjasyat) between both the subject and the object of the comparison (ubhaya), according to which just as the sun is all expanding, independent, and free from connection with the attributes of the limiting adjunct, while its reflection is minute, dependent, and connected with the attributes of the limiting adjunct, so are God and the individual soul.

### Text 13

**Перевод:**

**Therefore, like Brahman, the jiva is an individual conscious soul from beginningless time, as stated in the Kaṭha Upaniṣad (2.2.13): nityo nityanam cetanas cetananam, “There is one eternal being among many eternal beings, one sentient being among many sentient beings.”**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author concludes the present topic. The meaning is clear.

### Text 14

**Перевод:**

**Although the jivas are different from the Supreme Lord, being His energy, they are said to be His parts and parcels. It is proved in the Bhagavad-gita (7.5) that they are actually His energy: apareyam itas tv anyam prakṛtim viddhi me param, jiva-bhutam maha-baho yayedam dharyate jagat, “O mighty-armed Arjuna, you should know that other than these, there is another energy of Mine, the superior energy in the form of the living entities, by which this universe is accepted for enjoyment.” Therefore, it is also expected that there is a relationship between them and the Lord. This is also explained in the Govinda-bhāṣya on the Amsa Adhikaraṇa (2.3-41-51).**

**Комментарий:**

It had been said that the jivas are parts and parcels of the Supreme Lord on account of their subordination to Him. Here, the author demonstrates that this is their real nature, since they are His energy. The quoted verse is from the Bhagavad-gita (7-5). The word ‘prakṛtim ‘means ‘energy, ‘and ‘tasya ‘refers to the individual soul. “Therefore, ” i.e., “since the jivas are His energy. ”

### Text 15

**Перевод:**

**Although the jivas are parts and parcels of the Supreme Lord, on account of their turning away from Him, Who is their source, they become overwhelmed by the illusory energy. When they turn towards Him, the illusory energy vanishes and the realization of their svarūpa becomes eternally manifest. As stated in the śruti: ksaram pradhanam amrtaksaram harah ksaratmanav Isate deva ekah, tasyabhidhyanad yojanat tattva-bhavad bhuyas cante visva-maya-nivrttih (Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad, 1.10), “Pradhana is perishable, and thejiva is immortal and immutable. One God, the Supreme Soul, controls pradhana and the jiva. By meditating on Him, one then attains the state of samādhi, on account of which the svarūpa of both oneself and God becomes manifest, and at the end one becomes free from all illusion.” na sa punar avartate (Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 8.15л), “The liberated soul does not return again to this world.”**

**Комментарий:**

The word ‘tasya ‘refers to the jiva. As stated in the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam: bhayam dvitiyabhinivesatah syad isad apetasya viparyayo’smṛtih, tan-mayayato budha abhajet tarn bhaktyaikayesam guru-devatatma (11.2.37), “By the influence of the illusory energy of the Lord, the living entity becomes absorbed in something different than spirit and becomes deviated from God. The result of this is fear, false identification, and forgetfulness. Therefore, having accepted a guru as one’s worshipable deity, an intelligent person should worship the Supreme Lord with exclusive devotion." janasya kṛṣṇad vimukhasya daivad adharma-silasya suduhkhitasya, anugrahayeha caranti nunath bhūtani bhavyani janardanasya (3-5.3), “The fortunate devotees of Lord Kṛṣṇa actually roam in this world to show their kindness to people who are averse towards Him due to their past activities and are thus suffering and prone to sinful deeds. ” Whenever due to material activities or merely by māyā itself the living entity identifies oneself as connected with a material body, this is considered worldly life. It is to be inferred that in this situation, one is already averse towards God. Such aversion has no beginning, and without it, one would not be covered by the illusory energy. It should be understood that just as darkness envelops a lamp covered by an earthen pot, māyā envelops a living entity covered by aversion to God. When one turns towards Him on account of good association, that aversion is removed, and then māyā vanishes, just like darkness vanishes when the earthen pot is destroyed by the strike of a hammer. Henceforth, as in the case of a lamp, the living entity’s svarūpa becomes manifest, and one always sees the Supreme Soul. The author then gives evidence from the scriptures. The text ‘ksaram pradhanam ‘is from the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad (l.io). On account of its being mutable, pradhana is called ‘ksara’. The word ‘harah ‘refers to the Supreme Soul. One (ekah) God (devah), the Supreme Soul (harah), controls (isate) the pradhana and the jiva (pradhanatmanau). By meditating (abhidhyanat) on God, one then attains the state of asamprajfiata-samādhi (yojanat), on account of which the svarūpa of oneself and the svarūpa of God become manifest (tattva-bhavat), and at the end one becomes free from all illusion, i.e., attains liberation. The text ‘na sa punah ‘is from the Chāndogya Upaniṣad (8.15.1). The liberated soul does not return from the abode of God, i.e., does not fall down from there.

### Text 16

**Перевод:**

**Although the jiva’s aversion towards God exists from beginningless time, by the association of saintly persons it disappears, and one then turns towards Him. As stated in the scriptures: anadi-mayaya supto yada jivah prabudhyate, ajam anidram asvapnam advaitam budhyate tada (MandUkya Upaniṣad, 1.16), “When the jiva, asleep by the beginningless influence of maya, awakes up, then it realises the unborn, sleepless, dreamless, and non-dual Supreme Lord.” mahat-sevam dvaram ahur vimuktes tamo-dvaram yositam sangi-sangam, mahantas te sama-cittah prasanta vimanyavah suhrdah sadhavo ye (Śrimad-Bhāgavatam, 5-5-2), “The great souls of equipoised minds, who are peaceful, free from anger, kind-hearted, and saintly, declared that service to exalted transcendentalists is the way to liberation, and association with those attached to women is the way to hell.”**

**Комментарий:**

It should be understood that aversion towards the Supreme Lord exists since beginningless time due to the prior non-existence (prag-abhava) of mental perception of Him. The jiva is bewildered by Lord Hari’s illusory energy, which results from beginningless aversion towards Him. When the jiva awakens on account of the association of devotees, that aversion disappears and one turns towards the Supreme Lord. Then one realises Lord Hari, i.e., accepts Him as one’s Lord. The verse ‘mahat-sevam ‘is from the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (5.5.2) and was spoken by Rsabha.

### Text 17

**Перевод:**

**When the jiva is purified by saintly association, a relationship with the Supreme Lord is established, which is characterized either by the destruction of the particular ignorance that covered His svarūpa in the form of bliss and knowledge, or by the destruction of the prior nonexistence (prag-abhava) of mental perception of the Supreme Lord by hearing about Him from the Upaniṣads. This relationship is due to one’s turning towards Him, which is caused by good association and is said to be the Lord‘s kindness. After turning towards Him, by the constant practice of loving devotional service, the particular ignorance that covers the qualities of the Lord disappears for the jiva. One then realises Him. Thus, the destruction of both kinds of ignorance is liberation.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author explains the meaning of what was said above. When one is purified by saintly association, i.e., when the jiva’s aversion towards God has been destroyed by good association, then on account of turning towards Him, which is said to be the Lord‘s kindness and is caused by that good association, one has a relationship with Him, characterized as accepting Him as one’s Lord. This is the connection of the words here. Expecting that someone may ask what that relationship is like, the author states two attributes: a relationship is characterized either by realizing the Lord‘s form as bliss and knowledge, or by hearing about Him from the Upaniṣads. It has been said that as a result of good association, the aversion towards God is destroyed, and then one turns towards Him, which makes His svarūpa become revealed to oneself. Then, the author states that by loving devotional service, the Supreme Lord‘s qualities become revealed.

After that, one realises Lord Hari, Who displays unlimited qualities, pastimes, and powers.

### Text 18

**Перевод:**

**The śruti states the same thing: vimuktas ca vimucyate (Kaṭha Upaniṣad, 2.5.1), “The liberated soul becomes free.” bhūyas cante visva-maya-nivrttih (Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad, 1.10), “At the end, all māyā disappears.” As for those who propound the falsity of bondage by these śruti statements, they are actually non-believers (nastika), for they render the Vedas unauthoritative, wherein the material manifestation is described as real. This has already been previously mentioned.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author gives evidence of two kinds of ignorance. The text ‘vimuktas ca ‘is from the Kaṭha Upaniṣad (2.5.1). The meaning is that the soul liberated (vimuktah) from the ignorance that covers the Lord‘s svarūpa then becomes free (vimucyate) from the ignorance that covers His qualities. The meaning of the text ‘bhūyas cante ‘is that when one is liberated (vimuktah) from the māyā that covers the Lord‘s svarūpa, at the end (ante), i.e., at the subsequent time when one attains ultimate devotion to the Lord, the māyā that covers His qualities also vanishes (nivrttih). “Those who,” i.e., the monists, who think that since the material body, etc., are illusory creations like silver in a shell, the jiva is actually liberated even during such perception, and by constant meditation on the meaning of the sentences of the Upaniṣads, such as ‘ekam evadvitiyam ‘and ‘neha nānāsti kittcana, ‘one is freed from that illusion. They explain the text ‘bhuyas ca ‘in the same way. The words ‘tad-visayaka ‘mean that the Vedas actually describe the material manifestation in sentences like ‘yato va imani bhutani jayante (Taittiriya Upaniṣad, 3.1), “You should know Him from Whom all living beings are born. ” “Previously, ” i.e., in the sixth chapter.

### Text 19

**Перевод:**

**The destruction of both kinds of ignorance is an eternal effect, because its form is non-existence (abhava), and because of the scriptural statements that one never returns.**

**Комментарий:**

Someone may argue, “As the destruction of both kinds of ignorance is an effect, and hence non-eternal, when both are destroyed there is a chance that they might appear again». The author responds to this here. Such effect is of the nature of non-existence that follows destruction (dhvamsabhava) and is eternal, for a destroyed earthen pot never becomes the same pot once again.

### Text 20

**Перевод:**

**“Lord Hari’s nature is invisible, so how can you say that He can become visible?” One should not speak so, for although the Lord is indeed invisible, His dariana is possible by constant practice, just as with demigods and other beings, and as with the musical notes as well. Vyāsadeva, the revered compiler of the Brahmaputra, states the same thing: prakāśas ca karmany abhyasat (3.2.26), “The Supreme Lord becomes revealed by the practice of meditation.” Thus, liberation is the disappearance of suffering and the attainment of happiness.**

**Комментарий:**

The Supreme Lord‘s nature is invisible, i.e., His svarūpa is manifest inward. The word ‘aparoksyam ‘means ‘to directly see’. In the Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad (1.14) it is said: dhyana-nirmathanabhyasad devam pasyen nigudhavat, “By constant meditation, one sees God hidden in the core of the heart, just as by constantly rubbing wood, one sees the fire that was hidden within it. “By the Lord‘s mercy potency (krpa-śakti) that is awakened by the constant practice of devotional service, it is possible to actually see the Lord, although He is invisible by nature. As stated in the Padma Purāṇa: sac-cid-ananda-rūpatvat syat krsno ‘dhoksajo ‘py asau, nija-sakteh prabhavena svam bhaktan darsayet prabhuh, “Although Lord Kṛṣṇais Adhoksaja, beyond perception, since His form is eternity, knowledge, and bliss, nevertheless, by the power of His energy, He reveals Himself to His devotees. ”By the constant practice (abhyasat) of meditation (karmani), the Supreme becomes revealed (prakāśah). “Thus,” i.e., “in the way as it has been described. ”

### Text 21

**Перевод:**

**There are some who speak as follows: “In the stage of liberation, the ‘I ‘disappears, and only something in the form of consciousness and happiness remains. That is the real soul, the real nature of liberation.” This idea is very foolish, for it suffices to explain that in this case, consciousness and bliss would be something that is not experienced by anyone, and the soul would not be the experiencer of consciousness and bliss. Such definition of liberation is contradictory, as stated by the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (4-25.4)" duhkha-hanih sukha-praptih sreyas tan neha cesyate, “Liberation is the disappearance of suffering and the attainment of happiness. This is not expected from Vedic rituals.” The very desire that ‘let me be happy and free from distress ‘is evidence of this truth, in the absence of which that imagined concept is baseless. It is actually seen that the goal of life, characterized as the disappearance of suffering and the attainment of happiness, has the soul for its basis, and this goal would not be possible if the soul, the ‘I, ‘is destroyed.**

**Комментарий:**

It had been said that the liberated soul is happy and free from distress. The monists hold a view contrary to this, and although that has been previously refuted, here the author refutes yet other of their conclusions. It is accepted by the monists that at the time of liberation, there is no one who experiences or has attributes. Yet it is accepted by the Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad (4330) that the soul is an experiences na hi vijnatur vijnater viparilopo vidyate, “The consciousness of the conscious being is never destroyed». “Such definition of liberation is contradictory, 33 i.e., different than the disappearance of suffering and the attainment of happiness. The verse ‘duhkha-hanih 3 is from the Śrimad-Bhāgavatam (4.25.4) and was spoken by Nārada. The word ‘iha3 refers to the performance of Vedic rituals. “This truth,” i.e., the fact that liberation consists of the disappearance of suffering and the attainment of happiness. “In the absence of which,” i.e. without such a desire. The words 1 atmavadhikah 3 mean ‘having the soul for its basis.3 The word ‘asau3 refers to the goal of life, which has the soul for its basis.

### Text 22

**Перевод:**

**Some speak the following: “The soul is actually the cause of suffering due to its acquiring sins. Therefore, by annihilating the soul, suffering disappears.” This idea is in fact a display of folly. Suffering arises only in the waking state, and not during deep sleep, and thus either directly or indirectly, the soul is not the cause of suffering. Moreover, the cause of suffering is the perceptions from the material body, the senses, and the sense objects. When the results of one’s activities fade away by knowledge and enjoyment, there being no cause anymore, another material body and senses are not produced again, on account of which suffering disappears. Therefore, it is not proper to accept that the soul is annihilated.**

**Комментарий:**

“Some,” i.e., the Buddhists who propound the theory of momentariness (ksanikavada). The compound ‘paparjanat ‘means ‘on account of generating sins’. The waking state, in which there is awareness of the body, the senses, and the sense objects, causes suffering. However, during deep sleep, when that awareness disappears, there is no suffering. Thus, either directly or indirectly, the waking state itself is the cause of suffering, and not the soul. The word ‘karmanah ‘means ‘of material activities, ‘the cause of sin. There being no influence of sinful reactions, their effects in the form of a material body and senses are not produced again.

### Text 23

**Перевод:**

**Furthermore, the absence of suffering can be liberation when it has the soul for its basis. Otherwise, absence of suffering is similarly present in objects like pillars. Therefore, during liberation there is the end of the causes of suffering, which are different from the soul, but the soul does not end. Otherwise, even the non-existence of objects like pillars would also be liberation. If it is accepted that there is difference between the non-existence of the individual soul and the non-existence of a pillar, then the theory of non-difference would be undermined. Moreover, if liberation is the annihilation of the soul, then who attains this result? The soul or something other than the soul? The first case is not possible, for the soul is no more existent. A result is certainly not produced in the absence of what yields it. The second case is also not possible, for the body and senses would derive no benefit with the annihilation of the soul. Therefore, such fools are to be ignored.**

**Комментарий:**

Otherwise, ” i.e., if it is not accepted that during liberation the absence of suffering has the soul for its basis, then even the absence of suffering found in objects like pillars would be liberation. The cause of suffering is the perceptions of the body, the senses, and the senses objects, whose connection is acquired during the waking state, and the cause of this is the material activities. “Otherwise, ” i.e., if it is accepted that the soul also vanishes during liberation. Just as no one has ever seen a non-existent soul, no one has ever seen a non-existent pillar. If it is accepted that there is any difference between both non-existences, the equivalence will be lost. Thus, the existence of the individual soul is proved. This is the idea here. According to the mentioned theory, who would attain the result in the form of liberation ? The first proposition is that the result, the annihilation of the soul, is attained by the soul. This is not possible, for the soul who desires to attain this result no longer exists. The second proposition is that the result, the annihilation of the soul, is attained by something other than the soul, such as the material body and senses. This is also not possible. Why? Because the body and senses can derive no benefit from the annihilation of the soul. It is only in connection with the soul that they derive benefit.

### Text 24

**Перевод:**

**In this way, there is difference between Ṡyāmasundara, the Supreme Lord of all, and the material manifestation composed of the living entities and matter. The living entities can attain ultimate happiness and freedom from suffering by knowledge about Him and devotion to Him. Thus, it is concluded that all scriptures are about the difference between God and the individual soul.**

### Text 25

**Перевод:**

**There are certain sentences found in some places in the scriptures that seem to be about non-difference. In some places, they teach that the universe is a form of the Supreme Lord on account of its being dependent on Him, being maintained exclusively by Him, having Him for its basis, and being pervaded by Him. In some places, they teach the non-difference between God and the living entity due to their dwelling in the same place and sharing the same views. In some places, they speak of non-difference because the living entities and material nature are energies of the Supreme Lord, the possessor of all energies, and are hence non-different from Him. In yet other places, they oppose the apparent internal difference among the Lord‘s many avataras.**

**Комментарий:**

Someone may argue, “If all the scriptures are about difference, then how to reconcile the sentences about non-difference?” Expecting this question, the author clarifies the matter again at the end of the book, although this had already been previously explained. The words ‘tan-matrayatta-vrttikataya ‘mean that the stability and maintenance of the universe are exclusively dependent upon the Lord, and ‘tan-nisthataya ‘means that He is the basis of it. Both the Lord and the living entities dwell in the same place, just as cattle graze separated in the morning and then gather in the evening. They also share the same views, just as kings who argue today become unanimous tomorrow. Thus, although there is difference between the svarūpa of God and the svarūpa of the individual souls, non-difference is mentioned due to their sharing the same place and views. The Lord‘s energies are non-different from Him because the qualities are non-different from the qualified. There is no internal difference among the Lord‘s avataras because all of them have the same svarūpa as the avatāri, the source of all avataras. On the other hand, in the theory that there is only one consciousness, neither creation nor any instruction could be possible from such single consciousness. Therefore, those who are intelligent should not believe in that doctrine.

### Text 26

**Перевод:**

**There are others who explain the meaning of the scriptural sentences in the following way: “There are śruti sentences that describe the Supreme Lord, Brahman, as non-different from everything. For instance, atmaivedam sarvam (Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 7.25.2), ‘The Supreme Soul is all this’. sarvam khalv idam brahma (Chāndogya Upaniṣad, 3.14.1), ‘All this is indeed Brahman’. neha nānāsti kincana (Bṛhad-āraṇyaka Upaniṣad, 4.4.19), “Within the Supreme Lord there is no difference whatsoever.” There are other śruti sentences that describe the Lord as different from everything. For instance: prthag atmanam preritaram ca matva (Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad, 1.6), ‘Considering oneself and the Supreme Ruler as different, etc’. justam yada pasyaty anyam isam (ib., 4.7), ‘When one sees that the blissful Supreme Lord is different than oneself, etc’. dva suparna sayuja sakhāyā (Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad, 3.1. l), ‘1Two birds are sitting together on the same tree as friends’. karma-ksaye yati sa tattvato ‘nyah (Śvetāśvatara Upaniṣad, 6.4), ‘When the results of all material activities are destroyed, the liberated jiva attains the Supreme but remains different from Him in svarūpa’. niranjanah parāmaṁ samyam upaiti (Muṇḍaka Upaniṣad, 3.1.3), ‘The wise seer whose heart is pure attains transcendental similarity with the Supreme Lord’. Although both kinds of śruti sentences appear to have mutually contradictory meanings, both should be explained as having different topics, because it is unbecoming that both would be unauthoritative, and because it is also unbecoming that only one of them is authoritative if both can be so. In the Subala Upaniṣad (7), it is similarly stated: antah-śarire nihito guhayam aja eko nityah, yasya prthivi sariram yasyapah sariram yasya tejah sariram yasya vayuh sariram yasyakasah sariram yasya manah sariram yasya buddhih sariram yasyahaūkarah sariram yasya cittam sariram yasyavyaktam sariram yasyaksaram sariram yasya mrtyuh sariram esa sarva-bhutantar-atmapahata-papma divyo deva eko narayanah, ‘The eternal unborn and unique Lord is situated within the core of the heart. He Whose body is the earth, water, fire, air, ether, mind, intelligence, false ego, consciousness, pradhana, the jiva, and time, He is the unique Divine God, Lord Nārāyaṇa, the Supreme Soul, free from all evils, and situated within all beings’. According to these statements, the topic of the śruti about oneness is the non-difference as sarira-sariri-bhava (body-soul relation), while the topic of the śruti about duality is the difference between the svarūpa of God and the svarūpa of the jiva. Otherwise, the defects that exist in the jivas would also affect the Supreme. Thus, by explaining the difference between the topics of the śruti sentences that describe either difference or non-difference, the idea that their meaning is mutually contradictory has been removed.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author corroborates the Visistadvaita philosophy. “Others,” i.e., the Sri-vaiṣṇavas. In the text ‘karma-ksaye yati sa tattvato ‘nyah, ‘the word ’sah ‘refers to the liberated soul, which attains (yati) the Supreme but even then remains different from Him in svarūpa (tattvatah). It is then stated that if the entire śruti were either only about non-difference or only about difference, there would not be a direct connection between both statements, and thus the śruti must have an eternal division as twofold sentences. Although both kinds of śruti sentences that teach either difference or non-difference appear to have meanings that nullify each other, the division of both should be explained as having different topics. This is the connection between the words here. Why should such a division of both kinds of sentences be made? The reason is mentioned, “Because it is unbecoming, etc. ” The idea is that it is so because both kinds of sentences are equally apauruseya (supramundane). Norafter demonstrating either duality or non-duality as false should one say that only one out of the two kinds of śruti sentences is unauthoritative. It should be understood that the principle that both kinds of sentences equally speak the same truth is also applied here. The word ‘tathatvat ‘means ‘because it is similarly unbecoming’. The idea is that if the authority of the śruti is given up, that is the view of the non-believers (nastika). In the text from the Subala Upaniṣad, the word ‘avyaktam ‘means ‘pradhana, ‘the word ‘aksaram ‘means ‘the jiva, ‘and ‘mrtyuh ‘means ‘time’. The concept of sarira-sariri-bhava (body-soul relation) is understood as follows: just as it is seen that words such as ‘go ‘(cow), which express attributes like ‘cowness, ‘determine cows, which are qualified as having ‘cowness,’similarly words such as ‘prakṛti, ‘‘jiva, ‘and ‘kala, ‘which express attributes, living beings, and bodies, determine the Supreme Brahman, Lord Nārāyaṇa, Who is qualified by those respective bodies. Therefore, there is оnlу оnе qualified (visista) Brahman. This is the conclusion of the Sri-vaiṣṇavas. Since Brahman has eternal attributes like all-pervasiveness, and the jiva has eternal attributes like minuteness, the svarūpa of both is eternally different. This difference is the topic of the śruti sentences about duality. “Otherwise” i.e., if it is accepted that even the svarūpa of Brahman and the svarūpa of the jiva are non-different.

### Text 27

**Перевод:**

**“Since there is contradiction if consciousness and matter are said to be both different and non-different in nature, and there is self-contradiction if people who substantiate non-difference from matter are themselves insentient matter, there is no possibility that the living entities, material nature, and Brahman are non-different. Hence, it is demonstrated that their svarūpas are different.” In this proposition also, it is very clear that the scriptures teach about the difference between God and the living entities.**

**Комментарий:**

Someone may argue, “Having corroborated that there is difference and non-difference between God, the living entity, and material nature, why is the congruity of those two kinds of śruti sentences not supported?”

The present paragraph gives the answer. Due to the impossibility that the living entities, material nature, and Brahman are non-different, it is established that their svarūpas are different. This is the connection between the words here. Why cannot all three of them be non-different ? The Sri-vaiṣṇavas say, “As there is contradiction, etc.” Non-difference is contradicted by difference, and difference is contradicted by non-difference, for one nullifies the other. If people substantiate that the soul is non-different from material nature, their being conscious itself is contradicted, and therefore they cannot substantiate anything. In this way, since the nondifference between the living entities, material nature, and God is unproved, it is proved that their svarūpas are different. This is the meaning.

### Text 28

**Перевод:**

**Some recent philosophers think as follows: “The effect and its cause must be different and non-different, and so are the body and the embodied, the quality and its possessor, the jiva and God, just like the svarita sound. As udatta and anudatta, svarita is twofold, but as svarita itself, it is only one. Similarly, if cause and effect were different, then a pot would have its own weight, and each of its constituting parts would also have their own weight, and thus when put on a scale they would have double weight. If they were non-different, then results like fetching water would be baffled, and the potter’s effort would be useless. Therefore, cause and effect must be different and non-different. Nor is there contradiction here, for although there is contradiction by a one-sided view, by this double-sided view, there is none. As clay, there is no difference; while as pot, potsherd, and so on, there is difference. The body and the embodied, as well as the quality and its possessor, are non-different as Brahman, but they are different as primary and secondary. So is the case with God and the jiva. The sentences about difference and non-difference would have a primary meaning in this way.” This argument is not reasonable. If there were no difference between the svarūpa of God and the svarūpa of the jivas, then God would also experience the suffering and happiness experienced by its parts and parcels, and the jivas would be able to create, maintain, and destroy the universe and so on. If non-difference is merely attributed, as in the case of the sentence ‘vacam dhenum upasita, ‘ then this establishes another doctrine. Since svarita has actually two forms, like Ardhanari£vara, it is no illustration to such theory. If results like fetching water are baffled, this does not hinder non-difference between cause and effect. The non-difference of both is corroborated in the Tad-ananyatva Adhikaraṇa and its commentary. Thus, this theory is mere imagination.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author points out flaws in a modern concoction. Desiring to remove the baselesness of their own concocted ideas, some recent philosophers like Bhatta Bhaskara reconcile sentences about difference and sentences about non-difference in the way described here. According to them, God and God’s body must be different and non-different, and so it must be the case between God and His qualities, and God and the jivas. If an earthen pot is non-different from its cause, i.e., if it is the same as a lump of clay, then its capacity to fetch water and so on would be baffled. If the cause, a lump of clay, is non-different from its effect, i.e., if it is the same as an earthen pot, then the potter’s effort would be useless. Therefore, there is no non-difference. Next, they say that it is also so in other instances, such as the body and the embodied, qualities like omniscience and the possessor of those qualities. The word ‘brahmatvena ‘means ‘as Brahman, which is consciousness and bliss’. The body and the qualities are secondary, while the embodied and the possessor of qualities are primary. The words ‘tat-tat-visayakani ‘refer to scriptural sentences that describe either difference or non-difference. The word ‘anjasyam ‘means ‘primary meaning, ‘and ‘bhunjiran ‘means ‘would have’. The author then refutes this view. If there were no difference between the svarūpa of God and the svarūpa of the jivas, then the creation, maintenance, and destruction of the universe would also be done by His parts and parcels, the jivas. Moreover, this contradicts Badarayana’s conclusion. His conclusion is that God never experiences the suffering and happiness of the conditioned souls, and that not even the liberated souls are able to create the universe and so on. This is to be seen in the Govinda-bhāṣya and the Suksma-tlka. The word ‘asau ‘refers to non-difference. If non-difference is merely attributed, then this theory of difference and non-difference would be unproved, and the theory of pure difference would have its inception. The author then points out the flaw in the illustration about svarita. Since a short svarita has only one measure, it is single, and since a long svarita has two measures, it is dual, and therefore that is no illustration. Both short and long svarita are nevertheless twofold. The word ‘tatra ‘refers to the theory of difference and non-difference. If cause and effect were different, cause and effect would appear separated, and when combined their weight would increase. Therefore, cause and effect are actually non-different. The effort of the potter is meant to bring the pot into a manifest state, and once the pot is manifest, results like fetching water are accomplished with it, and not baffled. This is described in the Govinda-bhāṣya, which should be consulted.

### Text 29

**Перевод:**

**There are some who speak as follows: “According to some, Brahman is only one and has no attributes, while others say that it is different from everything else and has attributes. However, we do not see accuracy in either of these views, for by dividing the Vedic sentences in this way, they undermine their meaning. On the other hand, Brahman is actually only one and exists in the form of everything. Only this conclusion is correct. Although in this view the svarūpa of God and the svarūpa of the jiva are one, they are eternally treated as different, like Lord Nārāyaṇa and His quadruple expansions, and therefore there is no contradiction anywhere among the scriptural sentences. Nor is there a thorough exchange of attributes between God and the jivas, for even though there is oneness, such exchange is not seen. Although there is oneness between an earthen pot and its constituting parts, and between the qualities and their possessor, we certainly do not see such absolute exchange in either. The principle is that sentences about saguṇa and sentences about nirguṇa should be understood as having different topics, just like sentences about violence and sentences about non-violence. Thus, everything is flawless.”**

**Комментарий:**

Desiring to remove the baselesness of their own concocted ideas, there are some recent philosophers who judge themselves followers of Viṣṇu Svami and speak in the way described here. According to them, there is no accuracy in either the views of some philosophers like Śaṇkaracarya or of others like Madhvācārya; the former because they take the meaning of sentences about difference to be otherwise, and the latter because they take the meaning of sentences about non-difference to be otherwise. If someone asks, “How do you explain the meaning of those sentences ?” they say, “On the other hand, Brahman actually exists in the form of everything, all the fourteen planetary systems consisting of God, the jivas, material nature, and time. Thus, the whole material manifestation is consciousness. It appears to be inert matter only due to ignorance of its conscious nature, just as water appears to have a beautiful colour externally. Although there is no difference between Lord Nārāyaṇa and His quadruple expansions starting with Vāsudeva, there is a distinction as the original Supreme Lord and as His quadruple expansions. Such distinction between God and the jivas should be similarly accepted, and not difference. By accepting such a distinction in sentences like ‘dva suparna, ‘there is no contradiction among the employed words such as ‘dva ‘(two) and ‘anyah ‘(other). There is no exchange or mixture of attributes between God and the jivas. Although they are non-different, we do not see any such exchange of attributes. Just as the quality of being a pot does not exist in a potsherd, nor the quality of being a potsherd exists in a pot, the quality of being a jiva does not exist in God, and the quality of being God does not exist in the jiva. This view does not undermine the doctrine of bhakti». This is the idea here. The scriptural sentences about saguṇa speak about the intrinsic qualities of the Supreme, and those about nirguṇa deny any apparent, illusory quality. Sentences about violence describe the slaying of animals offered in sacrifice, and sentences about non-violence forbid slaying animals in situations other than sacrifices.

### Text 30

**Перевод:**

**This statement is ill thought out, because since the jiva’s suffering would not be possible if the jiva were non-different from God, the idea that both are non-different is removed. If the jiva were indeed Brahman itself, then it would not experience suffering. Someone may argue, “If the material manifestation were real, this would be a defect. Yet, just as in the case of another doctrine, in our view too the universe is actually false.” It is not so, for such view incurs faults like decrying the authority of the Vedas. Moreover, the Lord‘s qualities like mercy would require explanation, since they would serve no purpose. Thus, these two theories are imagined by dull-witted people who are unable to penetrate into the meaning of the Vedic sentences. Therefore, such views are insignificant.**

**Комментарий:**

Here the author refutes the above mentioned view. It is heard from the scriptures that the jivas experience birth, old age, death, and hell. If they were non-different from God, such experience would not be possible. Since this is not the case, the concept of the non-difference between God and the jivas is removed. Someone may argue, “Just as the monists consider this material world false like the chariots and horses one sees in a dream, so do we. Thus, since the experience of birth, old age, and so forth, is not real at all, the concept that God and the jiva are non-different cannot be removed because such experience would not be possible otherwise». The author then refutes this idea.
